CHAPTER I 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


“Johnny Bowlegs, pack your kit and trek!” 
—Kipling. 

For some time after we got back from our last ex- 
pedition to the Pamirs, everything was as peaceful 
as a May morning. It did not seem as if there could 
be anything more delightful in life than Oyster Bay 
and our families. There was much to do for both of 
us, and days passed like scenes in a cinematograph. 

More than a year drifted by without our noticing 
it. Then strange but familiar voices began whisper- 
ing to me. Spirits of the high places of earth, from 
the barren boulders and snows, hinted of days when 
the driving storm caked the ice on beard and face; 
spirits from the desert sang of blowing sand and 
blinding sun. For a time I put them resolutely aside, 
—refused to listen to them. 

Then one evening Kermit and I were alone to- 
gether. I found he too had been hearing these voices. 
That was the beginning of our downfall. Soon we 
began to lunch more frequently with the brown lean 
men who drift quietly into New York, not those who 
go tourist-like across Africa in automobiles, camping 
each night in richly upholstered luxury, but fore- 

1 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


loopers, who trek to lonely places where food is scant, 
travel by foot, and danger a constant bedfellow. 

Almost without realizing it we started to discuss 
where we should go next. Naturally we turned to 
the blank spaces on the map, those fascinating white 
blotches with perhaps the dotted course of a sur- 
mised river marked and “unknown” printed across 
them. These are much fewer now than they were 
twenty years ago, but such as they are they beckon 
just as irresistibly. We thought of crossing Borneo, 
but just as we were setting on foot inquiries con- 
cerning it we heard that some one had been before us. 
We discussed collecting in Angola. 

Finally our eyes turned to that part of Asia north- 
west of Indo-China, where the rugged ramparts of 
the Himalayas gradually descend to the tropical 
coastal plains. Through this mountain wall five great 
rivers have forced their paths, the Brahmaputra, Ir- 
rawaddy, Salween, Mekong and Yangtze. 'Thou- 
sands of miles separate them at their mouths. The 
Yangtze runs into the Pacific by Shanghai, while the 
Brahmaputra rolls its placid muddy waters through 
India to the Bay of Bengal. Where they hurl them- 
selves tumultuously down their mountain passes, the 
deep mystery gorges that are their beds are some- 
times separated by but forty miles. 

Gradually our plans took shape. Kermit and I 
would strike north into this country either through 


Q 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


Tibet or by the old Bhamo-Talifu trail, which runs 
from Burma to Yunnan. Where we were travelling 
through uncharted country we would make a rough 
attempt to map it. After pressing in as far as possi- 
ble we would collect such of the animals as we could. 

The Golden Fleece of our trip was the giant 
panda. Atluropus melanoleucus is its scientific name. 
It lives in the dense bamboo jungles in Szechuan, 
and authorities seemed to agree that so far it had 
never been killed by a white man. Pére David, the 
French missionary scientist, discovered it sixty years 
ago when he got skins from the natives of Muping. 
The animal is the size of a bear and strikingly col- 
ored. Around its eyes are black spectacles. Its ears 
are black and there is a heavy band of black over its 
shoulders and front legs. The rest of its body is white. 
No one knew exactly how it should be classified, 
whether it was a bear, a panda or an entirely new 
species. From time to time such men as General 
Pereira, Ernest Wilson, Zappey, and McNeill had 
hunted it, but without success. We had slight hope of 
getting it. So slight in fact that we did not let even 
our close friends know what our real objective was. 
Our information was so scant as to its range and hab- 
its that our quest seemed even more doubtful than 
Jason’s. 

Besides the panda there are numerous other ani- 
mals of great interest, among them that strange crea- 


3 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


ture the takin, half goat, half antelope. It lives in 
the high mountains and is surprisingly active in spite 
of its clumsy build. Its hair is long and in certain 
species yellow. The Chinese call it Yea gnu, or wild 
cow. The scientific name of the species we hoped to 
find is Budocras taxicolor, as odd as the animal itself. 
Mason Mitchell, now living at Hollywood, was the 
first white man to shoot a specimen of the western 
race. 

Equal in interest with the takin is the golden mon- 
key. It lives in the forest in bitterly cold country 
where the snow lies deep for five months each year. 
The color, especially of the old males, is brilliant. 
The hair shades from yellow to rich russet and snow- 
white. As far as I know there is no group in any 
museum. 

Finally there are little-known deer, McNeill’s stag, 
and the most northerly representative of the sam- 
bhur. There are also burrhel, serow and ghoral, of 
which not a dozen specimens have ever been collected. 

Whatever we could get in the way of small mam- 
mals or shoot in the way of birds would also be of 
unquestioned scientific interest. In Szechuan there 
are numbers of brilliantly colored pheasant. One in 
particular, the Lady Amherst, looks as if modelled in 
silver. 

When we had finished we would swing around and 
strike south to the hills of Indo-China, where the first 


4 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


part of our expedition would end. There we would 
be in one of the finest game countries in the world. 
In the jungles are elephant, water-buffalo, sledang, 
banting, tiger, leopard and a variety of deer. It is 
rumored that somewhere in the back country may be 
found the Shomburgk deer that has never even been 
seen alive or dead by a white man. 

We decided also that it would be well to have in 
Indo-China a group of trained naturalists to make a 
thorough collection of the birds and small mammals 
of that region. 

Once the plans had taken shape the question arose 
of financing the expedition. Neither Kermit nor I 
have sufficient money to carry the expense necessary 
for such a trip. We decided to go to Chicago and 
tell our old friend Stanley Field, President of the 
Field Museum of Natural History, for whom we 
went in search of Ovis poli. 

The moment we mentioned it to Mr. Field he was 
enthusiastic. He gave us a dinner that night at his 
house. There we met William V. Kelly, a generous 
patron of the Museum. We broached the subject to 
kim and without a moment’s hesitation he told us he 
would stand behind us and finance the venture. 

Next morning the Kelly-Roosevelt-Field Museum 
Expedition was fact. There were many hurdles, how- 
ever, still to be taken. To begin with, there were our 
families to consult. It would be a gross overstate- 


5 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


ment to say that our wives were enthusiastic about the | 
idea. They were not, but both were game. Remem- 
bering the words in the marriage service “For better 
and for worse,” they eventually gave us their per- 
mission. 

As soon as our financing was arranged we asked 
Suydam Cutting to come with us. He had been on 
our last trip to Central Asia. Since then he had been 
on two expeditions of his own, one to Abyssinia, one 
to the Naga Hills in Assam. No one could wish for 
a stouter companion in hardship or danger. He is a 
man of undaunted courage. No matter how forlorn 
the hope he would always stand firm. 

Neither of us could get away before the middle of 
November,—Kermit because of his shipping business, 
I on account of the presidential campaign, during 
which I wished to do all in my power for Mr. Hoover. 
Nevertheless, though it was still six months away 
there was much we had to do. The first thing, of 
course, was to get permits to visit the various coun- 
tries. This was not easy, for the lands we wished to 
travel lay so far away that “the King’s justice” 
hardly ran that far. Now if there is one thing that 
embarrasses governments, it is to have an atrocity 
committed on a foreign national in some back-prov- 
ince where their control is shadowy. If it is a citizen 
of their own country that is scuppered, everything is 
easy of adjustment. It can be maintained, with a fair 

6 


T. R., C. 8S. C., AND K. R. UPON THEIR ARRIVAL AT YUNNAN 


STHSVN GHOOVa UVONdd ASANIHO 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


show of truth, that the victim was only enjoying the 
full privileges of citizenship. With a foreigner all is 
very different. His government asks questions. The 
press plays up the incident in flaring headlines, un- 
less some home murder or divorce of exceptional 
prurience has taken place. The end may have to be 
an expensive punitive expedition. 

At first, therefore, the governments with whom we 
dealt naturally refused our requests. In time, mainly 
through the kind offices of our friend Doctor Chao 
Chu Wu, the Chinese Government consented. The 
British Foreign Office then courteously agreed to 
forward our request to the Dalai Lama for permis- 
sion to cross Tibet. As everything in the East moves 
very slowly, we heard nothing of this for a long time. 

Meanwhile we had been actively organizing. We 
asked Herbert Stevens, a British scientist who had 
spent much time collecting in Assam and Indo-China, 
to be one of our party in China. We asked Harold 
Coolidge of Boston, a trained naturalist, to lead the 
group of scientists in Indo-China. To complete his 
party we got Russell Hendee from Brooklyn, Josslyn 
Van Tyne of the University of Michigan and Doctor 
Ralph Wheeler of Boston. 

All of these men had adventured in far lands. Cool- 
idge though only twenty-six had collected in the 
north and in Africa also. In the latter place he had 
been charged by a band of gorillas who came cough- 

7 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


ing and crashing through the undergrowth to his very 
feet. In the country where he was these animals had 
never been disturbed by white men, and had no fear 
of the naked, ill-defended natives. They would come 
down to the cornfields and if the farmer attempted to 
drive them away, would turn on him and tear him in 
pieces with their powerful hands. 

At the same time we had been purchasing supplies. 
There is little that is more delightful than purchasing 
equipment for an expedition. Every article suggests 
possible and delightful contingencies. We visited 
stores where the air was redolent with the smell of 
gun oil, and the blue barrels of rifles showed behind 
the glass of the cases like the spotless driving-shafts 
in the engine of a warship. We talked with countless 
men on the details of our supplies. We went to the 
American Geographical Society, and under the tute- 
lage of Doctor Isaiah Bowman studied means and 
methods of mapping unknown country. 

We finally decided on a battery of Springfield 
rifles, sporting model. They are sufficiently powerful 
for almost all game. Kermit had killed an African 
elephant with one. They are nearly fool-proof, even 
with natives. Their bolt action stands equally well 
the dust of the desert and the soaking rains of the 
tropics. Above all, they weigh only a little over 
seven pounds, which is a real consideration when it is 
necessary to carry your rifle yourself. It is surpris- 

8 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


ing how even an extra pound counts when you are 
hunting hard country. I never believed the proverb 
of the straw and the camel’s back until I found how 
much difference a little weight in a rifle makes. 

We also bought two 410-gauge shot-guns, as their 
ammunition is very light and it is possible to carry 
far more than for a larger gun. The small size of the 
bore makes no difference, for on an expedition we 
never take a wing shot if we can avoid it. Where 
every ounce of equipment counts there is no chance 
for sportsmanship in bird shooting. On trek we shoot 
our birds on the ground, preferably when they are 
standing still and huddled together. An old western 
friend of mine once shouted to me when I was ad- 
vancing on half a dozen partridges: “None of yer 
dam’ fancy shots! Shoot for the pot, man!” 

For emergency use we each bought a .38 Colt au- 
tomatic and an armpit holster. This way of carrying 
a pistol is particularly good. The weapon is concealed 
so that it does not offend a sensitive host. More im- 
portant still, it can be drawn with lightning rapidity 
should necessity arise. Many a man lies buried now 
because he could not draw his pistol in the split second 
of time that stood between him and death. 

We selected three lightweight tents, 6 feet by 9 
feet in size, with the floor cloth sewed in to keep out 
the bitter wind of winter. For bedding we took our 
veteran rolls of the last trip. 

9 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Anthony Fiala, an Arctic explorer of note who was 
with my father in Brazil, packed our food supplies in 
light but tough fibre cases. Each case when packed 
weighed fifty pounds. 

As clothes we took with us shorts, khaki shirts, 
heavy trousers, leather coats, and sweaters as we ex- 
pected to meet every kind of weather from the steam- 
ing heat of the tropics to the dry biting cold of the 
high mountains. For formal occasions, such as dining 
with or calling on native officials, we packed jodh- 
purs, the long tight-fitting riding trousers that origi- 
nated in India. 

One branch of our equipment to which we gave 
particular attention was an array of presents for the 
natives. Among our purchases were a dozen Hom- 
burg soft hats of a bilious green color, which are 
greatly prized in Tibet. We got mechanical electric 
torches that generate their own power, wrist-watches, 
trick steel measuring-tapes, knives, folding chairs and 
a dozen other oddments. 

As gifts for the most exalted, Suydam bought half 
a dozen turquoise rings. Among the mountain fast- 
nesses of the Himalayas the turquoise is the most 
treasured jewel. In some places it is customary for 
the women to wear a headdress with a long queue-like 
strip of cloth hanging down behind, on which they 
sew the turquoises that represent their dowry. 

Ceremony and swank count greatly in the hinter- 


10 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


land. If we had been able to stage the initiation cere- 
monies of certain American secret societies for some 
of the native chieftains we have met, I am sure they 
would have given us their daughters and half of their 
kingdoms. Knowing this Kermit and I cast about 
for some means of turning it to account. At last we 
hit on a plan. We recalled war days when the troops 
were formed, the band struck up and some officer or 
man marched forward. We remembered the stiffly 
approaching French general, resplendent in robin’s- 
egg blue and whiskers. We remembered the crimson 
ribbon that was thrown around the neck of the hero, 
and the embarrassing kiss on each cheek. Accord- 
ingly, I went to a New York theatrical costumer’s, 
and bought a dozen medals hung on ribbons as bril- 
liant and various as a desert sunset. 

A matter that was giving us serious concern was 
that of an interpreter. All of us speak Hindustani 
after a fashion, but that tongue loses much of its 
value beyond the borders of India or Burma. We 
none of us speak Chinese, which is the official language 
in most of the territory to which we were going. That 
is only half the story, for there are about twenty 
different dialects used by the natives on whom we 
would have to depend for guides and information. 
At first we tried to engage a man known, at least by 
sight, to literally hundreds of thousands of people, 
Kru, the hero of the film “Chang.” He is a Christian 


ll 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Shan, and was with Merion Cooper in Upper Siam 
for nearly two years. He speaks most of the dialects 
and is entirely fearless. We failed to get him, as the 
man to whom we cabled in Siam advised us that Kru 
was with a mission in Yunnan that was threatened 
by bandits, and was needed to help protect it. 

This was bad news, for there is nothing more help- 
less than a traveller in a strange country where he 
does not speak the language, and has no reliable in- 
terpreter. He is cheated right and left, he is told he 
cannot go where he wishes because there is no trans- 
port, whereas the real reason is that the interpreter 
does not want to go there. 

Just at this moment fate stepped in to aid us. A 
young Chinaman came with a letter from the Chinese 
Minister and asked to go with us. His name was 
Jack Young. He told us that though American- 
born he had worked as an engineer for the Chinese 
Government near the territory to which we wished 
to go, and spoke not only Chinese but many of the 
dialects. He was a slight, nice-looking boy about 
twenty years old. We closed with him at once and 
sent him to Chicago, where the Museum gave him an 
intensive course in skinning birds and small mammals. 
Then we put him on board one of my brother’s boats 
with instructions to meet us at Calcutta. 

By this time our plans were sufficiently definite for 
the Museum to announce them publicly. The day 


12 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


after this was done the reporters came to see us at 
the American Museum in New York, where we were 
brushing up our taxidermy by skinning some birds 
and mice. 

A scientist often lives in a little world of his own, 
which may seem very odd to the uninitiate. We were 
standing in a corridor talking to the newspaper men, 
when an old man with long white hair rushed up to 
me, saying, “Colonel, how I envy you going where 
those beautiful monkeys are!’ At once a reporter, 
scenting a possible story, asked, “What kind of mon- 
keys?” 

With a snort my scientific friend replied, “The 
Golden Snub-nosed Monkeys, of course! I have a 
case waiting for them downstairs,” and rushed off. 
With one accord the newspaper men turned to me. 
“Do they worship those monkeys there?” “Not there 
but here!” I answered. 

As soon as the press notices appeared letters began 
to stream into our offices. Every one seemed to want 
to go with us, regardless of sex or age. A troop of 
Boy Scouts volunteered as a body. Any number ap- 
plied as individuals. A small cousin of ours wrote to 
Kermit: 


Dear Cousin Kermit 
I have always wanted to go on an expedition for 
game or something and in the wilds, but I have never 


13 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


had a chance. I would like to go with you on one of 
your expeditions, and I saw in the papers that you 
are going on another one, so I thought I would see 
if I could go into the wilds for once in my life. I 
speak about them as the wilds because I don’t know 
what else to call them. I am 11 years old and my 
chief occupation is going to school gee its awful. I 
makes lots of huts and tunnels with other boys. 
Love from 
Daniel S. Roosevelt 
Coolidge Lane, 
Dedham Mass. 


One girl wrote: “I am eighteen, I have always 
wanted to see the world and this is my opportunity.” 
Another letter began, “Well, Colonel, when do we 
start?” Many seemed to feel that if they volunteered 
their services without pay there was no question but 
that they would be accepted. My secretary was kept 
busy typing answers to the effect that all the per- 
sonnel had already been selected and only trained 
scientists were going. 

Besides these there were a host who wished us to 
bring them presents when we returned. These ranged 
from those who asked for “Some small live animal as 
a memento,” to an enthusiastic angler who wanted 
bird feathers to make dry flies with which to fish for 
trout in Pennsylvania. One correspondent, a wo- 

14 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


man, modestly requested “A rare piece of Chinese 
porcelain.” 

Those who asked for presents were almost equalled 
by those who gave us presents. One man presented 
us with a coil of “unbreakable rope.” Then there were 
a multitude of medicines, many of them as weird as 
any that are found in Hippocrates. Some smacked 
of the witch doctor as much as of the licensed medical 
practitioner. I received a horse-chestnut, with the 
statement that if I carried it in my pocket while cross- 
ing the Himalayas I would not have rheumatism. 
The climax was reached when an acquaintance sent 
me a rabbit’s foot, saying it had carried his father 
“safely through two shipwrecks.” 

The last weeks were so crowded with work that 
they flashed by like an express train. I was speaking 
two and three times a day for Mr. Hoover, and often 
travelling all night as well. Kermit was finishing 
some business arrangements. Suydam was the only 
one who could devote his undivided attention to the 
trip. Before we knew it November 10th arrived, and 
we were on the Homeric bound for England with a 
rough passage ahead. Our wives had decided to go 
with us to Europe. Fortunately they are good sail- 
ors, for nearly every night the trunks and bags played 
tag about our staterooms. At meals the food and 
drink would leap from the table into our laps. Dur- 
ing the height of the storm we received a grim re- 

15 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


minder that great as modern science is it has not been 
able to make travel always safe. We got an S.O.S. 
from the ill-fated Vestris. Unfortunately we were 
too far away to be of any assistance. 

In London we stayed two days. I visited the South 
Kensington Museum, where they very kindly fur- 
nished me with such information as they had on the 
giant panda. Mr. Arthur Hinks, Secretary of the 
Royal Geographical Society, was most kind also, and 
took much trouble in looking up data for us in their 
records. One night Arthur Vernay, the big game 
hunter, gave us a dinner. The company was a de- 
lightful mixture of scientists and sportsmen. Two of 
them had been with Sir Francis Younghusband on 
the first British Expedition to Lhasa in 1905. One 
was a retired British Admiral who was a great au- 
thority on birds and fleas. 

In Paris we stayed two days more. There we saw 
M. Jean Delacour and had our final talk on the 
Indo-Chinese end of our expedition. We bought 
some colored plates of the animals we were seeking. 
Getting game is often largely a matter of finding the 
locality where it is. There have been animals that 
were considered so rare as to be almost non-existent, 
where the only skin in the hands of any museum had 
been taken in trade from natives. Then some one 
stumbled on their habitat and found they were plenti- 
ful but only in a restricted district. This happens for 


16 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


two reasons. The first is that in barbarous countries 
accurate information goes a very short distance. 
Tribesmen a hundred miles from the locality where 
an animal lives may not have the vaguest idea where 
it can be found. The second is that when there is no 
competent interpreter the natives may not know what 
the white hunter is asking. I have seen a white man 
laboriously explain a type of game to a group of pa- 
tiently waiting brown men, only to have a native arise, 
disappear, and return in triumph with a branch of a 
tree as his interpretation of the object sought. It 
was to cut this Gordian knot of misunderstanding 
that we bought the pictures. We could show them to 
the local inhabitants, and avoid the risk of making a 
week’s march only to find we were being guided to the 
wrong animal. 

We left our wives in Paris for they had to return 
to the children. On the 23rd we were in Marseilles. 
It is a fascinating city. Around it clings the romance 
of a long and adventurous existence. From it set 
forth Pytheas, that old Greek explorer who, in the 
fifth century B. C. sailed into the Baltic first among 
civilized men. Like many another before and since, 
his narrative was discredited when he came back, not 
because of the inaccuracies but because of the truth 
it contained. He spoke of Ultima Thule—the last 
land in the world. He spoke of a place to the north 
where there was neither land nor water nor sky, but 


17 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


a combination of all three which resembled a thick 
soup. All this his contemporaries accepted with grave 
nods. He told of the tides in the Baltic, which in 
many places flow in over the flat sands as fast as a 
man can walk, and rise ten feet or more, facts that 
any child knows to-day. That is where he made his 
mistake. Tides in the Mediterranean practically don’t 
exist. His listeners at once dubbed him a lar and 
discredited his entire story. He had piloted his vessel 
safely through the shoals of falsehood only to foun- 
der in the harbor of truth. 

Marseilles is like its history, various and colorful. 
As you walk its streets you often see the fragment 
of some medieval tower framed in the side of a mod- 
ern house as a fossil is bedded in a clay bank. The 
people who throng the streets are of every type. 
Here, a middle-aged French couple will be plodding 
along; there, a coal-black Senegalese with a red kepi 
will pass with the peculiar loose-jointed swagger of 
his race. Behind will come a rollicking group of sail- 
ors with swart skins and shining earrings, followed in 
turn by a Spahi riding a horse whose action shows 
the pride of the desert-bred. On the water-front the 
fish-wives are gathered. They are a thickset, power- 
ful lot. On their stalls lies the spoil of the Mediter- 
ranean, cuttle-fish, oysters, and shrimps; stream-line 
steel-blue mackerel, and great ugly bright red crea- 
tures with mouths like toads. Arms akimbo, they ped- 

18 


ANCHORS AWEIGH 


dle their wares with the stridency of barkers at a 
side-show. 

From there we sailed on the Narkunda of the P. 
& O. Line. After another rough passage we made 
Port Said where East and West meet. A few more 
days saw us passing through the Gate of Tears, as 
the Arabs call the straits which guard the southern 
end of the Red Sea. From the deck of his vessel the 
traveller can see Africa and Arabia on either hand, 
baking in the sun. In the old days it was a veritable 
Gate of Tears, for the ill-built, clumsy Arab dhows 
were helpless in a storm. Time out of mind they piled 
up on the coast. Even to-day it has its sinister his- 
tory. It was on Perim not long ago that a P. & O. 
liner crashed. 

After passing from the Red Sea I always feel that 
I am really East, where a European is a spectator, 
never an integral part of the deep-seated life. 

At Aden, where we stopped for a few hours, we 
went ashore and renewed old acquaintances. From 
there we bore northeast across the blue Indian Ocean, 
flying-fish flashing from wave to wave like bits of 
silver. Four lazy uneventful days passed and early 
one morning standing on deck we saw the low coast- 
line of India.

CHAPTER II 
OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


We had written to Kashmir in the hopes of secur- 
ing some of the Kashmiris who had served us so faith- 
fully in Central Asia in 1925. Avery, of Cockburn’s, 
cabled that he had engaged two new shikarries, Mo- 
khta Lone and Gaffar Sheikh, whom he would send 
to Bombay to meet us. Suydam wrote our old head 
cook, Jemal Sheikh, whom he had with him the pre- 
vious winter in Assam. Lusu, another old standby, 
was also with him in Assam, but had been left there 
with Captain Kingdon Ward, who was engaged on 
a botanical and geographical exploration of the Mish- 
mi Hills. I had written Ward asking him to send 
us Lusu if he could spare him, but had received no 
reply. 

We felt that it would make a great difference in 
the smooth starting of the expedition if we had a 
nucleus of retainers who understood our ways, and 
when we were drawing close to Bombay we spent 
time speculating as to which of our old friends would 
greet us. Both of the shikarries being new, Ted and 


I drew lots for first choice. He won and chose Gaf- 
far Sheikh.

OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


As we drew alongside Ballard Pier we were de- 
lighted to see old Jemal Sheikh and a somewhat 
emaciated Lusu waving to us. The former was quick 
to take possession of Suydam, who was more than a 
little embarrassed by his affectionate attentions, while 
the latter hurried over to ask about my wife, whose 
servant he had been in India and Nepal after our re- 
turn from Central Asia. 

We entrained for Calcutta after a day in Bom- 
bay spent in seeing old friends and admiring the 
progress the Bombay Natural History Society has 
made in its admirable programme, thanks to Sir 
Reginald Spence. 

Mervyn Morgan met us at the station and installed 
us in his delightful house in Ballygunge, where we 
stayed until we took ship for Rangoon. Our objec- 
tive being the border lands between the Chinese 
province of Szechuan and Eastern Tibet, we had 
hoped to obtain permission to enter either through 
Sadiya and the Mishmi country or up by way of 
Sikkim and Tibet. No reply to our petitions hav- 
ing been received from the Tibetan authorities, we 
adopted the alternative route, which we had always 
had in mind, passing through Burma and crossing 
into Yunnan, whence we could strike north to Sze- 
chuan. 

We were well on our way toward the heart of the 
province of Yunnan when word reached us from 


21 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Burma that permission had been accorded to us to 
go through the Mishmi country into Tibet and so 
across to Batang. Unfortunately it was too late to 
turn back and avail ourselves of the sought-for op- 
portunity. 

We found many old friends in Calcutta, among 
them Major Kenneth Mason, recently Gold Medal- 
list of the Royal Geographical Society for his nota- 
ble work in the Karakoram Himalayas. All the of- 
ficers of the Indian Survey were more than kind and 
helped us in every way possible. 

Jack Young had landed in Karachi from the 
freighter Egremont, carrying a large shipment of 
American mules for the Indian army. He reached 
Calcutta a couple of days before us, bringing with 
him the afterthoughts of equipment which we had 
gathered when the bulk had already gone forward on 
a freighter bound direct to Calcutta. 

The day before we left, Kingdon Ward arrived 
from Assam, showing very clearly the effects of short 
rations and long marches in the Mishmi Hills. I 
knew him first in Mesopotamia during the war, and 
he had taken part in our original plans for the ex- 
pedition on which we were starting. After some hur- 
ried consultations it was agreed that he should go to 
England and return during the winter to join us in 
the work along the Indo-China frontier. 

Bidding good-by to our most hospitable friend 

22 


OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


Morgan, we embarked for Rangoon on the 14th of 
December. Lusu had sent for a younger brother, 
Aziza, to join us. Through a misunderstanding he 
had gone to Bombay, whence he was due to arrive in 
Calcutta less than an hour before our boat sailed. 
Nevertheless, Lusu proudly produced him a few min-

with our five Kashmiri retainers. It was the first 
time that three of them had been on anything larger 
than a houseboat on Wolar Lake and they were pro-

by Jemal Sheikh from his store of nautical know]l- 
edge gained during his voyage from Constantinople, 
whence Suydam had returned him at the close of our 
Central Asian Expedition in 1925, and by Lusu from 
his shorter experience during a trip with me from 
Karachi to Bombay. 

Sir Charles and Lady Innes, friends of former 
days, invited us to stay with them at Government 
House, where we spent a few most pleasant days be- 
fore leaving for Upper Burma. Sir Charles had been 
of help in every way imaginable, among other things 
arranging for the purchase of thirty mules for us in 
Bhamo. 

In Mandalay we spent a night with O Ah Wain, 
one of the leading Chinese merchants in Burma, and 
the representative of the Burma Corporation, the 
huge mining company employing twenty-five thou- 

23 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


sand men, with which Mr. Hoover was at one time 
connected. Ah Wain had followed the campaign 
with great interest, for he had been well acquainted 
with the President. 

We wandered about the bazaars under the tutelage 
of a most energetic and self-sufficient Burman, 
Maung Wike, owner of a Chevrolet, and thoroughly 
endorsed Mr. Kipling’s soldier’s appreciation of the 
Burmese woman with her “whacking white cheroot,” 
six or eight inches long with a wrapper of what looked 
like bamboo fibre. 

The train journey to Katha was complicated by 
the action of wind and water in flooding a long sec- 
tion of track, but after numerous halts and changes 
we at length boarded a little stern-wheeler and 
chunked up-river to Bhamo. As companion we had 
Major Williams, returning from hospital in Man- 
dalay and still largely crippled with neuritis. He 
commanded a mixed battalion of Punjabis, Sikhs, 
and Jats. All through the cold weather they are back 
in the hills of Upper Burma on frontier service, and 
the life is far from uneventful. Last year eleven of 
his men were killed by tigers, five by one animal. One 
station brought back memories of sickness, for there 
he had lost seventeen men from cholera four or five 
months back. There are primitive problems still to 
be coped with in Burma. 

The village of Bhamo sits at the Burmese end of 


24 


OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


the old trade route across to Yunnan and China. Its 
European population consists of seven. Vivian Clerk, 
the Deputy Commissioner, had purchased thirty 
mules for us, and we hired some extra ones to take us 
across the border. 

Christmas Eve we dined with the Clerks, and after 
dinner three lots of men and boys came around from 
the missions and sang carols. The setting of palm 
and bamboo and the dark faces were remote indeed 
from all that we had hitherto associated with Christ- 
mas carols. The singing was earnest and whole- 
hearted, but it was at times difficult to determine 
either from word or tune whether it was to “Good 
King Wenceslaus” or “Once in Royal David’s City” 
that we were listening. Oyster Bay and our families 
and friends in Christ Church singing “Christmas Day 
on the River” seemed very far off. 

We spent a pleasant and interesting afternoon 
with Captain G. H. M. Medd, representative of the 
Irrawaddy Flotilla Company and for thirty-seven 
years resident in Burma. His great uncle was Ad- 
miral Cochrane, founder of the navy of Chile. Cap- 
tain Medd had many tales to tell of river adventure. 
Not long ago one of his stern-wheelers stopped to 
take on fuel after dark and turned the search-light, 
which is used to feel out the course, full upon three 
tigers which had come down to drink. The native 
boatswain in his excitement pulled the steam siren, 


25 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


and the beasts stampeded through the near-by vil- 
lage. The town elders laid before Captain Medd a 
solemn complaint of the boatswain’s action, asking 
that it never be allowed to occur again! 

The first seventeen miles on the road between 
Bhamo and Tengyueh is possible for motor trans- 
port, so on the day after Christmas we got our mules 
under way early, and ourselves followed with the bal- 
ance of our kit in motors. We got to our stage at 
noontime, and, armed with collecting guns, separated 
to start in on the natural-history work of the expedi- 
tion. 

Next morning there was the usual confusion inci- 
dent to the first few days of pack-train life. We had 
hired some mules to supplement those we had pur- 
chased, and had also arranged a riding-mule for each 
of us. It now developed that there would not be suf- 
ficient additional pack-mules to take up the slack, so 
we had to turn in half our riding-mules to lift the 
surplus loads. The system of packing the mules was 
new to me. It consisted of two skeleton saddles; one 
was placed upon the mule and was secured fore and 
aft by breastplate and crupper; there was no girth 
whatever. The second fitted neatly upon the first; to 
it was lashed the load, and lacking the support of a 
girth, it had obviously to be packed so as to nicely 
balance. To load the mules two men would lift up 
the second saddle and call to a mule, who would duck 


26 


OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


his head and run between them so that they could 
drop the load upon the base saddle. 

Our muleteers, and all those we met on the trail, 
were Yunanese. We had, as head man, a little fellow 
whose name, Chin Tso Lin, was easy to remember as 
closely approximating that of the defunct Manchu- 
rian warlord. Chin was a good man, but did not seem 
able to impress his authority sufficiently upon his fol- 
lowers. 

For our first march of fifteen miles Ted, Stevens, 
and I walked, leaving the others to share the mules. 
The trail wound in and about the mountains border- 
ing the Taping River. The jungle enclosed the path 
on either hand, a tangled mass of vines and creepers 
with great trees rising straight as a die without a 
branch for from eighty to a hundred feet from the 
ground. This trail, the principal trade route between 
Yunnan and Burma, was opened to Europeans only 
in the seventies, and such men as Cooper, Gill, and 
Baber have left us delightful accounts of their jour- 
- neyings. Cooper, after the eventful expeditions de- 
scribed in his “Travels of a Pioneer in Commerce” 
and the “Mishmi Hills,” during both of which trips he 
had more than one hairbreadth escape, fell a victim 
in Bhamo to the rifle of an Indian sepoy. Captain 
Gill, whose “River of Golden Sand” so vividly re- 
lates his adventures in Szechuan and Yunnan, met a 
tragic end through Arab treachery. Among more re- 


a7 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


cent accounts of travel and exploration in this remote 
corner of China none combines more happily a de- 
lightful narrative with information and deductions 
of value than Johnston’s “Peking to Mandalay.” 

Our first halt was at Khalongkha, and as I was 
walking through the village I caught sight of the 
head of a newly killed sambhur. Through the assis- 
tance of a shopkeeper who spoke Hindustani I finally 
found the hunter. He said that in the early morning 
and late evening both sambhur and barking deer came 
out to feed in the small clearings far up on the moun- 
tainside where the Kachins planted their crops. Ted 
and Suydam and I set off in different directions on 
the chance of coming upon either of these deer. The 
Kachin with whom I went started straight up through 
the trackless jungle and led me a merry chase be- 
fore we finally came to the upper clearings. All our 
climbing proved in vain, and neither of the others 
met with any better success. Next day Suydam 
passed two Kachins with a muntjac or barking deer 
slung between them. There is undoubtedly good 
shooting to be had in the neighborhood; but to prop- 
erly work it out time would be needed, and as our 
objective lay in the vicinity of far distant Tatsienlu, 
time to us was a most valuable commodity. 

The Chinese will go to vast expense and surmount 
great difficulties in order to bury their dead beside 
their ancestors. A very elaborate coffin had accom- 


28 


OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


panied Stevens and Jack on their river boat, and for 
the first four or five days it kept pace with our cara- 
van. A Chinese coffin is always expensive. It is built 
from hard wood, a huge cumbersome affair, some- 
what resembling a deep dugout canoe with a high 
prow. This was one of the heaviest. Suspended on 
poles, four men in front, and two behind, it swung 
along the bridle path; the coolies chanting antipho- 
nally to the corpse, advising it to lie quiet, and telling 
it that it was homeward bound. A brilliant barnyard 
cock was tied to the coffin’s lid to keep the spirit of 
the dead from rising. A few personal objects were 
also attached to the coffin. It was amazing that the 
coolies should have been able to carry their burden 
along our stages. We eventually left them behind 
when the trail became very difficult and rough across 
the China border. 

These marches through the gorge of the Taping 
were a delight. Suydam complained that the great 
height of the wall of jungle enclosed and oppressed 
us too straitly, but we had passed through it before 
I had time to sense that oppression, which we had all 
of us felt when similarly hemmed in by the forest 
during the exploration of the River of Doubt. 

Once Stevens and I made a long chukker up the 
mountainside after a band of gray langurs, twice we 
came up to them, but we failed to get a shot. At 
length they definitely eluded us and we turned back. 


29 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


We had not gone far before we heard a Kachin feud 
underway up the valley. We counted twenty shots, 
a goodly number to expend where rifles are hard to 
come by and cartridges almost as scarce. The British 
have made great strides in introducing the Pax Bri- 
tannica into the hills of Upper Burma, but the Ka- 
chins are a warlike race, and not yet prepared to 
arbitrate all their intertribal difficulties. 

As we came round a sharp bend in the trail we ran 
upon a Kachin with a rifle on his shoulder. Upon 
seeing us he jumped to one side and flung his rifle to 
aim. For a moment it looked as if we might be in 
for trouble, but the Kachin, although apprehensive, 
was apparently on the alert for some personal en- 
emy and a second later his face was wreathed in some- 
what sheepish smiles. 

We were making fairly long stages, and it was only 
by collecting birds along the route and setting out 
traps after dark that we were able to secure any of 
the small mammals and birds of the country through 
which we passed. ‘The skinning was carried on chiefly 
by lamplight. The difficulty lay in the fact that in 
order to reach the big-game grounds of Szechuan 
during the most advantageous hunting season we 
were obliged to make all haste possible, whereas for 
Stevens to obtain the collection of small game he 
sought for the Field Museum it was essential that 
he take easy marches, halting for a day or two from 


30 


OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


time to time where the country appeared propitious 
for his purposes. We knew all this before starting 
and had planned to split caravans at Tengyueh, leav- 
ing Stevens to follow along more slowly. He could 
join us at Tatsienlu, where we would return after 
hunting the hills to the north. This would enable him 
to spend a month or six weeks longer on his way to 
Tatsienlu and yet be there in time to meet us on our 
return from the hunting-grounds. 

We were very fortunate in having Stevens with us. 
In his capable hands we could feel safe in leaving 
the working out of the small-game collecting, know- 
ing that the Field Museum would receive as repre- 
sentative a faunal survey of this part of Yunnan and 
Szechuan as it would be possible to procure under the 
circumstances. Stevens had been a tea planter in 
Assam and Darjeeling for many years, during which 
he had made an intensive study of bird and mammal 
life. He has written a standard monograph on the 
birds of Sikkim. Not long ago he spent a year in 
Indo-China, but almost all his valuable collections 
were lost in a river shipwreck which came very close 
to costing him his life. Since then he has been at- 
tached to Lord Rothschild’s Museum at Tring. 

The fourth day out brought us to the Chinese bor- 
der. There was nothing to tell us that we were there 
except two Chinamen in the verandah of a grass hut, 
one lying down cooking himself an opium pill for his 

31 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


pipe, while the other tended a pot of greens boiling 
over a small fire. Neither so much as lifted his 
eyes as we passed. A better indication of the fact that 
we were in China lay in the condition of the trail. It 
was speedily evident that we had passed from the 
jurisdiction of the British Raj. A landslip or a fallen 
tree was left undisturbed so long as it was possible to 
get over or around without the labor of removing it. 
We soon passed out from the heavily forested coun- 
try into a long broad valley, checkered with paddy 
fields. The hills were all but denuded of trees. 
Chinese inns were substituted for the clean dak 
bungalows of the Burmese frontier. Although these 
inns were only occasionally clean, the innkeepers 
were invariably courteous and ready to do whatever 
lay in their power for our comfort and convenience. 
The villages through which we passed now were 
typically Chinese, consisting of a single long street 
paved with large irregular cobblestones. There were 
usually two or three caravanserais, large courtyards 
with sleeping quarters for the muleteers hived in be- 
neath the walls, and sometimes a tier of rooms above 
in which we laid out our bedding rolls. At night time 
the street was lined with the booths of meat and 
vegetable vendors, lit by the flickering wicks of oil 
lamps and tallow dips. Here the gossip of the day 
passed from group to group, and the chattering lasted 
far into the night. There was no need for an alarm 


32 


OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


clock in the serai. Cockcrow was a movable feast; 
there were multitudes of cocks and each had his own 
idea of time. Sometimes they would be perched 
right above us, and a hurried sally would be neces- 
sary to dispose of them. Cats also joined in, and 
dogs barked at intervals all night through, with an 
occasional bicker among the mules to vary the mo- 
notony. 

The greater number of the inhabitants were Shans, 
with an admixture of Chinese. It was difficult to 
say how many straight-bred Chinese there were. The 
Shans varied greatly in height, some were really tall, 
the high blue turbaned head-dress of the married 
women accentuating their height. We also saw a 
number of veritable dwarfs. The Chinese women 
were easily distinguished by their bound feet. It was 
difficult to understand how they were capable of the 
long day’s work in the fields and the heavy labor of 
water carrying with such very inadequate underpin- 
ning, for their legs seemed to have shrivelled in sym- 
pathy, and were no larger than match sticks. The 
new National Government, among many other ex- 
cellent reforms, has forbidden the binding of chil- 
dren’s feet, and in time their edict will surely take 
effect, although we saw many cases in which it was 
obviously being disregarded. 

Many of the Shan women would have been fine 
looking were it not for the disfiguring effects of 


33 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


betel-nut chewing. This concoction, composed of the 
nut of the arica palm mixed with lime, and wrapped 
in a leaf, stains the mouth a brilliant red and black- 
ens and wears away the teeth. The Chinese are not 
addicted to the use, and their white teeth stand out 
in sharp and pleasing contrast to the melanistic fangs 
of their Shan sisters. 

A market is held every fifth day in the principal 
towns; and we wandered about among the cheerful 
bargainers during a noonday halt. The Kachins had 
come down from their mountain fastnesses with loads 
of firewood strapped to their backs. The market was 
divided into quarters like a big bazaar. Here the 
sweetmeat sellers were segregated, there was the 
butchers’ row; next the vegetable vendors and the 
dealers in the gimcrack knives and petty ornaments 
that had found their way across from Burma. 

The old saying that the pig pays the rent seemed 
heartily indorsed by both Chinese and Shan. Pigs 
of all sizes swarmed in the villages and rooted about 
in the paddy fields. Great flocks of ducks and pon- 
derous water buffaloes shared their dominion in the 
fields, but all appeared a happy enough family on 
the live and let live basis. 

We owed our introduction to the ruling family of 
this valley to a misadventure of Stevens. He had 
dropped behind on the march, and in coming after us 
had mistaken the road. When he failed to appear at 

34 


OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


the noonday halt we organized ourselves into search 
parties. He had strayed far from the trail and had 
fallen in with a retainer of Philip Tao, the English- 
speaking brother of the Saw Bwa, the hereditary 
chieftain of the valley. In the late afternoon I came 
across Stevens and Tao about to embark on a small 
bamboo raft on which they were to be poled up- 
stream to our day’s destination. 

Tao asked Jack and myself to join them, and with 
some misgivings I did so, sending the mules on by 
road with Gaffar Sheikh. They were scarcely out of 
sight before I realized that my apprehensions were 
to be justified. The side stream up which we were to 
be poled shallowed rapidly and before we had gone 
three hundred yards we came to a definite halt and 
took to the shore. Traversing the ridges between the 
paddy fields offered no very formidable difficulties 
as long as the light lasted, but after nightfall you had 
need to be a lynx-eyed acrobat to avoid falls, and it 
was a weary and muddy group that stumbled into 
Kanai at half past eight. 

Tao invited us to breakfast with him in his house 
across the river next morning, so we sent the caravan 
on ahead to our next halting place, and accompanied 
our host over the broad rocky river-bed to old Ka- 
nai, on a hill overlooking which we were welcomed 
to the group of small buildings forming 'Tao’s home- 
stead. He took great pride in the garden, in which 

35 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


he had made miniature lakes and planted fruit trees 
of all kinds. We had an excellent Shan breakfast 
served in a summer house situated on an island on 
one of the lakes. The piece de résistance was drawn 
from an earthenware pot on the centre of the table. 
A. charcoal fire burned beneath the pot, which al- 
ready contained a basis of meat and vegetables when 
brought in. To it we added fresh greens and condi- 
ments from small plates placed before us. The re- 
sult, a close kin to the olla podrida of Argentina, 
was excellent. Unexpected treasures were occasion- 
ally revealed by the searching chopsticks, such as the 
duck’s head with which I presented Suydam, and the 
large rooster’s talons which Ted uncovered. 

Tao was of a modern progressive type that seemed 
out of place in this “Happy Valley” of Rasselas. He 
spoke workable English, acquired during several vis- 
its to Rangoon. He must have inherited many char- 
acteristics from his father, who had spent a number 
of years in Japan, served as Governor of Yunnan, 
and died in office in Peking. There were three other 
brothers, one, twenty years old, was born in Japan 
and left there by his father with some Japanese 
friends. He has never left Tokyo, where he is a stu- 
dent in the Imperial University. Our friend hoped 
that he would come home shortly. There is another 
younger brother who speaks no English and was clas- 
sified as “very old-fashioned.” The oldest brother, 

36 


OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


the hereditary chief, also speaks no English, and is 
addicted to opium. Philip Tao told us he had spoken 
to him three times, urging him to give up his bad hab- 
its, but had met with no encouragement, so he had 
given up his attempted reform as a bad job. 

Tao himself is no Ulysses; he briefly summed up for 
us his philosophy of travel, “Why spend much money 
to get very tired?” 

He was obviously the actual ruler. Here we were 
shown where he had to make a great bund to cor- 
rect the damages of a flood; there he had built a guest 
house in a village. He wished our opinion as to the 
possibility of adapting the valley paths to automo- 
bile traffic, for he had it in mind to bring a car 
across the mountains in parts on mule back. His 
hobby was firearms, both rifle and shot-gun. The day 
he met us he shot two geese and some ducks. There 
were swarms of cranes along the river and in the 
paddy fields, and once a great wedge of geese passed 
overhead; I counted twenty-six in the shorter side of 
the “V.” Tao did not confine himself to bird shooting, 
for in the hills he shot sambhur and barking deer, 
and once he had bagged a leopard by waiting up over 
a pig which it had killed. We regretted that we were 
unable to stop over a few days with him and try our 
luck in his hills. 

Times were not always peaceful in this “Happy 
Valley.” Twice in these last five years Chinese sol- 


37 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


diers from Tengyueh had appeared. The first time 
was apparently in connection with a general rebellion 
in Yunnan. The second must have been in the nature 
of a punitive expedition. The Shans retired to the 
hills and rolled down rocks upon the soldiers, killing 
some, both in that manner and by sniping, but the 
valley villages were looted and badly knocked about. 

We learned in Tengyueh that as a sequence Tao’s 
mother was captured by the Chinese and dragged 
about the streets in a cage. She apparently suffered 
no ill effects from this treatment. We were sorry not 
to have the opportunity of meeting her. The only 
member of the family we did see was Tao’s only son, 
a chubby baby a year old. The father explained he 
had not yet named him and asked us to choose a name. 
He wished one that began with W, so after some 
thought we made the somewhat prosaic choice of 
“Walter” as easy both to remember and to pro- 
nounce. Philip had been given his own Christian 
name by the Governor of Burma, when he was study- 
ing in Rangoon. The only book we saw in his posses- 
sion was the “Making of a Monarch,” a stalwart 
volume giving an account of Queen Victoria’s youth- 
ful training. 

We were sorry to bid our host good-by. He im-- 
pressed us as a kindly, intelligent man, very ener- 
getic, and I am afraid also very lonely, for there can 
be few to sympathize with him in his tastes and pas- 


38 


OVER THE RANGES TO YUNNAN 


times. On parting he presented us with a small gray 
pony, which accompanied us throughout the major 
part of the expedition and was left with the Fathers 
of the Mission Etrangére in Ningyuanfu. 

We were now only two days’ journey from Ten- 
gyueh. It is possible with a small caravan and good 
ponies to make the trip in seven days, but what with 
the process of shaking down, and various small de- 
lays, it was not until early in the morning of the 
ninth day that we rode into town. The last two days 
had been good going for the pack animals, and where 
hitherto we had only seen mules, we now came upon 
bullock trains. You could foretell them from a dis- 
tance, for usually each bullock carried a large bell, 
sometimes hanging above the saddle in a miniature 
belfry, at other times slung around the neck. Unlike 
our mules, the pack bullocks were girthed. 

The night previous to reaching Tengyueh we spent 
in a temple attractively set high up on a hill. China, 
with a broad tolerance, does not object to its temples 
being put to other uses. 

We had met Mr. Lawrence Peel, the Commissioner 
of Customs at Tengyueh, starting off on a short in- 
spection trip. Most hospitably he had asked us to 
stay in his bungalow, and given us a note to his as- 
sistant, Mr. Wilkinson, who helped us in every way. 
Through his aid it was possible for us to complete 
our preparations with only one day’s delay. 

39 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


We spent several hours with Chwin Yin Kyi, the 
leading banker, a fine-looking old fellow with a white 
beard. Through his banking house we opened credits 
in Talifu and arranged for the transfer of funds to 
Tatsienlu. 

There was a certain amount of purchasing to be 
attended to in the bazaar. The cook had to lay in 
supplies, and the muleteers to repair such of their 
gear as had suffered on the journey. Jack and I 
spent some time with Chin Tso Lin, wandering about 
the apothecary shops in search of a medicament that 
he told us would revive expiring mules. It is made 
from the horns of the muntjac and is very expensive. 
Twice we were offered small bottles of what pur- 
ported to be the medicine that we sought, but each 
time after smelling and tasting it Chin Tso Lin shook 
his head and proclaimed it to be unauthentic, so we 
were forced to remain unprovided for certain of the 
contingencies which our muleteers foresaw. 

On the 5th of January we bade good-by to Ste- 
vens, who was to follow us to Tatsienlu more slowly, 
stopping to collect at likely places, and headed our 
pack train toward Talifu.

CHAPTER ITI 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


For he said, I have been a stranger in a strange land.— 
Exodus 2: 22. 


AT six o’clock on the morning of January 5th we 
tumbled out of bed. It was still dark and a biting 
cold made our shorts seem wofully inadequate. Suy- 
dam said he felt as if he had on nothing at all. By 
the time we had breakfast day was breaking. Though 
mist still lay like a veil over the valley the peaks of 
the mountains that form the Sweli divide were bright 
with the rising sun. 

Soon the bedding was rolled and we stood shiver- 
ing in the compound, waiting for the muleteers whom 
we had told to be ready at seven. At eight not one of 
them had put in an appearance. 'Then came those we 
had just hired, but not a sign of Chin Tzo Lin and 
his men, who were handling our mules and going 
with us for the whole trip. Fifteen mimutes passed 
and our guard of honor arrived. It consisted of four 
chubby little soldiers looking for all the world like 
brown cherubs dressed in tattered linen. Unfortu- 
nately experiences in the past had taught us that this 
innocent expression often cloaks a very shady char- 
acter. | 

41 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


We told one of them to go at once to the serai 
where our muleteers were and tell them to bring the 
mules immediately. He disappeared and another hour 
passed. The sun was high in the sky, and Kermit and 
I decided we would go ourselves. At the serai all was 
confusion. The head muleteer was full of excuses of 
the flimsiest sort. We lined the men up and gave them 
a severe lecture. We told them we would turn them 
over to the magistrate for punishment,—the soldier 
standing at our elbow as an outward and visible sign 
of authority. Even after this matters did not go fast. 
There was a scandalous amount of shouting, but 
progress was slow. The East in a hurry is much like 
the West in a day dream. 

Finally we got them started and went back to the 
bungalow. There more trouble was waiting. While 
we had been away the door of the stable had been left 
open, and the gray pony given us by Philip Tao had 
run away. As usual no one was to blame. The fact 
remained that the pony was gone. With Jack Young 
and the two shikaris I ran through narrow, muddy, 
village lanes and splashed across paddy fields in a 
vain endeavor to find the animal. At last we had to 
give it up. 

We got back about eleven and found the caravan 
just starting. 

After the loss of the gray pony we all rode mules. 
They are not our large American mules, but litile 


42 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


shaggy creatures about the size of a Welsh pony. 
They have surprising strength and stamina, and will 
carry a man or a hundred and twenty pound load 
all day over the roughest kind of roads. We had our 
McClellan saddles and bridles which had been made 
particularly small for this reason. Even so they were 
too large. It was impossible to girth our mounts 
tightly enough. As a result, when we tried to ride up 
hill the saddle slid back to the mule’s hips. Going 
down hill was even worse, for then the saddle slid 
forward until it seemed as if it were held on only by 
the mule’s ears. It is hard work dismounting on a 
very steep rocky hill, especially when the saddle is out 
of place. The way we generally did was to reverse 
the process and throw one leg over the head. Such 
is the nature of mules that they never would stop 
when this occurred. As a result we generally slid off 
in an undignified heap. All our mules led badly. It 
would have tried the patience of Job to try to lead 
them up a rocky mountain road. The day we left 
Tengyueh, Suydam twice pulled the bridle over his 
animal’s head. 

For the first couple of hours we wound across the 
Tengyueh plain, passing clustered huts and paddy 
fields. An occasional clump of bamboo creaked in 
the wind. The graceful tops looked like ostrich 
plumes as they nodded. 

Soon our path led up a steep hill. It was the usual 


43 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Chinese “road,” made of rough ill-fitted rocks, much 
like the bed of a mountain stream. The Chinese prov- 
erb runs that a road is good for one year and bad for 
ten thousand. Personally I doubt the truth of its 
condition for the one year. 

At evening we passed over the divide and saw 
stretching before us the valley of the Sweli. Except 
where a few evergreens clung, the deforested hills 
were painted bright red by the setting sun. Just be- 
yond the crest we came on a rough stone tower, on 
which we were told beacons were lighted when there 
was war in the land. 

In the quick twilight we wound down a rugged 
trail by the little town of Hsiangpo, or Elephant’s 
Neck, to Hsmushu, where we spent the night. Our 
quarters were in the inn. A Chinese country inn does 
not in any way resemble what we mean by the word. 
It generally consists of a square surrounded by mud 
walls, with rough sheds for animals. On one side 
there is a ramshackle wooden building of perhaps two 
rooms, each with four or five board frames on which 
are lying dirty straw mats. There are no windows, 
but the walls are so poorly made that not only every 
wind but every noise and smell comes through them as 
if they were not there. The floors are usually mud. 
The only attempt at decoration are Chinese proverbs 
or sayings on tattered paper tacked here and there. 

It was in such a room that we put up for the night.

TYPICAL ROADSIDE INN 


THE MARKET AT LIKIANG
