THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


We were tired and did not have the straw mats re- 
moved. We thought that the cold weather must have 
killed the vermin, so we had our bedding rolls placed 
on them. We were wrong, as we found out later in 
the night. When we first came into the room we 
heard a faint squeaking under one bed. “It must be 
mice,” said Kermit. He was mistaken. On flashing 
a light in that corner we saw a hen, seven chickens 
and a large red rooster. With the aid of an incredibly 
old woman with bound feet, who nevertheless was 
quick as a cat, we got rid of our poultry. It was only 
temporary. Our room was evidently their ordinary 
roost. ‘They continually found their way back through 
holes in the wall, whereupon the old woman would 
dart in and remove them again. 

Supper over, we crawled into our bedding and set- 
tled down for the night. All we heard was the sooth- 
ing gurgle of our Kashmiri servants’ hookahs in the 
courtyard. Suddenly the most appalling squealing 
started in one corner. It was a cat. Suydam got up 
and chivied it away. Again we settled to sleep, but not 
for long. Two pie dogs started a battle which, from 
the standpoint of noise at least, resembled a slaugh- 
ter house where a hundred pigs were being killed at 
once. The victor settled himself at our door, and for 
the rest of the night barked at anything and every- 
thing he saw or heard. When we chased him away he 
came back. At four-thirty the muleteers were up 


45 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


feeding and watering the mules, with much shrill 
side-comment. 

We crossed the Sweli and started up the Salween 
divide. At first the country was thoroughly culti- 
_ vated. Sometimes the small fields were divided by 
hedges of candelabra-like cactus, which cannot be in- 
digenous, for I did not notice any on the surround- 
ing hills. Soon we came to dense forest. At a small 
town we found a caravan and some coolies waiting. 
They told us there were bandits on the pass ahead 
and asked to join us for protection. We were sure 
they were wrong but slipped on our Colt automatic 
pistols, for it is better to be safe than sorry. We filed 
up through the pass without incident and down into 
the Salween valley. The Chinese call it the “Valley 
of Death,” and it is supposed to be haunted by evil 
spirits. At this time of the year nothing could be less 
death-like, though in the rainy season it must be rot- 
ten with fever. Only Shans live in the bottom, which 
they have cultivated and largely deforested. 

Next morning we saw some animals, in size much 
like otters, wandering in a marshy field. I shot at 
one with a .22 rifle but missed, so I doubt if we ever 
know what they were. An excellent bridge spans the 
Salween River, which is a beautiful, turbulent stream 
of the deepest sapphire blue. 

In the afternoon we crossed a small mountain 
stream on a bridge that bore the significant name of 


46 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


“Washed Away by Waters.” Just beyond as we were 
winding up the hillside there was great excitement. 
A man came running back to say that bandits had 
attacked the caravan ahead of us. Inquiry developed 
that they must have been of a very milk-and-water 
variety, a sort of “Pirates of Penzance,” because all 
they had stolen was one mule. Our soldiers became 
much excited, evidently inspired by their duty of en- 
forcing order, and started to scramble up the hillside 
in the direction the mule had been taken. In a short 
time they returned, having recovered the mule, a di- 
lapidated gray animal which looked hardly worth the 
trouble. Then another phase of the plot developed. 
The soldiers did not return it to the owner, saying 
that now it was theirs. Apparently it was simply a 
question as to which bandit got it. 

The following day we were up before daylight, as 
we had decided to make a double march in order to 
reach Yungchang in time to spend a day there and 
make some necessary changes in our caravan. Dur- 
ing the night bullock trains had been constantly 
passing our serai. These animals, besides mules and 
ponies, are used for packing. Many of them have a 
large soft-toned bell hung from a little cupola on 
their back. The head driver uses a gong to hold them 
together. The sound of the bells punctuated by the 
deeper tone of the gong blended pleasantly. 

As day dawned we were riding along a ridge that 

AT 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


rimmed a broad plain. A white mist frosted the land- 
scape. Plain, ridge and hill seemed engraved in sil- 
ver. A bird was calling with a note like our peewee, 
only more sustained and louder. We wound up a 
barren valley. The slopes on either side were covered 
with a coarse grass. Soon the sun rose, slanting 
through the hills ahead. By nine it was full day. 
Overhead arched the changeless blue of the dry sea- 
son, with hardly a cloud. Indeed, the very name 
Yunnan means “South of the Clouds.” My enjoy- 
ment of the scenery was marred by one of our Chinese 
soldier escort, who sang continually in a pea-hen voice 
which would make a slate pencil melodious by com- 
parison. 

We had a stiff hard climb up an abominable trail. 
Occasionally coolies passed us. Often they carried 
two bell-shaped wicker cages filled with chickens, 
slung at either end of a long pole. These serve a 
double purpose, for with the bottom out they are used 
in the villages as portable coops. We halted at noon 
in the gut of the pass. A cold breeze searched it with 
the all-pervasiveness of the mountain winds and we 
could find no sheltered nook. We were drenched with 
sweat and soon were shivering. 

Our head muleteer turned up to tell about some 
trouble with one of the animals. He was a swart, 
finely built Yunanese named Ma, rather appropri- 
ately, as ma means horse in Chinese. The muleteers 


48 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


as a whole are a hardy, happy lot, generally smiling. 
Each man has charge of four or five mules. They 
rarely use their whips but shout at their animals or 
throw rocks at them. Their command of language is 
notable. Even our army mule-skinners would envy 
their ability. I have often watched a muleteer hurl- 
ing rocks and talking a steady stream of Yunanese 
Billngsgate, while struggling up an almost perpen- 
dicular hill. 

Yungchang, where we spent the night and rested 
one day, is quite a big town with a magistrate. Marco 
Polo tells of a great battle that took place here be- 
tween Chinese and Burmese. It is surrounded by high 
battlemented stone walls that have withstood many a 
siege. Now they are crumbling and the roots of bush 
and tree are prizing the gray stones apart. There are 
beautiful stone gates carved in intricate and fantastic 
designs with weird animals standing on their cornices. 
These too are mellowed by time and weather. On 
the top of one a big cactus was growing like a cockade. 

We wandered through the bazaar picking up trifles 
and watching the natives. Of course it was filthy as 
all bazaars are, but it was fascinating as most bazaars 
are. There were butchers’ booths where duck, dried 
and flattened like pancakes, hung from beams with 
entrails and other noisome pieces of flesh swarming 
with flies; confectioners with clotted, bilious-looking 
candy; apothecary shops where each medicine was a 

49 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


charm and the horns of deer as a sovereign remedy 
were displayed; junk shops with odds and ends rang- 
_ ing from old opium pipes to old military buttons, and 
the thousand and one other merchants that go to 
make up trade in the Orient. Around them wandered 
dropsical hogs, snarling dogs of every size and color, 
chickens, of which an astonishing number were roost- 
ers because the Chinese are too economical to kill 
any bird for food until it has its full growth. Over 
all was the smell of the East, indescribable and un- 
forgetable. 

Naturally the people are most interesting. Here 
in Yunnan they are dark of color,—much darker than 
the Chinese we see in our cities. Unquestionably 
they are blended with the original natives, such as the 
Shans. They dress very generally in blue cloth. It 
is home made. The traveller may see the women weav- 
ing and dyeing it by the road as he rides by. The 
poor wear a blouse and trousers much the color of our 
overalls. The better class generally wear gaberdines. 
Girls are cleanly and rather prettily clothed, some- 
times with a bright-colored headdress. The little boys 
have blouses, but their trousers resemble Gunga Din’s 
as far as the rear is concerned. 

There seems to be much eye trouble. We con- 
stantly saw men, women and children either totally 
blind or with but one eye. Many were wearing the 
spectacles which are sold casually by the junk deal- 

50 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


ers. I tried a few and could not see they contained 
anything but ordinary glass. 

The older women hobbled about on pathetically 
inadequate bound feet. The younger ones, particu- 
larly the girls, have abandoned this foolish and bar- 
barous custom. Credit for this goes in large measure 
to the National Government. China has suffered 
greatly from intellectual inbreeding. Now that she 
is able to marry her thoughts to those of other peoples 
there should be a great change. 

The National Government is striving to this end 
through obstacles that would discourage most west- 
ern statesmen. Many of their national decrees are 
simplicity itself to conceive, but to promulgate and 
put them into effect against the dead weight of preju- 
dice and tradition accumulated through the centuries 
requires courage and ability beyond the ordinary. 
Such a one is that against the binding of feet. An- 
other is the law against the deforestation which has 
destroyed and is destroying so much of the country. 
Another and even more important is that against the 
use of opium. Practically every town we have passed 
has had posters on this subject, yet the first thing we 
saw on crossing the border into China was a soldier 
lying on his side, his head on a stone, cooking his 
opium pill. Every bazaar has numbers of dealers in 
opium pipes who are well patronized. In half of the 
inns the unmistakable fumes drifted into our rooms. 


51 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Among the magistrates we had already met, two had 
the yellowish whites to their eyes and black teeth of 
the opium addict. The law seems at present to be hon- 
ored in its breach, as Jack Young said, “like prohi- 
bition in the United States.” 

While wandering through the bazaar at Yung- 
chang we came on four Tibetans, wild-looking moun- 
tainy men. Dressed in their felt boots and ragged 
heavy coats with flint and steel hanging from their 
belts, they formed a sharp contrast to the patient, 
peaceful looking lowland peasants and merchants. 
We tried to speak to them. They could not under- 
stand, but smiled and shook their heads. It was like 
a breath from the high mountains to see them, and 
carried us back to days spent in the fastnesses of the 
Himalayas. 

A little later a native rushed up to us and began 
talking excitedly, smiling all the while. He was 
clearly overjoyed to see us. He was not a Chinese. 
Jack Young spoke to him but failed to make him un- 
derstand. Then in succession we used Hindustani, 
Turki and Arabic, without avail. At last in despera- 
tion we tried French in the hope he might have been 
brought up at some French mission. That exhausted 
our accomplishments and he eventually left us rather 
sadly. He had evidently come from some country 
where there were whites and was now in a strange 
and lonely land. He had thought when he saw us 


52 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


that here were people who would understand him. 

It was in these bazaars in the back country that we 
felt most keenly our lack of knowledge. Unques- 
tionably there were really good bronzes and china in 
some of the booths, but it is impossible for the un- 
learned to tell the valuable from the worthless. Ker- 
mit and I saw in the medicine shops row after row 
of Chinese porcelain jars, filled with potions and pow- 
ders. We knew some must be good, but separate the 
sheep from the goats we could not. 

At Yungchang we paid a call on the magistrate, 
as our soldier guard was to be relieved and another 
substituted. He told us that the road to Talifu 
was much safer than it had been, as the soldiers had 
recently broken up the bandit Chang’s followers, who 
numbered two thousand. 

That night we had one of those experiences that 
always threaten in lonely lands. Kermit was taken 
with violent cramps. Something in food or water 
had poisoned him. For a time it looked very serious. 
At length with the aid of the medicine chest we re- 
lieved him. During the moments when things looked 
blackest we sent Jack Young in search of a Chi- 
nese doctor, who we heard had studied at Rangoon. 
When he came the worst was past. He carried his 
medicine in a small tin box with a brilliantly colored 
picture on the cover and he spoke a little, a very little, 
English. He shed no light on the trouble whatsoever, 


53 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


but had at least learned in his studies the medical 
creed, ““When in doubt say nothing and look preter- 
naturally grave.” 

Kermit recovered rapidly and next morning, 
though a bit shaky, was able to march. 

In the plain after Yungchang we saw three dif- 
ferent types of water mill. One, the most primitive 
of all, was a beam with a trough at one end and 
a mallet at the other. The water running into the 
trough tilts up the mallet. Then it runs out and the 
mallet descends on the grain. Another was two 
wheels on a central shaft which rotated around a 
track on which the grain was strewn. The last was a 
development of the first but much more complicated, 
for a series of levers ran a number of mallets in dif- 
ferent pestles. All three mills possessed the virtue 
that they required little attention and their power 
cost nothing. 

We also saw natives threshing. They use a flail 
much like those that were used in our country forty 
years ago, and that may be found now in the lumber 
rooms of old farmhouses. There was one real dif- 
ference,—their tail piece was of rope, not of wood, so 
they must be much less efficient. 

It was market day and the roads were thronged 
with people bringing their wares to the city. There 
were vegetables and garden truck of all sorts, chick- 
ens, ducks, wood, boards for coffins, and cloth. Pony 


54 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


caravans were jingling along laden with salt which 
comes from salt-mines eleven days’ journey away. It 
is carried in solid cubes perhaps ten pounds in weight. 
It is not white like our salt, but a dirty drab color. 

A number of times we saw enormous pigs being 
carried in baskets. Sometimes one man would be 
struggling under the weight. At other times two 
would have the animal slung between them on a pole. 
‘Once we came on two natives panting up a hill with 
a basket from which a huge boar gazed placidly, for 
all the world like a fat Mandarin making a journey. 
Why this was the custom we could not find out. Per- 
haps the pigs refused to lead or drive. If they were 
going to market they at least went in such state as 
none of our American pigs enjoy. 

On the 11th of January we crossed the Mekong. 
Its gorge at this point is far more rugged than that 
of the Salween. The mountains that form it are very 
steep and there is little of any cultivation. It looks 
what it is, one of the desolate places of the earth. The 
river itself was not as handsome as its sister stream 
to the west. It was green and rather sluggish. 

One of our Kashmiri servants, Lusu, who had 
been with us in Turkestan three years ago, told us 
that in the Mekong there was a Crim. The only time 
we ever heard of this fabulous monster before was 
near Maralbashi, thousands of miles away, where our 
shikaris were much worried because we insisted on 


55 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


taking a bath in the river, which they said was haunted 
by one. A Crim, incidentally, is a creature that lives 
in a deep pool. It eats people and cattle, some even 
say whole caravans. Naturally descriptions of it are 
vague, but all agree on one point,—that it is 


“More o’ horrible and awful, 
That e’en to dream wad be unlawful.” 


Incidentally, Baber in the account of his journey 
mentions this fable of the Mekong, but, not being as 
learned as our Kashmiri, he merely calls it a blanket 
fish. 

That night we spent in the town of Shayang. Here 
we got our first glimpse of the scourge the bands of 
robbers have been to the inoffensive small folk of the 
countryside. The main street was a line of ruins. 
House after house was bare blackened mud walls and 
piles of débris. In corners of their former homes the 
natives had put together rude hovels of bamboo and 
mud. The yamen was destroyed. The scene was as 
desolate as the devastated area in France. We asked 
what had happened, and were told that a year ago the 
bandits had descended and given the town to fire and 
sword. The leader had been a man named Yung. I 
am glad to say that since then he was killed by his 
followers in a dispute over the division of spoils. 

On the next day, after a stiff two hours’ climb, we 
came into the first really wooded country we have 

56 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


seen since just after we left Burma. The mountain 
crests and upper slopes were covered with forests of 
pine and rhododendron. Of the latter none were in 
bloom but one species. Its blossom was a living scar- 
let and formed a vivid contrast to the sombre green 
of the foliage. Flaming on the hillside it seemed like 
Moses’ burning bush. 

For some reason our mules were very fond of the 
green pine needles, snatching mouthfuls of them con- 
tinually,—a taste I have never seen even in mules be- 
fore. 

The following day we got a further reminder of 
the bandits. As we passed a few huts some people 
ran out and chatted excitedly at our escort of six sol- 
diers. It developed that they were warning us that a 
band of robbers were lying in wait on the main trail. 
Accordingly we turned off and took a mere track to 
the right, and the afternoon passed without incident. 

The whole country had made me think of the words 
of Deborah, “The highways were unoccupied, and 
the travellers walked through the byways.’ I hope 
that as a result of the labors of the present Central 
Government the people may be “delivered from the 
noise of archers in the places of drawing water.” 

We were now travelling through a real wilderness. 
As far as the eye could reach in every direction for- 
ested peak succeeded forested peak. The population 
was very scanty. Every hour or so we would come on 


57 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


a hut or two by a small hard-bought clearing on a 
steep hillside, but no villages worthy of the name. 

At sunset we arrived at the little town of Lachu- 
di; in English, “The Place of the Candle-Makers.” 
It was a miserable village, with half a dozen houses 
and a serai. Its only resources seemed to be fierce 
dogs and pigs. These two animals, indeed, are the 
commonest in this part of China. The pigs are the 
most numerous. They are all sizes and shapes and 
live in or out of the houses indiscriminately. 

By this time the weather was getting raw and cold. 
Every night the temperature went below freezing. 
Our heavy bedding-rolls, trusted companions of for- 
mer days, stood us in good stead. In the morning be- 
fore sunrise it was very difficult to leave their warm 
folds for the frost-covered icy world that lay outside. 
This morning, when I did get out of my blankets, I 
walked to the top of a near-by ridge. Though the vil- 
lage I had left lay in the bitter twilight, a pale cold 
sun had lighted the country before me making it seem 
even more melancholy and desolate. 

Again that day we were reminded of the outlaws, 
for the first village we passed, Huanglinpu, was 
nearly levelled to the ground. About a year before it 
had been sacked, but not satisfied with this the ban- 
dits had returned only three weeks ago, and destroyed 


the feeble attempts of the remaining people to re- 
build it. 


58 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


The following day all the natives agreed we would 
go through the worst of the bandit country when we 
climbed a pass and descended to Yungpeh. We got 
up particularly early. There was no sunrise. For the 
first time during our march the sky was covered with 
clouds. Soon it began to sleet and rain. Kermit, Suy- 
dam and I walked and carried our rifles. The trail 
was glassy with mud and slime-coated rocks. We 
slipped and scrambled up the steep mountain side. 
Two or three times I came a cropper, landing among 
the rocks, rifle and all, with as loud a clang as the 
Great Lord of Luna under the sword of Horatius. 
The mules fared no better, sliding around under their 
loads like the proverbial hog on ice. We had adopted, 
with modifications, the approved military formation 
for guarding a baggage train. I formed the ad- 
vance guard with two soldiers. Kermit protected the 
centre with a couple more, while Suydam brought up 
the rear with the remaining two. I walked about fifty 
yards ahead of the lead mule, largely because I 
thought that the sight of a fully armed white man 
with two soldiers might discourage any one with de- 
signs on the caravan. My two soldiers were as active 
as crickets. They ran this way and that in spite of 
the wretched going, ranging over the front like bird 
dogs. Soon it became impossible to see anything, for 
mist, drifting and eddying through the trees, flowed 
up the valley and engulfed us. I had to drop back to 


59 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


keep contact with our train. The stumbling drivers 
and mules swathed in the white fog looked ghost- 
like. 

In the early afternoon we dropped down a steep 
slope to a broad valley. We had not seen a sign of 
a bandit. Probably they were too sensible to go out 
in such weather. 

We spent that night in the little walled town of 
Yungpeh. When we arrived we were thoroughly 
drenched. The serai where we stopped had been made 
into a veritable cesspool by the wet. We stripped and 
put on our pajamas. Suydam, for evident and prac- 
tical reasons, wore a coal-black pair. Probably the 
most striking garment we had with us was his extra 
pair. They were purple silk. His other clothes did 
not maintain this standard. He had forgotten his 
good coat for state occasions, so when we made any 
formal call he wore a light brown ready-made hunt- 
ing coat with an extremely large oil spot. It bellied 
out in the back like a bag. 

Next morning it was still raining, so we got out 
from our baggage some large black umbrellas in- 
tended for use much later when the rainy season be- 
gan. Under these we plodded along the road looking 
like Robinson Crusoes, beards and all. At noon we 
sat around a very small fire, and while the rain 
dripped off my umbrella I worked on this chapter. 
That night we stopped in the usual little bandit- 


60 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


ravaged town. We had the usual number of false 
alarms. Once one of the caravan men rushed in to 
say bandits were looting the next village. 

Next morning we marched up the rough gorge of 
the outlet of Tali Lake. By noon we reached the 
walls and fort of the city of Siakwan. It lies on the 
shores of the lake and is the real business centre of 
the district. 

Here we stopped to arrange our money transfers. 
This is a most complicated matter as the different de- 
nominations, though ostensibly dealing in the same 
medium, the cent, have entirely different relative 
values. The standard, of course, is the dollar “Mex,” 
worth about fifty cents of our money. The half-dollar 
piece is the most valuable,—the paper dollar the least. 
One dollar in half-dollar silver pieces is worth many 
dollars in paper. The twenty-cent pieces stand in be- 
tween. It takes some 120 cents in them to equal 100 
cents in the half-dollars. This is because certain en- 
terprising officials had them coined in brass outside 
of the country. To make matters worse, the values 
are different in most places and in certain districts 
paper and small coin are not accepted at all. The final 
straw is added by the practice of separate coinage for 
the different provinces. Making out an income tax 
in America is simple compared with figuring ex- 
change in China. 

While we were in the city we bought some furs for 


61 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


presents. They were not very numerous and of course 
either raw or poorly tanned. We picked up half a 
dozen pelts of the small panda, a little creature of a 
bright reddish color with a ringed tail like a raccoon. 
The Chinese call it the ho hu, or fire fox. 

In the afternoon we marched north along the shores 
of Tali Lake. The water was a deep blue. A number 
of small junks dotted it. On our left lay the moun- 
tains capped with snow. Over them hung a heavy 
bank of clouds. From time to time chilly gusts carry- 
ing spurts of rain swept down from them. 

At dark we made the city of Tali, and after a half- 
mile walk over slippery cobbles came to the China 
Inland Mission. There we were most hospitably re- 
ceived by Mr. W. J. Hanna and his wife. During 
the time we spent in the city they did everything for 
us, from looking out for Kermit, who had a return 
of his Yungchang trouble, to engaging additional 
muleteers and a helper for our cook. This latter had 
long been a problem. ‘Twice before we had apparently 
succeeded in hiring a Chinese boy, but both times 
when our caravan moved in the morning he had de- 
serted. This helper, of course, spoke no language 
known to the rest of our servants, but a satisfactory 
system was devised by a series of signs and grins. 

There are but four Kuropeans in Tali, the Hannas 
and two French priests. Though all are friendly their 
intercourse is limited. Periodically they all dine to- 


62 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


gether and converse in Chinese, the only language 
they have in common. 

We spent two days here making arrangements 
and resting the men and mules. It is an interesting 
city. Once it was a far greater place than it now is, 
but the wars incident to the Mohammedan rebellion 
some fifty years ago partially destroyed it. There are 
the remains of heavy gray stone outer walls, but much 
of the land within is waste. In the mornings the city 
was lovely. The sun shining on the white pagodas 
made them look like pillars of snow. Behind was the 
mountain with tier on tier of graves climbing its side 
and frowning clouds on its frosty summit. 

Mr. Hanna was a mine of information. He had 
spent more than twenty years in the country and 
spoke the language like a native. Much of his time 
he has spent ministering to bodies, as well as souls, as 
always must be done with primitive people. The great 
twin curses of the land are opium and poverty. Each 
breeds the other. He told us fully 80 per cent of the 
men are drug addicts. The French priests at Ning- 
yuanfu later testified to the same proportion. The 
taxes that the people pay are grinding and leave lt- 
tle to them but a bare existence bought by ceaseless 
labor. There are no public improvements. Education 
is practically non-existent. ‘The women have the worst 
time. In the lower classes they do much of the por- 
terage. The streets and roads are lined with them, 


63 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


ranging from girls to grandmothers, bending under 
loads that would tax the strength of a strong man. 
They bear from eight to twelve children, but rarely 
more than four survive. Mr. Hanna said that one 
of the commonest cases he had was opium poisoning, 
generally among women. Life held nothing but gray 
days for them and they wished to end it. Naturally 
the people know very little of anything beyond their 
immediate surroundings. As far as other nations go 
they live in as fantastic a world as that of Sir John 
Mandeville. They believe in a nation of women, a 
race with tails, and, strangest of all, a people with 
a large hole through the centre of the stomach. When 
one of these people, who literally might be said to 
have no bowels, wishes to go somewhere, he gets two 
coolies who thrust a pole through the hole, take it on 
their shoulders, and trot off with him suspended be- 
tween. 

Mr. Hanna also told us that in his medical work he 
often had cases due to the bandits. Men came to him 
with hands and arms severed or great gashes in head 
or body, the wounds untended and generally crawl- 
ing with maggots. The Chinese authorities make war 
on the outlaws with equal ferocity. Some months ago 
it was discovered that certain robber chiefs lived in a 
village not far from Siakwan. The local troops 
marched on it, formed a cordon around it, and then 
fired it. Not a living thing was allowed to escape. 

64 


THE BACK DOOR OF CHINA 


Man, woman and child either perished in the flames 
or were shot down by the soldiers. 

During the two days we were in Tali Mr. and Mrs. 
Hanna gave us the most delicious food to eat. Only 
those who have lived for weeks on a series of chickens 
muscled like professional athletes, cooked by a jun- 
gle Mohammedan, can appreciate what a good din- 
ner means. One day we had roast beef. It was tough, 
but we ate every scrap. Mr. Hanna apologized for it 
by saying that the Yunnanese never kill a cow except 
to save it from dying. 

The evening before we left we were saying good 
night to the Hannas when we heard a commotion in 
the courtyard and saw in the flicker of the lanterns 
Azizo, the Kashmiri we had left with Stevens. 
Wreathed in smiles he announced, “Stevens Sahib 
idar hai.” We trooped out into the street and saw his 
caravan clattering along over the cobbles. In a mo- 
ment he appeared riding on the gray pony which had 
run away at Tengyueh. We were delighted to see 
him as we had been worried at the thought of his go- 
ing alone through the supposedly bad bandit country 
that lay between us and Likiang. That very evening 
we arranged for additional mules to take him with us, 
and turned into our sleeping bags much relieved. 


65 


CHAPTER IV 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


Own the morning of the 20th we sorted out our 
loads, and getting the combined caravans into some 
semblance of shape set off along the lake on our first 
day’s stage to Likiang. If you halt for even a day 
it is invariably difficult to get under way again with 
any despatch, and our situation was further compli- 
cated by hiring additional mules for Stevens. It was 
therefore late when we started, and it was comfort- 
ing to think that we would have an early moon to help 
us on the march, which, it took no prophet to fore- 
tell, would not be terminated until after dark. 

Tali Lake is about twenty-five miles long, but no- 
where more than three miles across. There is a good 
deal of produce transported to and fro in small junks, 
whose owners further eke out a livelihood by fishing. 
The lake fish which we tested were no great delicacy, 
but we were told that there are two sorts, one de- 
scribed as something in the nature of a trout, which 
are well worth a place on the table. As we reached 
the upper end of the lake we saw ducks and cranes, 
and had we made an earlier start we might have 
added a welcome variation to our bill of fare. Ste- 
vens collected a number of birds and two squirrels. 


66 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


The muleteers groaned and complained about the 
moonlight march, but we explained to them that it 
had been entirely with them to obviate it, and that 
we had warned them that a late start spelled a late 
arrival. No amount of experience could impress this 
simple fact upon our grasshopper muleteers. 

When we reached Tengchwan, the small walled 
town which was to be our night’s halt, we found the 
gates closed, and a lengthy parley ensued, termi- 
nated by our passing through the crack one of our 
visiting-cards. The gates were thereupon flung open. 
These visiting-cards, which we had each had printed 
in Chinese, proved most useful upon all sorts of occa- 
sions, and secured passage for messengers and grain 
for our mules, often when undecipherable to the re- 
cipients. The only available serai was both small and 
dirty, and soon the patio was a confused jumble of 
kicking and squealing mules, shouting muleteers, and 
toppling pack-saddles. It was late before we evolved 
a sort of order out of the chaos. 

Next day we rode for some distance along the bank 
of the large canal through which flows the river that 
feeds Tali Lake from the north. It was market day, 
and through the stream of traffic our mules buffeted 
a passage. Sturdy donkeys and ponies laden with 
firewood bore down upon us, sheering off at the last 
moment. Stocky peasants carried newly fashioned 
chests of drawers on their backs; above the women’s 


67 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


heads towered great baskets of vegetables. Most 
colorful were the loads of red peppers; bundles 
heaped on top of baskets, with peppers bursting 
through the seams. Next came. two youths with a 
pig in a pannier slung between them on a pole, its 
head sticking out with a foolish uncertain expression. 
Three types of carrying gear were in use, the shoul- 
der straps of the Adirondack guide, the tump line 
across the forehead worn by certain American In- 
dians, and a wooden yoke fitting around the neck. 
Sometimes these last two methods were combined. 

Niukai, Ox Village, was our night’s halt. There 
are some hot springs near by, and when we got in we 
were greeted by the information, retailed through our 
Kashmiris, that there was a hamman, a Turkish bath, 
in town. Jack and I went off to investigate; we 
found that a wooden shed had been built over a rock- 
edged tank which was fed by a water channel run- 
ning from the hot springs. There was also an outlet, 
but the men and boys grouped around the pool, and 
the quantity of foreign matter visible floating in it 
did not render it a tempting bath to any one who had 
been able to indulge himself as lately as three days 
previous at Talifu. 

The bugaboo of brigandage was never far distant. 
The General from Talifu with his entire army (we 
never could learn even approximately of what this 
consisted) was encamped not far off, engaged in an 

68 


GOING TO MARKET 


“MAIN STREET” IN YUNNAN 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


expedition against some particularly militant ban- 
dits. The magistrate had told us that the General 
would provide us with some regular soldiers to escort 
us on our next day’s march, which led through what 
was regarded as a special hunting-ground of the out- 
laws. We sent the magistrate’s letter to the General 
and he replied that he had no regulars to spare, and 
advised us to go another and roundabout way. At 
length, while still admonishing us not to proceed, he 
provided us with a dozen militia. 

The country through which we passed on our first 
stage to Tienwei was indeed admirably suited for 
a field of operations for brigands. High, pine-clad 
hills enclosed us, and through them the trail wound 
its smuous way, often so deeply cut that it was no 
more than a channel three or four feet wide with 
walls ten or twelve feet high on either hand. A swarm 
ef men and women with burdens of grain and vege- 
tables and household goods had availed themselves of 
the protection of our passage. They were dotted 
about here and there among the mules, but got so 
much in the way that we eventually segregated them 
in the rear. 

The long winding ascent brought us to grass up- 
lands, with pine-clid hills hemming them in. We 
passed a small hamlet where a handful of soldiers 
stood aside to watch us. They were regulars, better 
armed and clad than our ragged draft. We later 

69 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


learned. that they had made an unsuccessful attempt 
to extract tribute from our muleteers. Four or five 
miles further on we heard shots behind, which our 
retinue interpreted as a brush between the bandits 
and the soldiers we had passed. 

I was acting rearguard. As we made our way 
down from the plateau we trooped through a village 
out of which buzzed an irate inhabitant with an an- 
tique gun in his hands. His endeavor to halt our foot 
followers met with only partial success. His fury 
rendered him so inarticulate that it was some time be- 
fore I could discover his grievance. It developed that 
he wished to collect money for having afforded a safe 
passage, although in just what way he had been re- 
sponsible was hard to say. Our soldier escort had 
probably already received something of the sort, for 
they took an active part in the altercation and left the 
impotent villager furiously gesticulating. 

The inhabitants of the country through which we 
were passing are known to the Chinese as Minchia, 
which means simply “dwellers.” This part of Yun- 
nan is a rich field for an ethnologist, but probably, 
owing to the lack of written records, much will ever 
remain guesswork, no matter how extended the re- 
search may be. There has been a great deal of specu- 
lation as to whether any trace of the so-called original 
inhabitants remains. Possibly all the different tribes 
now living in the lands are remnants of immigration 

70 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


and invasion. In a comparatively small area may be 
found Mozos, Lolos, Mantzu, Minchia, and Nashi. 
With all of them the Chinese have to a greater or less 
extent mingled and intermarried, but as yet not suf- 
ficiently to make any real impression, and as far as it 
is possible to foresee there is very little chance of their 
doing so for a long time to come. 

The young Minchia girls whom we met along the 
trail had fine features and rosy cheeks; if their Sat- 
urday baths came around more often, they would cer- 
tainly have been both attractive and good to look at. 
In many cases the apple cheeks were of artificial ori- 
gin, and pigments must have been cheap and plen- 
tiful. 

Both men and women wore coats and jackets of 
untanned skins, usually of domestic animals. We 
passed one young fellow with a fine coat made from 
a black bear’s hide. The hair was always left on, and 
usually worn outside. 

We were scattered about throughout the length of 
the caravan for better protection against a possible 
raid, and some of our Kashmiris were detailed to 
special loads of value. One of the latter, Gaffar 
Sheikh, when far separated from 'Ted, Suydam, and 
myself caught sight of a black bear above him on the 
mountainside, within what he described as long rifle- 
range. 

Tienwei we found to be an attractive village close 


71 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


by a largish lake. Just on entering it a temple stands 
amid a grove of chestnut trees; the conventional lions 
of China guard its entrance, and its enclosure is dotted 
with stone arches and pillars and minor shrines, all 
leading to the main temple with its columned entrance 
and courtyard and altar. Even in its autumnal trap- 
pings it was a temple of romance, and made one wish 
for a sight of it when the leaves of tree and bush are 
young and when flowers stud the grass. 

Our fourth day’s march from Talifu took us 
through a good-sized walled town, Kienchwanchow. 
In contrast to the pillaged and razed villages of which 
we had seen so much, this town seemed unusually 
prosperous; the walls were in excellent repair, and 
many houses were in process of construction. The 
long main street with busy shops on either side 
stretched out indefinitely. Kienchwanchow is prob- 
ably an excellent haven from bandits and real-estate 
values must be soaring. As we walked on hundreds 
of coolies with pine boards on their backs swarmed 
past us toward the town. A whole squad was loaded 
with heavy coffin boards of the conventional design, 
and calculated by their cost to plunge the family of 
the deceased into hopeless debt. 

The valley through which our march lay was eight 
thousand feet in altitude, and its wintry appearance 
and the snow-covered mountains that surrounded it 
made the groves of fish-tail palms about the farm 


72 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


houses and villages seem very much out of place. 
Many of the brier hedges and groups of almond trees 
were in bloom. Occasionally a flowering cherry tree 
added a splash of additional color. 

We passed an unusual number of wayside shrines; 
beside a stone bridge stood an altar with an image of 
a cheerful, fat, laughing god, corresponding to the 
familiar Hotei of Japan. 

Stevens brought in two Brahminy ducks and a fal- 
cated teal, a species peculiar to Asia. Aside from their 
scientific interest we found them a pleasant addition 
to the larder. 

Next day we reached Likiang, after a lovely 
march among rolling pine-clad hills with snow moun- 
tains in the distance. We walked through patches of 
snow for the first time. The weather was that of a 
brisk day in late October on Long Island. After a 
short halt for lunch among the pines we crossed a last 
valley and over the final ridge from which we de- 
scended to Likiang. Behind it towered Mount Sat- 
seto, more than twenty-one thousand feet high. 

Mr. Andrews, of the Pentecostal Mission, rode out 
to meet us. His birthplace was Somerset, but his 
wife proved to be a fellow countrywoman, hailing 
from Cleveland. Our protests were overruled and 
the hospitality of the Andrewses prevailed, so that in- 
stead of going to a serai we stayed with them in the 
Mission compound. Such a welcome to an unkempt 


73 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


ragged outfit must, we felt, be attributed to the ex- 
treme scarcity of foreigners, for Andrews could count 
on the fingers of one hand those who had passed 
through during the six years since he first came to 
the post. No one could have been more kind nor 
taken more trouble than Mr. and Mrs. Andrews. 

We sallied forth immediately with Mr. Andrews 
to make the various arrangements with regard to fi- 
nances and food and mules. We were fortunate in 
finding a merchant who agreed to give us a credit of 
four thousand five hundred Chinese dollars (equiva- 
lent to about one thousand eight hundred dollars) in 
Tatsienlu. Next day the merchant asked if we would 
let him send two mules with us. We agreed, provid- 
ing there was a muleteer with them. Later the two 
loads arrived at the compound without mules or 
driver, but with a present of Chinese cakes and con- 
densed milk in gaudily painted wooden boxes. Be- 
fore long it dawned upon us that our friend proposed 
sending the cash for our credit with us on our own 
mules and in our own care! We reiterated our origi- 
nal agreement, but neither pack animal nor man ap- 
peared, and we left the loads in the compound. 

Mr. Andrews proved indefatigable. He was ob- 
viously intimately known and liked by the adults of 
Likiang, but it was the children whose hearts he had 
completely won over. The night we spent with him 
there were two hundred and seventeen gathered to 


74 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


sing hymns, and listen to a short talk. At times he 
shows them lantern slides and tells them the story of 
the pictures. Wherever we walked there was invari- 
ably a group of children about us, like porpoises 
sporting around an ocean liner. Some were looking 
after Mr. Andrews’ dog and seeing that it got into no 
bickerings with the town mongrels. Others were 
ready to run messages or warn merchants that we 
were about to call. With each and all he chaffed and 
joked, and we might have been part of the procession 
of a benevolent Pied Piper. 

Jack had gone with our cards to the Magistrate 
and brought back the disquieting news that the au- 
thorities said there was a band of eight hundred Ti- 
betan marauders hovering in the offing, and we would 
not be allowed to go on. We heard that the troops 
had gone forth to drive back these bandits, but had 
themselves been routed. This complication caused a 
day’s delay before we were able to secure our permits 
and a bodyguard of five soldiers. 

The day was not wasted, for through Mr. Andrews’ 
aid and energy we exchanged for ponies some of our 
mules that were too evidently showing the weariness 
of travel. In addition I got from him a sturdy white 
riding pony, admirably adapted to the work before 
it. We hired a subsidiary caravan of Tibetans, great 
stalwart fellows with finely chiselled wild features. 
Luzon was their headman. A cheerful Nashi, A-ju, 


75 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


with two mules, also joined us, and most important 
of all, Hsuen, born in Likiang of Tibetan parents, 
and speaking many dialects, was added. He had 
learned some English at the mission—the only man 
we met in Likiang who could speak a word. He was 
hired as caravan head, to take the responsibility from 
well-meaning, hard-working little Chin Tso Lin’s in- 
competent hands. 

Likiang is a busy town. On a central square there 
each day congregates a throng of buyers and sellers. 
Everything to fill the wants of the inhabitants was 
ready for purchase. It was a lively gathering—a 
cheerful crowd to rub elbows with. 

As we trailed about the narrow streets Mr. An- 
drews pointed out the serai where General Pereira 
stayed when passing through on his last tragic jour- 
ney. 

On the 26th of January, after the usual delays in- 
evitable to starting a newly composed caravan on its 
initial stage, we moved out up the valley. During 
the next five days we would be marching through the 
mountains enclosed in the great bend of the Yangtze. 
The river, although invisible, would be only a short 
distance to our left and to our right. 

Our first halt we bivouacked beside a rocky out- 
crop in the valley, close below the snowy peak of 
Mount Satseto, rearing its twenty-one thousand foot 
summit thirteen thousand feet sheer above us. Ti-

betans seem impervious to wind, and were uncon- 
cerned at the blasts that nearly blew us out of our

The morning proved a dismal awakening. Half 
our own caravan had strayed, in addition to a few 
mules and ponies from the hired caravan. After a 
long search and a wait filled with gloomy forebod- 
ings, all but two of our mules were found, and the 
Tibetans were missing but one. Jack’s pony also 
failed to put in an appearance. Adjusting our packs 
anew we set out and made a short march, camping in 
a sheltered dell with a noisy brook beside us and the 
glaciers of the white peaks straight above. We put 
up our tents and fared well. Ted and Jack Young 
set out lines of traps and caught four mice of two 
species. 

Next day we did some stiff “up going,” as our 
Kashmiris called it. We were now amid snow, ard 
camped that night near a lonely Lolo hut in a very 
exposed hollow. The men left behind to look for the 
strays returned, reporting no success. It was bit- 
terly cold, and preparing yesterday’s haul of mice 
with numb fingers was not an agreeable task. Dur- 
ing the night the Kashmiris’ tent blew down several 
times, and we were constantly expecting to be buried 
in the folds of our small tents. They held firm, how- 
ever, but it was a cold, uncomfortable night, and we

TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


each made mental reservations that no matter how 
late we reached our halting-place we would in the fu- 
ture bend every effort to securing some sort of shelter 
from the blasts that came roaring down the moun- 
tainsides. We could hear them thundering through 
' the tree-tops some little time before they struck— 
quite sufficiently in advance to allow us the full dread 
of anticipation. 

Each day it snowed a little with occasional flurries 
of hail. Our way led through great forests of fir and 
pine and hemlock and spruce, with occasional groups 
of oak. This part of the world is the happy hunting- 
ground of the botanists, and with it are associated 
such names as Rock and Forrest and Kingdon Ward. 
Reginald Farrer’s: delightful accounts in the “Rain- 
bow Bridge” and the “Eaves of the World” deal 
with his travels in the province of Kansu, for his work 
was always north or south of this country, and it 
was in Burma that he made his last expedition and 
met his death. Our fellow countryman, Joseph Rock, 
I had last seen when he lunched with me at India 
House in New York just before returning to Yun- 
nan. At Likiang we heard the good news that we 
should find him at Yungning. 

The fauna of this region has been but little worked. 
Roy Andrews and Edmund Heller made a compre- 
hensive survey of parts of Yunnan, their farthest 
north being Likiang. Both Rock and Forrest in their 


78 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


spare moments collected birds and turned in exceed- 
ingly valuable collections, but their best efforts were 
quite naturally turned to botany. 

Stevens was very keen at the prospect of entering 
what in many respects must prove a virgin territory, 
and we were in much the same situation as regarded 
the large game. If anybody has hunted big game 
here he has certainly left no record of it. This not 
only applies to a comprehensive study of the district, 
but even to sporadic hunting. From Rock we had 
heard rumors of stag and takin and serow and goral, 
but he was always careful to add that he never car- 
ried a rifle, and his interest being in the flora of 
the country he paid little or no attention to the big 
game. : 

For the last three days of January we continued 
to march through these glorious wooded mountains. 
Looking forward from some pass such a maze of 
towering mountain ranges would fill the horizon that 
it would seem impossible that there should be a way 
to weave through them. The patient trail, however, 
wound along ridges, down into valleys, and around 
mountain shoulders, tracing a tortuous course, always 
in the same general northeasterly direction. 

Our Bhamo muleteers, with the exception of Chin 
Tso Lin, were useless. Always lazy and worthless, 
they now gave in completely with the unaccustomed 
cold and snow. The trail at times was very difficult, 


79 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


particularly where the sun could reach it but for a 
few hours and partially melt the snow and ice. Leop- 
ards were reported plentiful and savage, but all 
we saw of them were some fresh tracks along the 
path. 

One bitterly cold night two more of our horses got 
loose and were never found. That afternoon saw us 
embarked on our stiffest climb, to a pass over fifteen 
thousand feet from which we would drop, with one 
more ascent, down into the valley of the Yangtze. 
One of our Bhamo mules gave out completely, and 
five or six others, even though unloaded or with very 
light packs, caused us much anxiety. They had not 
only come a long distance, but in addition the incom- 
petence and carelessness of the muleteers had added 
to their hazards. Chin Tso Lin and Tsao, Forrest’s 
former servant whom we had picked up at Talifu, 
both suffered from the altitude and lay down fre- 
quently in the snow. Their comrades were callous to 
their sufferings, and had not Ted realized Tsao’s 
plight the poor fellow might well have been left be- 
hind on the trail to die of exposure. 

Our first view of the Yangtze showed it a thin 
silver thread far below us. Valley there was none, it 
appeared to run through a deep narrow gorge, the 
walls rising precipitously on either hand. 

The night before crossing the river we stopped at 
the Lolo hamlet of Fengkow. The villagers streamed 


80 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


out to review us and our accoutrement. Great and 
small, all wore the old familiar pigtail. Hitherto we 
had seen none, save those of our Tibetan muleteers, 
for the Chinese had cut them off, following the cus- 
tom and edicts of the new order. 

Next morning Ted and I took Lusu and four 
mules and went on in advance in order to reach Yung- 
ning ahead of the caravan and start in on arranging 
for the hire of mules and purchase of supplies for the 
three weeks’ journey to Tatsienlu. An hour and a 
half’s descent brought us to the river’s bank, here 
at an altitude of four thousand feet above sea level. 
The ferry reminded Ted of the craft in which the 
Jumblies put to sea. After each trip it was necessary 
to bale furiously before again embarking. The mules 
scrambled over the high sides without difficulty or 
fuss, but Ted’s pony was unaccustomed and went 
through a course of gymnastics, during which it 
seemed inevitable that he would put a foot through 
the bottom of the boat. 

An hour’s distance from the farther bank we halted 
at the village of one of our muleteers. He was a fine 
looking fellow that we had met on the trail. His 
outfit had been travelling without loads so we had 
engaged a few of his mules to spare our weary ones. 
The cost was light enough, less than thirty cents a 
day a mule. 

Our host’s residence proved him a man of substance 


81 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


and consequence. It was built around a courtyard. 
In the lower story the principal apartment was a 
spacious kitchen, the rafters blackened with the soot 
of generations of cooking. Hung around the walls 
were riding gear and swords and ancient blunder- 
busses. In niches were the Familiar Gods of the 
house. The great copper pots shone in the obscurity. 
We had our lunch upstairs on a balcony in front of 
the family shrine, a well-built altar ornamented with 
figures of the deities and with brazen incense bowls. 
On either side were shelves with volumes of the Bud- 
dhist Bible bound in old carved wood, a collector 
might well say “in the original boards.” 

When we came out to resume our journey we 
found Ted’s pony suffering from a colic brought on 
by a “surfeit of green peas.” Our host proved equal 
to the occasion. From the folds of his robe he pro- 
duced a small silver box. Extracting a pinch of 
snuff-like powder he blew it, through a long metal 
tube, up the nostrils of the pony. Twice he did this, 
each time taking care not to let the pony sneeze the 
powder out. The effect was undeniably good. 

During the afternoon we climbed up a stiff ascent 
to spend the night in a mountaineer’s hut. While 
climbing we several times heard “the thunder of Ti- 
betan drums” and passed groups of native priests 
gathered together performing upon drum and cym- 
bal. As far as we could learn they were not orthodox 


82 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


Lamas, but more in the nature of native witch doctors. 
One gathering that was close by a house was engaged 
in curing a sick man. 

Next morning we were off at six-thirty, with a 
series of stiff climbs ahead of us. It is easy to tell a 
principal trail by the wealth of worn-out grass san- 
dals with which it is strewn. The rougher and rockier 
the path, the greater the number of sandals. The 
snow lay deep in the hollows and the horses we led 
behind us struggled and slipped on the icy ascents 
and descents. Eight hours’ steady marching took us 
to the Lamasery of Yungning. A struggling line of 
huts outside the walls formed the village. Inside the 
enclosure there were a large temple, several lesser 
ones, and many houses, both great and small, in which 
the Lamas lived. 

We received a friendly welcome from the Abbot, 
who told us that Doctor Rock was on an island in a 
lake, distant three hours’ ride. We despatched a mes- 
sage to him, and set to work on the necessary ar- 
rangements for securing new mules and supplement- 
ing and augmenting our equipment. My only coat 
had been burned by blown embers from the camp- 
fire; the Abbot kindly let me have some of the Ti- 
betan-made cloth of his yellow robes with which to 
patch it, so that I became in part a yellow Lama. An 
excellent native dinner was prepared for us, with yel- 
lowed ivory chop-sticks to take the place of knife and 


83 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


fork. We were housed in the Abbot’s residence, a 
three-storied stone affair built around a large court- 
yard. Outside the Abbot’s door a monkey was 
chained, rather an evil-tempered little rascal who de- 
lighted in sinking his sharp teeth into the clothes of 
' an unwary passer-by. 

We had hoped to find some sort of bazaar, but 
there was none, and the only shopping to be had was 
in the private houses. We managed to get some heavy 
boots for Tsao and Chin Tso Lin, but our shikarries’ 
feet proved larger than those of the natives, so it was 
impossible to better their footgear. Had we known 
what was ahead we would have made fuller use of 
the bazaar at Likiang. 

Next morning we borrowed a couple of riding 
mules from the Lamas, so as to allow our ponies a 
full day’s rest, and rode out to meet Rock. The Chi- 
nese type of saddle was in use here, with the heavy 
round-based stirrup in which either heel or toe may 
be placed indiscriminately. Sometimes you see a 
rider with his heel in one stirrup and toe in the other. 

A couple of hours out we met Doctor Rock. I had 
written him four or five months since, from New 
York, telling of our plans and saying that our trails 
might cross, but he had had no mail since early No- 
vember, so our advent came as a complete surprise. 
His first question was as to the result of the Presi- 
dential election; and we brought him up to date, as 


84 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


far as our own ancient information permitted, with 
other world affairs. 

On our return to the Lamasery we found Suydam 
and the balance of the caravan awaiting us. A num- 
ber of our Bhamo mules were too travel-worn to un- 
dertake the cold mountain trek to Tatsienlu, so we 
set about exchanging them for fresh ones. Even- 
tually we engaged a Lama to accompany us with 
fifteen mules. He was a fine strapping fellow of dis- 
tinguished connections, for his father was a bandit 
and his brother, known as Balung Chu Dje, is the 
reincarnation of a fearsome deity. When possessed 
of this god’s spirit, he ties steel sword blades into 
knots, and is a dangerous companion. The Lamas 
whose duty it is at such times to seek prophecies from 
him not uncommonly suffer at his hands. His prede- 
cessor had killed one of them, and our muleteer’s 
brother had not been chary of his buffets. In the 
temples we were shown a sword he had bent while in 
his trances. Of course at these times he is not held 
accountable for his actions. 

It was necessary to wait over a day to complete our 
caravan transactions, so we took the opportunity of 
riding out with Doctor Rock across the valley to see 
the swarms of duck and of crane with which the val- 
ley abounded. We shot one of each with our Spring- 
fields. Suydam was having his first ride on a pinto 
pony which we had exchanged for one of our mules. 


85 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


It was a spirited little animal, but without an ounce 
of viciousness, an ideal mount for steady marching. 
Our Kashmiris adopted certain English words, whose 
orthography they altered to permit easier pronuncia- 
tion. Thus a stew or any dish of varied contents was 
~ known as a “mickus” (mixed). Suydam’s mount 
was speedily christened “the Mickus.” 

Returning to the Lamasery we came upon a group 
of Nashi sorcerer priests at work just outside the 
walls. They had built a small altar in front of which 
was set a pig’s head; prayer flags formed an alley. 
One man held a wooden ruler on which were engraved 
weird figures of man and beast. With the ruler he 
imprinted the figures upon wheaten cakes which he 
tossed to the evil spirits. Four or five men beat great 
and small drums and clashed cymbals. The ceremony 
was in the interest of a sick man. There seemed to be 
a friendly live and let live spirit in existence between 
the Lamas and these irregulars. With a Lama’s help 
I succeeded in purchasing the ruler and one of the 
small drums. 

The Abbot showed us through the various temples. 
None contained any especially fine figures or paint- 
ings, but neither were any of them tawdry. The “dim 
religious light” was impressive, and the wooden floors 
had received a rich tone from the yak butter with 
which they had been polished, although the rancid 
smell was not particularly agreeable. In one of the

SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


lesser temples, where an old Lama sat in prayer, 
there were quantities of ancient weapons; old blunder- 
busses of every vintage all the way down from the 
matchlocks which one man supported and another 
fired—the repercussion knocking down both members 
of the crew. 

We were not long in learning from Doctor Rock 
how disturbed the country was, and what anxiety the 
safeguarding of his collections gave him. Lesser 
raids were of frequent occurrence. Some Lolo ma- 
rauders had recently carried off sixty-five Yungning 
peasants, whom the Lamas were making every effort 
to ransom. More disturbing were the reports of the 
larger bands. Chang Chi Pa, which signifies Chang 
the Stutterer, had been hovering in the offing. A 
former muleteer, he enjoyed a peculiarly sinister rep- 
utation. Rock quoted as common belief, to which he 
gave partial credit, that Chang each day ate a human 
heart. A Lama friend of Rock’s had been hung by 
Chang to a tree, his feet weighted down by stones. 
Although cut down before dead, he had been left a 
hopeless cripple for life. Other tales of the bandit’s 
cruelty were legion. Many had been confirmed by a 
Catholic missionary who had been his prisoner. At 
times he had a horde numbering five thousand fol- 
lowing him. Latest reports proved him to have joined 
the Tibetan raiders of whom we had heard near Li- 
kiang. The original objective of the joint band had 


87 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


been the sack of Yungning, but now their plans had 
altered in favor of first taking Likiang. As Rock’s 
collections were divided between the two places he 
was not in a happy predicament. 

Thanks to Rock’s help our pack-animal difficulties 
~ were satisfactorily solved. We sent back our Bhamo 
muleteers. They had proved both lazy and inefficient, 
Chin Tso Lin, the head man, doing all the work. Him 
we had found extremely conscientious and hardwork- 
ing. He expressed himself as eager to go along with 
us so we placed him in charge of the riding animals, 
with general supervision over the balance of our orig- 
inal pack-mules. When we told him that we would 
take him he was overjoyed and to each in turn he in- 
sisted on making the formal kowtow, touching his 
head to the ground three times in succession. 

On February 5th we left our comfortable quarters 
in the Lamasery. The question of presents to our 
hosts had been one of considerable difficulty. One is 
always uncertain what to bring on an expedition, for 
tastes vary greatly even among similarly situated 
peoples, and what may be a great success in one place 
may be a total failure in another. The most welcome 
gift here we found to be an automatic self-generating 
flashlight. Knives were also invariably appreciated, 
and the same may be said of pocket tool-kits. Our 
McClellan saddles were always greatly admired; and 
we were offered a pony with a Chinese saddle thrown 


88 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


in for one of these saddles. We regretted not having 
brought extra ones for our retainers that might later 
have been used as gifts. 

Doctor Rock rode out the first half day with us. 
He had told us of a mountain two days farther along 
where he knew that sambhur were to be found, and 
sent with us two of his Nashi servants. The ride 
through the valley was lovely but somewhat chilly. It 
seemed incongruous to see water buffalo at this alti- 
tude, close upon ten thousand feet. I had always as- 
sociated them with the paddy fields of the Philippines 
or the blazing heat of the Calcutta streets. The Ab- 
bot was unable to tell us whence they came or who 
first had brought them to the mountain-rimmed plain 
of Yungning. 

The peasants we passed on the trail were almost 
painfully polite, stepping to one side and kowtowing 
low to the ground. It was only within the last few 
days that we had come across the Tibetan method of 
friendly and respectful salute by protruding the 
tongue and scratching the ear. 

At a Lolo village we picked up three native hunt- 
ers, for we wished to hunt separately. Each Lolo 
was armed with an antiquated matchlock. The one 
who went with Ted always lighted his portefeu when 
they were following a fresh trail. These old weapons 
must have been highly inaccurate; they were tightly 
bound about the barrel with rawhide as a precaution 


89 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


against bursting. After leaving the Lolo village we 
crossed into the Muli King’s territory, and when we 
attempted at a hamlet to get additional guides we 
were told that the King allowed none of his subjects 
to hunt. 

We camped about halfway up the mountainside, 
reaching our mountain meadow in time for the after- 
noon hunting. We tossed coins for first choice of di- 
rection, Suydam winning. There was no scope for 
intelligent choosing, but we each set out with a stiff 
climb ahead of us. Immediately upon leaving the 
meadow I was in a magnificent pine forest, with the 
snow lying deep beneath the trees. Mokhta Lone 
and I followed the Lolo up along a ridge where we 
soon came on fresh sambhur tracks. Following these 
we continued our climb for an hour or more, seeing 
nothing but a couple of pheasants that whirred out 
from a covert of scrub oak beneath us. 

Gradually the trees grew smaller and sparser, until 
we came out upon all but barren ridges, separated by 
deep gullies. As we climbed cautiously to a vantage 
point the Lolo, who was ahead, hastily motioned us 
on, and indicated, with hoarse whispers, a serow 
standing among the rocks a hundred and fifty yards 
away across a deep ravine. It was not an easy shot, 
but my third bullet finally settled the business, and 
the serow rolled over and over down into the bed of 
the gorge. It was a smallish male. The serow is a 


90 


SOUTH OF THE CLOUDS 


mountain antelope, with horns much like our Rocky 
Mountain goat. He is not so clumsily built, however, 
and the pelt of this particular species is very dark 
brown. 

Ted’s native hunter had evidently not come to any 
very clear understanding with mine as to a division 
of territory, for we had not got our quarry far on our 
way to camp before we were joined by Ted and his 
men. They had heard the shooting and a few minutes 
later Gaffar Sheikh had sighted two sambhur disap- 
pearing over a ridge far ahead. Ted had also been 
following a fresh trail, but it was impossible to say 
whether the sambhur seen were those that he was fol- 
lowing or the ones which I was tracking. 

We gralloched the serow, and between them the 
Lolos brought it to camp, not, however, without much 
encouragement. It was long after dark when we got 
in, so I put off preparing the skin until the following 
morning. We had determined to stay over and have 
a full day’s hunting, so next morning at half-past five 
we reluctantly crawled out of our bedding rolls, and 
after a hasty breakfast Ted and Suydam set off up 
the mountain. I did not get as early a start, for first 
of all I had to prepare the serow. It was frozen 
stiffer than any Argentine steer in cold storage. My 
duties over I embarked on a long day’s climbing, dur- 
ing which I saw nothing save a mountain partridge, 
which I brought in for the pot. Having shot it with 


91 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


the Springfield its skin was unfortunately past saving. 
Neither Ted nor Suydam had seen anything, and 
though we all had found a good many tracks, some 
of which were recent, we felt that there was not a 
great deal of game in the country, and that we would 
not be justified in delaying longer. Early the fol- 
lowing morning we therefore packed up, and leaving 
Mount Gibboh set out on our march into Muli. 


92 


CHAPTER V 
THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


“And down the loaded air there comes 
The thunder of Tibetan drums 
And droned,—‘Om mane padme hums.’ ” 
—Kipling. 

On the morning after our hunt we broke camp 
early and started on our double march for Muli. A 
stiff climb brought us to the top of the pass of Mount 
Gibboh. The snow lay deep on the ground and the 
wind snarled around us, tearing at the scraggly 
branches of the few pines that struggled up the steep 
slope. As far as the eye could reach lay rough moun- 
tains and hills. So rugged was the country there 
seemed hardly enough level ground to pitch a tent. 
By our trail on the crest was the usual pile of stones 
with bits of rag floating from it,—an altar to the gods 
of the pass. 

All over the world I have seen these little wayside 
shrines, shrines for the consolation of travellers. I re- 
member in France marching by them, neat white 
stones sheltering the image of the Virgin or some 
saint. There are shrines by the bad passes in the 
Alps. In the Himalayas rude piles of rock to which 
each traveller adds a stone are raised on the high 
passes and by the dangerous fords in the rivers. In 


93 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


India I have seen them resplendent in Oriental col- 
ors, perhaps containing a figure of Ganesha, the ele- 
phant-god. Here in China, in the low country, weird 
and savage looking gods in little temples often stand 
by the bridges. It is certain that few need protec- 
tion more than those who go on journeys to far lands, 
even in these days when travel is so much safer than 
in the past. 

After leaving the top of the pass we wound down 
by zigzag trails into the valley. To our left on top of 
a hill we passed a square brownstone tower. The head 
muleteer, who was walking behind me leading my 
horse, pointed to it and said, “Mu Tien Wong.” 
Later I asked Jack Young what this meant. He told 
me it was the name of an esthetic immortal who 
travels through China putting up a house wherever 
the scenery is particularly beautiful. This tower was 
one of his building. 

Now we caught sight of Muli, clinging to the slopes 
of Mount Mitsuga. It was across a gorge not more 
than a couple of miles away as the crow flies, but 
hours by marching. A dark grove of evergreens lay 
above. Just below them was the city, a cluster of 
white buildings ringed by a white wall. 

The Kingdom of Muli is one of those small, only 
partially assimilated principalities that are found in 
Western China. It is only lately, through the pic- 
tures and articles of Doctor Joseph Rock, that it has 


94 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


become known to the outside world. Church and 
state are one, for its temporal king is also the spiritual 
chief. Its people are Buddhists, and he might be 
called the Prince Abbot. Naturally, as the yellow 
lamas are celibate, the crown goes to a brother or 
nephew. The powers of the throne are absolute. The 
present family is of Manchu descent. Of course they 
are in mortal terror of China’s encroachment. Not 
long ago a Chinese general heard of gold in the rivers 
of the Kong-lin tribes that live in the mountains 
southwest of Muli. He sent a messenger proposing 
that he should pay them a visit. Partially inspired 
by Muli influence, the reply of the wild mountain- 
eers was to cut off the miserable ambassador’s ears. 
Doctor Rock found him lying in a swoon on the 
Yungning plain. 

All afternoon we travelled. Two or three times 
we passed caravans. They were led by lamas dressed 
in red Tibetan cloth, with yellow caps to show that 
they belonged to the yellow or reformed sect. Most 
of them wore necklaces decorated with silver, tur- 
quoises, and a heavy ivory ring. On the trail half a 
dozen times we saw branches of green leaves, rhodo- 
dendron or pine, with a rock placed on top of them. 
I thought they were some pateran, a signal from one 
caravan leader to another, and was much surprised 
when I was told they were placed there in honor of 
Chinese New Year, which was but a few days away. 

95 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Just as night fell we reached the city. It was quiet 
and deserted. The white temples were ghost-like in 
the frosty dark. We found our main caravan had ar- 
rived early in the day and was comfortably housed. 
Hardly had we dismounted when the two head lamas 
came to see us. They were fat jovial men. One of 
them wore a little pointed black mustache. With 
them came a messenger from the King of the Mulis, 
who was at Kulu. Doctor Rock’s note had reached 
him and he sent a letter asking us to visit him there. 
The messenger also presented us with dried yak and 
goat meat, and leather bags of grain for horses and 
men. We thanked them with much ceremony and 
presented each with a mechanical electric torch and a 
turquoise ring. A few questions elicited the fact that 
a visit to Kulu would cost us four days. We could 
not spare the time, so we wrote a very polite letter 
explaining our problem. 

Next morning we started the caravan, but waited 
behind ourselves to see something of the city. We got 
the two lamas to act as guides and went to the tem- 
ple of their big Buddha. It is an impressive figure, 
well carved in wood and hung with handsome draper- 
ies. The building which houses it has a stone court- 
yard where justice is administered,—not justice un- 
der the law as we know it at home, but the will of the 
King. Muli is an Oriental despotism and the divine 
right of kings has never been questioned. 


96 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


Standing on either side of the entrance to the court 
were bundles of switches and paddles. The latter are 
about three feet long and an inch thick, with the end 
shaped like the blade of an oar. The wretched culprit 
is stripped and stretched on the ground. The man 
who wields the paddle must break it at the second 
stroke or he in turn is beaten. This does not finish 
the punishment, for another man with another pad- 
dle then takes his place. Often the victim dies or is 
crippled for life. 

In this building we saw, in one tiny room so thick 
with smoke that we choked, a lama placidly reading 
his holy book and sipping buttered tea. On the 
ground floor they showed us a magnificent porcupine, 
property of the King, which rattled its quills like cas- 
tanets when we touched it with our sticks. 

The next temple we visited had a multitude of gods. 
The idols, half-seen in the gloom, suggested all the 
mystery and evil of the back country in the East. 
Some of them were carved to represent grotesque 
fury, their faces twisted, their open mouths fanged 
with huge teeth. Some had the expressionless placid 
look that personifies Nirvana. 

The two lamas enjoyed themselves. They beat the 
great ceremonial drums for us and roared with laugh- 
ter. They turned a big prayer-wheel to show us how 
it was done, chuckling as though they thought it a 
great joke. 

97 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


After bidding them good-by we started with a 
lama guide on our day’s march. We had arranged to 
spend the night at the house of the King’s brother- 
in-law. All day we walked and rode. Toward eve- 
ning we climbed a steep gorge. As we progressed the 
sides drew closer. We thought many hours of travel 
still lay before us, as we could not believe we would 
find the house until we had crossed the pass. Sud- 
denly the trail bent sharply to the right and a broad 
valley opened up before us, nearly on the crest of the 
mountain. In it stood the house of the Prince. It 
was a three-storied building with windows shaped 
like triangles with the apex cut off, and was sur- 
rounded by rough wood palisades. As we approached 
our guide dismounted. A throng of feudal retainers 
trooped out, bowing and sticking out their tongues in 
Tibetan fashion. ‘They took the bridles of our horses 
and led us over a stone-paved road toward the house. 
Great black dogs, as large as calves, were chained to 
a wall. As we passed they foamed, barked and tore 
at their massive fastening in a most disquieting 
fashion. 

At the door our host met us and led us to a little 
room on the third floor, charmingly decorated with 
wall paintings and comfortably heated with a glow- 
ing brazier of coals. There he left us and in a mo- 
ment servants came with trays of food. The dinner 
was excellent,—bowls of steaming rice, boiled bacon, 


98

T. R., JACK YOUNG, THE BROTHER-IN-LAW OF THE KING OF MULI,
