THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


strangely fried eggs, bits of broiled chicken, and but- 
tered tea. The last named I like, especially when hot, 
but it in no way suggests our tea. It is more like a 
soup. 

Of course we had nothing but chop sticks. These 
did very well for Kermit and me, but Suydam has 
never learned to handle them. As a result we got the 
hon’s share of the feast. 

_ As soon as we were through we sent a message to 
our host, Chang Ta Li, asking him to join us at tea 
at 7.30. He arrived at about 6.30, for time is merely 
a matter of personal preference in Muli. He was a 
sturdily built Mongol with a cropped, deeply scarred 
head. He wore a round cap and a long red silk robe. 
He is guardian of the eastern border of Muli, Lord of 
the Marches, or Marquis,—for that is what that title 
originally meant. 

We politely gave him a cigar and some cherry- 
brandy in a tin cup. Then we presented him with a 
mechanical flashlight, a collapsible travelling-chair, 
and a 410 shotgun with some ammunition. After that 
we conversed in the labored fashion necessary when 
interpreters are used. “How many children have 
you?” “Ts the road good from here to the Yalung?”’ 
“What a nice house you have,—is it new?” etc. 
Among other things, with an ulterior motive, we 
greatly admired a painting of the Buddhist Wheel of 
Life that hung on the wall. At one time, when con- 


99 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


versation seemed trickling out, I went to my bedding 
roll and took out photographs of my family. These 
interested him greatly, especially one taken some 
thirteen years ago of Father, my son Teddy then a 
baby, and me. In some vague way he had heard of 
Father. At last with many politenesses we parted for 
the night. It was long past our bedtime, fully half- 
past nine. 

Next morning all was bustle and confusion. The 
dogs barked, the mules brayed, and all the natives 
ran hither and yon shouting. It was Chinese New 
Year. In honor of the day, we divided the King’s 
gifts of dried flesh and grain among the muleteers of 
our pack train. Just as the caravan was getting 
away, Jack Young brought us news that our host 
wanted to see us in his room. When we got there we 
found not only him, but his wife, a handsome woman, 
and their three-year-old son, a cunning, brown, 
chubby little fellow. 

We sat down on a divan covered with rugs and he 
gave us each four oranges. Then a servant brought 
him a flask. He picked up three cups from a table. 
Two were small, one nearly as large as a finger bowl. 
He poured them full of a liquid that looked like 
water. Kermit and I were not deceived. We had 
seen it before. When the tray was passed we firmly 
selected the little cups for ourselves, leaving the large 
one for Suydam. We were right. When our host 

100 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


called for a toast, the liquid that we tasted was so 
strong it nearly burned our lips. 

After an hour’s conversation centring largely on 
rifles, in which he was much interested, we got up 
to go. Then he brought out his presents to us,—the 
Wheel of Life we had admired, and a handsome wave- 
line steel, silver-embossed sword captured by his 
grandfather in some long-forgotten battle with the 
Tibetans. 

He and his three-year-old son came down to the 
gateway to see us off. We were really sorry to leave, 
and regretted that circumstances made it impossible 
to accept his oft-repeated invitation to stay longer 
with him. 

The last we saw of the Lord of the Marches was 
just as we turned toward the pass. He was still 
standing, a tiny red dot, in front of his house. 

Under the guidance of the genial red lama who 
had brought us the message from the King, we set 
out to overtake our caravan. We thought we should 
do so before noon. In this we reckoned without our 
host, for though a jovial individual, our lama was a 
poor guide and took the wrong trail. It was only by 
chance, and the eagle eyes of a dweller in a small 
stone cottage, that we were set right. He told us he 
had seen our pack train on the crest of a neighboring 
mountain. We made almost an about-face, and by a 
very bad path climbed down into a valley and up an- 

101 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


other mountain. Finally just at nightfall we found 
our men camping in a pretty little hollow. The only 
drawback to the site was that it was dry. Its local 
name told the story, “One cupful of water.” 

The evening was brisk but not toocold. Our mule- 
teers as usual built themselves low walls with the 
packs. These serve to break the force of the wind, 
which otherwise would whirl away their fire. Within 
these half-shelters they gossip and cook their tsamba 
and buttered tea. Each particular group has a fire 
and shelter of its own. When they first get to a camp 
they turn their animals loose to graze. Later they 
gather them for a feed of grain, generally Indian 
corn. This is done by calling them much the way a 
farmer’s wife calls the chickens. The mules gather 
from all sides, dim shapes in the flickering firelight. 
Sometimes if the grazing has been good they are then 
tied up for the night. A braided horse-hair picket-line 
is used, to which a forefoot of each animal is fastened. 

That night, as there was plenty of wood and little 
wind, blazing fires were started by all. The red light 
climbed until it lost itself among the tall trunks of the 
trees. The dried meat we had given the men was 
cooked, and every few minutes one of them would 
start a shrill Asiatic song with its monotonous ca- 
dences. It was very picturesque, but after we were 
tucked in our bedding rolls we would have gladly dis- 
pensed with the singing. 


102 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


As we were getting up next morning a well-fed 
man in yellow rode into camp. He was the King’s 
secretary and came with another letter asking us to 
change our plans and come to Kulu. He also brought 
a present for us, a powerful brown riding horse, 
looking in its winter coat rather like a moth-eaten 
Teddy bear. We asked our visitor to breakfast and 
politely explained that we did not have the time 
to turn aside, much though we should have liked to 
do so. We gave him the usual mechanical electric 
torch and a small pocket tool-kit and he went back 
quite happy to the King. 

All day we travelled at high altitudes. The drifted 
snow lay thick in the valleys and the strength of the 
lead mules was taxed breaking trail through it. The 
hilltops were barren and wind-swept. Once we passed 
through a rhododendron forest where the tracks of 
wild animals criss-crossed the snow, but the wind had 
blurred them so much it was impossible to read them 
with accuracy. 

Toward evening we picked a camp site that suited 
no one but our Tibetans. It was a treeless gut, fif- 
teen thousand feet high, between two bare peaks. It 
was covered with snow, save where the wind sweep- 
ing through it had blown bare spots. ‘The moment 
the sun sank it became very cold. We huddled around 
the fires that eddied and flared in the wind, showering 
us with sparks. One side would be cooked and the 


103 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


other frozen. Finally we crawled into our bedding 
rolls and lay there shivering. Not so the Tibetans. 
They were as merry as grigs. They were only reason- 
ably well clothed and have no blankets, but cold 
meant nothing to them. 

Next morning we reluctantly crawled out of our 
eight mules and Suydam’s horse. Leaving five Ti- 
ing. We had spent a restless night because of the 
cold and altitude, for whenever we got warm enough 
to drop to sleep we woke choking for lack of oxygen. 
Our Kashmiris had suffered from the same reason. In 
the shadowy half-light the muleteers came to us. A 
third of the mules had strayed and two of the riding 
horses, Suydam’s and Jack Young’s. Personally I 
did not blame them. Lying in blankets or huddling 
by a fire was bad enough. Standing unsheltered in 
the wind and driving snow must have been unbear- 
able, even for a salted mountain mule. 

We organized search parties and recovered all but 
eight mules and Suydam’s horse. Leaving five Ti- 
betans behind we piled our baggage on the animals 
we still had. Over the tops of the mountains we 
wound our way. The wind blew with such force it 
was possible to lean against it. The snow stung our 
faces like birdshot. Dodging the deepest drifts we 
broke our way. Every now and then a mule would 
fall in a drift or on glazed ice. All hands worked un- 
ceasingly, pounding down snow to make a trail, lift- 


104 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


ing the packs off the animals which went down, driv- 
ing the strayed back into line. A mule will sometimes 
seem to try deliberately to kill itself by leaving the 
trail near a precipice. Not the least useful in all this 
was the large black Tibetan dog belonging to one of 
the muleteers. I had always thought of these cara- 
van dogs as valuable mainly for guarding the goods 
from thieves. This one, however, did far more. On 
the march he acted as a sheep-dog. If a mule lagged 
he snapped at its heels. If it strayed he followed it 
and drove it back. Bob, Son of Battle, never han- 
dled a flock with more skill. 

Just at dark we made camp at a slightly lower level. 
We were in a rhododendron forest that had been 
killed by blight. Ash-white gnarled trunks and limbs 
were moonwashed and goblin-like against the back- 
ground of snow. It was still very cold, but the trees 
broke the wind and it had stopped snowing. Toward 
ten o’clock there came the sound of singing, and after 
a few minutes in came the men we had left behind to 
find the remaining mules and Suydam’s horse. From 
early morning to late night they had tramped over 
that bleak snow-swept waste, and they came in sing- 
ing! 

Suydam was in his bed when they arrived, but he 
crawled out to see his pony. He had been very down- 
cast over its loss. It was a ridiculous little animal, 
piebald, with a mane as stiff as a scrubbing brush, and 


105 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


a tail that nearly trailed on the ground. It was so 
small it seemed almost able to run along under the 
larger horses like a coach-dog beneath a coach. The 
pony was named “‘Mickus” because of its color. Our 
Kashmiris when they gave us stew called it “mickus,” 
under the impression they were using the English 
word “mixed.” 

Getting out of bed for Suydam did not mean walk- 
ing around in night-clothes. Night-clothes in cli- 
mates like this are the same as day-clothes. All Ker- 
mit and I do is to take off our boots and put on an- 
other pair of socks. 

Next morning when we woke we saw for the first 
time the splendor of the view. In the clear light of 
the early sun a great mountain lay before us. Its cap 
of snow was circled by a dense evergreen forest. At 
the eastern end this was broken by a huge dome-like 
monolith of gray rock to which the snow clung in oc- 
casional white patches. 

We gathered our head men together and told them 
we must make a particularly good march to make up 
the time we had lost. Outside of our Kashmiris, the 
three most interesting characters in our train were 
Luzon, the Tibetan who handled our hired mules, 
Chin Tzo Lin, the Yunnanese who took charge of 
the animals we brought from Bhamo, and Hsuen, the 
Tibetan who interpreted and did odd jobs of every 
sort. 


106 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


Luzon was a fine figure of a man. Even though 
he wore an odd jumble of skins and rags, the grace 
of his proportions was unconcealed. He was never 
tired. All day he strode ahead of the caravan pick- 
ing the way, in his hands a rosary whose beads he 
was constantly telling. At his heels trotted his dog, 
a short-legged, long-bodied animal with black and 
white hair singed off one shoulder from lying too 
close to the fire. In the evening he superintended 
every detail of the care of his animals. In the morn- 
ing he was first up and about. His face was battered 
and twisted by weather until it looked like a black- 
thorn. He was always happy and I rarely saw him 
without a smile on his face. 

Chin was entirely different. He was a little fel- 
low with a serious look on his brown face. Some- 
times I wondered that a frame so slight could stand 
the work he did, which was prodigious. Coming from 
the comparatively warm climate of Yunnan, he still 
insisted on wearing his light blue cotton clothes and 
cartwheel hat, though we had given him more suitable 
clothes. As a compromise he wrapped his shoulders 
and head in a bright red blanket. At first when he 
had charge of the muleteers for our thirty animals he 
was lost, for he was no executive. He let his men 
malinger while he vainly tried to do the work of all. 
When time and hardship had thinned the ranks of our 
animals to ten, and the worthless drivers were gone, 


107 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


he acted as general veterinary and groom of the rid- 
ing horses. To him fell the task of shepherding the 
rear of the caravan. It was a common sight to see 
him trudging in herding a weak mule a couple of 
hours after the rest had reached camp. Practically 
all natives steal. I do not think Chin did. Though 
we had not two words in common we were all fond of 
him. 

Hsuen was a man of varied attainments. The mis- 
sionaries told us he was an evangelist, but I gravely 
doubt it. When we got near Muli he asked us to get 
him a red Jama’s robe to wear, which shows at least 
a wide religious tolerance. His interpreting was oc- 
casionally marred by the fact that we sometimes could 
not make him understand English. One day he asked 
to borrow the 410 shotgun, “To shoot birds on trail.” 
We lent it to him and gave him some cartridges. 
Our confidence in him as a sportsman was rather 
shaken when he came to Kermit with the gun, which 
is of the ordinary double-barrel pattern, and asked 
how many cartridges he could put in. An hour or so 
later he brought gun and shells back, saying, “Think 
not shoot birds after all.’ Thus finished the mighty 
hunter. 

That day we followed the beds of the streams. 
They were choked with snow, with not a sign of water 
save an occasional choked gurgle beneath the ice. At 
noon we reached a village,—of three huts. Here 


108 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


what is called a village may have only ten people. 
The huts were built of rough logs, the roofs of hand- 
hewn board held down by rocks. On top of each roof- 
tree was a rod with a bit of rag attached to it and a 
trident at its peak. The trident is the symbol of the 
Trinity of God. The flag says a prayer every time 
the wind blows. It is known as Jong Tsen. 

In Asia the idea seems to be to do your worshipping 
with as little trouble as possible. It is apparently the 
number of prayers offered that counts, not the attitude 
of mind of the supplicant. Quantity, not quality, is the 
aim. Hundreds of Buddhist texts written on scraps 
of paper are stuffed into metal cylinders. Sometimes 
these containers are fitted with handles so that a de- 
votee may whirl them while walking. Sometimes the 
cylinders are as large as a hogshead and fixed on up- 
right axles in some building. These are turned by 
hand like a primitive grist mill. Each turn automati- 
cally offers the prayers for the benefit of the turner, 
no matter what iniquity he may be planning. We saw 
prayer-wheels attached to water wheels to save even 
the slight labor of turning them by hand. Texts are 
carved on slabs of stone which are piled in low walls, 
called manes. The traveller passing by on their left 
receives their benediction even if he be a bandit riding 
on a raid. Besides these there are the flags I have 
mentioned, whose efficiency merely depends on a windy 
day. 

109 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


It was still very cold, so we went into one of the 
houses. It consisted of one room with an open fire 
in the centre on a hearth surrounded by three stones 
set on end. Pots and pans were standing on and 
near it. Around it were squatted the owners and some 
of our escort eating tsamba and drinking buttered 
tea. The smoke was suffocating, the houses in Muli 
have no chimneys, and the inside of the room was 
black with the soot of years. The only attempt at 
decoration was a series of rough white blotches on 
the walls. There was a pile of blankets and skins in 
one corner. Suydam sat down on the edge of it and 
started to light his pipe. At once a man called to 
him. Suydam, believing it was merely an invitation 
to make himself more comfortable, said, “Yes, yes, 
I'll sit in the centre as soon as this pipe is lit.” A 
woman rushed from the other side of the fire and a 
half-suffocated baby was rescued from imminent dan- 
ger of being crushed to death under him. 

In the afternoon we wound our way along a ridge. 
Toward evening we passed through a forest of spruce. 
The great trunks towered skyward like the columns 
at Karnak. Wisps of pale green moss drooped from 
branch and twig. The rotting trunks of fallen trees 
lay on either side. There was an atmosphere of pri- 
meval melancholy and loneliness. 

Just at dark, after leaving the white monastery of 
Watto to our right, we reached the town of Reddu, 

110 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


where we spent the first comfortably warm night we 
had known since we entered the Muli country. We 
took off our clothes and slept well, outside of a diver- 
sion caused by a mule knocking down our tent. 

Next day we dropped five thousand feet in two 
hours’ march and found ourselves in the valley of the 
Yalung. The river at this season is crossed by a 
ferry. In the wet season a rope bridge is used, for 
then it is a torrent in which no boat could live. At the 
ferry we found a caravan of Tibetans with furs. 
They came from the country northwest of Atunza. 
On the sand in the warm river bottom they were tak- 
ing the only type of bath a Tibetan knows,—a sun 
bath. Some of them had let down their hair, which 
stood out from their heads in wiry black mops. Others 
had stripped to the waist and were trying to kill their 
vermin. They had their packs built up in the usual 
wall-like enclosures. Kermit and I sat down with 
some of them there. We showed them our rifles, 
which interested them very much, and bought a couple 
of their tsamba bowls. I also purchased a small bar- 
rel-like bottle hooped with silver for about $1.20. 
With great ceremony they made signs that I might 
also keep its contents of vile and rasping native spirits. 

The ferry boats that took us over the river were 
each made of two medium sized logs hollowed and 
fastened together. The baggage was piled in the mid- 
dle and the passengers sat astride it, one foot in each 


111 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


log. The paddles the boatmen used were merely 
straight staves of wood. I doubt if there was a man 
there save Kermit, Suydam and me who could swim, 
yet no one seemed in the least afraid. 

Of course the animals could not go in any such 
frail craft. They were herded into the river with 
blows and yells and made to swim. After they were 
in the water they were directed by the drivers on one 
shore throwing stones at them, while those on the 
other called to them. Some of the mules swam with 
their tails partly out of the water crooked in a bow, 
which made them look as if they had a handle behind. 
When we got over the three of us took the first bath we 
had had since I began this chapter. Though the air 
was warm the water was numbingly cold and it re- 
quired more character than getting out of bed on a 
winter morning. 

At the ferry we noticed platforms of loose rock 
decorated with green branches. Thinking it was an 
offering to some local river god I asked particulars, 
and was told they were there in honor of the lama 
who was guiding us. Metilong, the town we were ap- 
proaching, was where he was born. We stopped at his 
family house. He had not been there for five years, 
during which time his father had died without his 
knowledge. 

When we got there and turned in the gate, a num- 
ber of little naked children scattered like a flock of

quail. Later they became quite friendly and crowded 
around us with wide solemn eyes. There were a num- 
ber of small goats in the courtyard. A woman gave 
them water by squirting it from her mouth into theirs. 
The people seemed a miserable lot, poor both in goods 
and physique. The women wore a few ornaments. 
Principal among: these were big silver or brass ear- 
rings which dragged the lobes of their ears an inch or 
two out of shape. 

Next day after a short march we camped under 
two beautiful cedar trees on a small upland meadow. 
They were quite by themselves. We saw hardly half 
a dozen in the valley. They were not like our east- 
ern cedars but rather like those Hiram must have 
rafted down from Lebanon for Solomon’s Temple. 

Next day we started for an unnamed pass on an 
unnamed mountain. In a few hours, looking back, 
I saw Reddu not more than two miles away as the 
crow flies, but two days’ journey for us up and down 
the sides of the canyon. For seven hours we climbed 
through pine woods, through live-oak forests, through 
scrub, through dried grass and finally out on the slide- 
rock and snow. When we reached the pass, over 17,- 
000 feet in altitude, it was afternoon. Luzon as usual 
had led the way, fumbling his beads and mumbling 
a prayer. Two of the men, Hsuen and Chow, had 
quite bad attacks of mountain sickness. 

At the crest was the usual pile of stones. One of 


113 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


our muleteers thrust a stick in it, and another pulled 
from the folds of his jacket a red rag which he fast- 
ened there. It was, of course, some sort of magic, 
but not such as required reverence, for the man 
laughed and joked while doing it. 

That evening after dark we reached the tiny mon- 
astery of Bedung, which is huddled in a lonely corner 
of these remote mountains. We were the first white 
men that had ever been there. The clergy were but 
three lamas. Passing the small gate in the stone wall 
that enclosed the lamassary, we saw through the black 
a faint glow from one dimly outlined building. We 
walked in and found it to be the temple. It was lt 
by the pale flame of candles. At the rear were the 
gods in a solemn row, their brilliantly colored faces 
and garments half concealed, half revealed. The walls 
and pillars were decorated with mythological scenes, 
dragons, strange animals, warriors on _ horseback. 
From the rafters hung silk banners. On the altars 
and arranged on two stands, one like a pyramid, the 
other like a big candelabrum, were myriads of small 
figures made of butter, for it was the butter festival. 

At the right, seated cross-legged in a row before a 
low board table, were the three lamas. The two at 
the right and left were middle-aged men, plump and 
well-fed, with sparse black beards. In the centre sat 
a very much older man, evidently the abbot. His 
long red robes muffied his figure. On his head was

ITOW LV SHLYUSVNVT AHL JO ANO 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


a yellow peaked cap with strange triangular flaps,— 
but dwarfing the mystery of the temple, the grotesque 
colored gods and his robes, was his face. It was old 
and smooth of hair. He had a hawk nose. His eyes 
were deep sunk. Every wrinkle that showed shadowy 
in the soft light told of asceticism and long knowledge 
of power. 

The three of them were conducting a service. We 
sat down opposite and watched. They intoned from 
their scriptures in a melodious monotone. The two 
younger read from holy books propped in front of 
them. The older man in the centre knew the verses 
by heart and chanted without the aid of the text. 
At intervals they would stop and blow on handsome 
bronze-mounted conches or engraved trumpets. Then 
they would clash their cymbals, and punctuate their 
singing with the sonorous boom of two great Tibetan 
drums that hung in front of them. 

From time to time an acolyte would take two cop- 
per ladles full of wine from an earthen bowl, and 
walking to the door, throw them into the courtyard, 
crying meanwhile with the quavering shriek of a ban- 
shee. These were for the dead who had no relatives 
to care for their memory. Other acolytes fed the 
singers with small bowls of tsamba and cups of but- 
tered tea, which they consumed without interrupting 
their devotions. In most of the services we had seen 
before there was a touch of levity, a suggestion that 

115 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


worship was with the lips, not the mind. Here there 
was no hint of anything but solemn reverence. 

After a half-hour we left them and went to the 
other side of the court where our servants had pre- 
pared supper. When we were through, we returned 
to the temple with three brown-colored slouch hats 
with colored hat-bands. These we presented to the 
lamas in a pause in the service. 

Next morning early they returned the compliment 
by coming to our room. We were sleeping in the 
larder among sides of dried meat and bags of rice. 
They presented us with yak butter and eggs. We 
saw that our gifts were appreciated, for each of them 
was wearing a hat. 

We went with them to the temple. There we first 
presented them with a knife each, and then very tact- 
fully opened negotiations through our lama guide 
for a conch, a bronze trumpet, and two old wood- 
bound Buddhist Bibles with beautifully written texts 
on loose leaves, rich brown from generations of fin- 
gers greasy with buttered tea. After much talk in 
which we took no part, we were told that these articles 
were very holy, and that therefore they were very ex- 
pensive. That the price was $30 Chinese,—$9 in our 
money,—for them all. Of course we closed. With 
our treasures in our hands we bade our friends good- 
by. In their new hats they stood in a row on the 
temple steps, and smiled best wishes for our journey.

AB, THE WOODCHOPPER 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


That day we passed a small river and left the King- 
dom of Muli with its strange mixture of barbarous 


cruelty and hospitality behind us, probably forever. - 


Just before crossing its border we came on a solitary 
wood-chopper. Never in my life have I seen such a 
simian type. His figure was squat, with promise of 
enormous strength. His arms hung from his shoul- 
ders nearly to his knees. In his face there was hardly 
a suggestion of what we call humanity, only a sullen 
savagery. Kermit got off to take a picture of him. 
With a quick turn of his head he glanced at us, al- 
most snarling with the furtive ferocity of a wild ani- 
mal. Had we been anthropologists in search of the 
ape man we could have called our quest ended. 

Next morning we travelled up the river valley. It 
was sparsely cultivated. On either side were moun- 
tains belted with woods, above which grassy slopes led 
to slide-rock and snow. Here we got our first news 
of some of the game we were seeking. A native whom 
we questioned said he believed there were yellow wild 
cows in the mountains not far away. We realized he 
must be speaking of takin. 

On the following day we crossed a 16,000-foot 
pass and dropped down into a valley. For the first 
time we saw clusters of villages. They were very 
small, not more than half a dozen houses each, but 
compact and well built of gray stone. Each village 
had one or more big stone towers. Some of these 


117 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


were shaped like six-pointed stars, others were square. 
They were mostly in ruins. The few still standing in- 
tact often had a cant like the leaning tower of Pisa, 
and gave a very unsafe appearance to the houses 
clustered beneath. Questioning the natives as to their 
use or origin brought little results. No one knew when 
they had been built. They had just “always been 
there.” Some said they had a religious purpose, 
’ others that they were designed for defense. Of one 
thing I am sure, the tribesmen who live in the valley 
now could not have raised them. 

After camping, encouraged by the news of game 
we had received, we sent out to the near-by towns to 
find a hunter. Soon three turned up. Two were 
partly Chinese, one a pure-bred Lolo from the back 
country. They squatted around the fire and we got 
out the portfolio of pictures. One by one they ex- 
amined them. Generally in a second they would iden- 
tify the animal and give us the local name, which we 
jotted down. When we came to the panda none had 
seen it. After studying it awhile they said there were 
bears near by (the panda looks like a bear), but that 
they were all either black or brown, and moreover 
were “sleeping” now. We had much better luck when 
we showed them the takin. At once they said, “Yea 
gnu! Yea gnu!” (Wild cow.) They told us there 
were very few near by but that seven days’ journey 
away there was a mountain named Yung Shuei Tung, 

118 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


“Salt-water Well,” where there were many. Some- 
how game is always over the next divide. They said 
they were very fierce and often charged hunters. The 
trail to the mountain was so bad that no pack-animals 
could use it. The Lolo said he would go with us if 
we would promise to protect him from the Lolos near 
the mountain, who belonged to a hostile tribe. 

Next morning after the shortest of marches we 
reached Chiulung, the seat of the first Chinese mag- 
istrate. It had seemed to us, when on trail, that a 
town with such a functionary must be quite a place. 
As a matter of fact it was nothing but a few ram- 
shackle buildings, surrounded by a rectangular stone 
wall with towers at the four corners. 

The village might be poor, but our reception made 
up for it. The Chinese official came out in person 
to welcome us. He was an active, bright little fellow 
with no chin at all. He told us that he had received 
word from the Central Government that he might 
expect us, and led us to a reception room, a dingy, 
cheerless small tank on the second floor. There he 
plied us with weak tea, and we plied him with nu- 
merous questions. 

He was an extremely well-informed man, having 
been an inspector in Szechuan and kept his eyes open 
while he was travelling. We asked him about dis- 
tances and got a wealth of information. Then we 
turned to the subject of game. I got out my folio 

119 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


of pictures and he sent for a native hunter. For an 
hour we went over them,—one animal at a time. It 
developed that there were sambhur, serow, ghoral and 
musk deer in the wooded valleys near by, burrhel one 
or two days’ journey away, and takin at the place of 
which the Lolo had told us. The let was that neither 
magistrate nor hunter had even heard of the giant 
panda. That decided us. We could not stop and go 
to the salt-well mountain. We must hold it in reserve 
for our return trip. Slight as our chance might be, 
we felt that we must make every effort to get to a 
country where the panda existed. All news we had, 
though of a very inconclusive type, pointed to the 
Muping district and the upper Min River. 

We compromised by deciding to send the caravan 
half a day’s march ahead while we hunted in the near- 
by valleys. The magistrate at once offered to help. 
Here it is customary to use dogs to start the forest 
game. He ordered the three best native hunters to 
get everything ready for the next morning. 

Meanwhile the three of us walked to a village a 
short distance away, where Suydam took pictures 
and Kermit and I poked about. Of course the whole 
population turned out to see us. I suppose they had 
never seen a white man before. The crowd numbered 
about thirty. They were very evidently not racially 
Chinese. Their complexions were dark brown, not 
yellow, and their features aquiline. They were crusted

AULNNOO ITAW AHL NI ONITIAMG ANOLS V

with dirt and ragged. Withal they were a cheery 
lot. They had the usual Tibetan form of salute we 
had noticed ever since Yungning—sticking out the 
tongue. The last time we had seen it was in Ladak, 
nearly four years ago and thousands of miles away. 
It is most disconcerting to see a man smiling in the 
friendliest fashion and at the same time sticking out 
a very large tongue. Among the people was one in 
particular who attracted our attention, an old, old 
woman, wrinkled and wizened almost beyond human 
semblance, who wandered around whirling a prayer 
wheel. 

In addition to the usual ruined tower there were 
three temples, square, solidly built of gray stone, 
three stories high. The first floors were dark and 
filled with small lumps of dried clay, modelled like 
squat vases but solid. These were the cremated dead 
of the village and explained why we saw none of the 
stone graves that had covered the hillsides in Yun- 
nan. Indeed, these temples were in a literal sense 
graveyards. From this dark lower room a notched 
beam led to the next story. There in each case we 
found the temple proper. Images of the gods were 
on an altar at one end. In only one did we find a 
statue of Buddha, or Song Ja, as they call him. 

All three temples were dirty and shabby. Some 
of the statues were scattered indiscriminately over 
the floor. Each had, however, a number of Bud- 


121 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


dhist holy books on a shelf at one side. They were 
big volumes, handsomely bound in stained wood and 
beautifully written in silver and gold wash. Every- 
thing was so ill-kept that Kermit and I thought 
we might be able to buy a couple of these voiumes. 
Through our lama we offered the man we were told 
had them in charge $50 Chinese. He readily agreed 
and we had them carried to our camp. 

That evening we dined with the magistrate. He 
had lunched with us. Dinner was served in a dark 
little upper room. His small son, aged eight, was 
one of the party and watched us with saucer-like eyes. 
The meal was excellent, but for some reason there 
was no rice and we missed it. Toward the end he pre- 
sented us with some dried calf’s legs, and Jack Young 
with a dried Tibetan pheasant, delicacies for use on 
the trail. 

Next morning the hunters came for us. The head 
man was a Lolo. He wore the red turban which the 
tribesmen here use to distinguish them from the Chi- 
nese. His gun was as primitive as his native hills. 
Its barrel was a heavy tube of steel without rifling or 
sights. The butt was simply a curved piece of wood 
with no flat surface to rest against the shoulder. Of 
course it was a muzzle-loader. It antedated even 
flint-locks, for it was fired by a fuse applied to a 
touch-hole. Strung on a cord around his neck were 
a variety of appliances,—small horns for powder, a 


122 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


pouch for bullets, and a flint and steel to light the 
fuse. 

On our way to hunt we went through the village 
where we had bought the Buddhist books. There we 
found every one much excited. It seemed that the 
man with whom we had dealt did not own them. They 
belonged to the whole village. The previous evening, 
when the people found what had happened, they held 
a meeting and told the guilty man to return us the 
money and bring back the books, or leave the village 
forever. We tried our best then to buy them from 
the village, without success. Though they were prob- 
ably rarely opened and certainly not really rever- 
enced, they were regarded as a fetish. Of course, 
under the circumstances there was nothing to do but 
give them back, which we did with much regret. 

All this took time and it was ten o’clock before we 
got started again. We were unable to speak any lan- 
guage which our hunters could understand, but with 
the aid of our Kashmiris, who are experts at sign 
language, we finally gathered that the dogs had gone 
ahead and were ranging the upper slopes of the moun- 
tain. We were taken to a central point from where 
we could go in any direction. There we sat down to 
wait. Time passed and nothing seemed to be hap- 
pening. We sat in the sun and fidgeted. At last we 
turned to our books. We always carry them when 
hunting. Kermit read Burton’s “Pilgrimage to 


123 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Mecca’; I read Dickens’ “Great Expectations.’ At 
twelve we had abandoned all hope of sport and were 
blaming ourselves for having wasted a day. 

Suddenly from nowhere two natives appeared. 
One of them seized my horse and led it to me. We re- 
alized game was afoot and, though we could not tell 
what or where, mounted and followed them. ‘Down 
the steep side of the hill we scrambled at breakneck 
speed, over a river and up a valley. Guffer Sheikh, 
my Kashmiri shikari, had in some miraculous way 
divined where we were going and ran ahead shouting 
“Jaldi, Sahib!’ After ten minutes fast going we 
came to a spot on the hillside where two villagers were 
standing pointing to the opposite side of the ravine. 
There in the scrub some two hundred or more yards 
away I made out a large animal, evidently a sambhur, 
trotting slowly uphill. At the moment I was some 
yards ahead of the rest. I jumped off my horse at 
once and wrenched my rifle from its sling. Neither 
Guffer Sheikh nor I could tell whether it was a buck 
or doe, as it was too far away to see horns with the 
naked eye and there was no time to use field glasses. 
That made but little difference, for though I wanted 
a stag, either would be welcome to the Museum. 

My first shot was too high, as a puff of dust told 
us. Holding lower I hit it with my second. The 
sambhur fell, but was up in an instant and trotted 
into a clump of trees. In the next three shots I reg- 


124 


THE KINGDOM OF MULI 


istered another hit. Then a dog appeared from the 
scrub and began to chivy the wounded animal. I ran 
into the ravine with the two shikaris to get closer and 
finish it. Half-way down the slope I got a clear view 
and did so with one shot. Then the three of us scram- 
bled to the stream bed. Like many a clear mountain 
brook, its depth was deceptive and we had to strip to 
the waist before we could wade it. The water was 
snow-cold and when I got out on the far side my legs 
looked as if they had been parboiled. 

A short climb brought us to where the deer was 
lying. It was a doe. A number of the local hunters 
were already there. They were greatly excited. We 
started skinning it at once and cut off some chunks 
of meat for the Lolos. They immediately lit a fire 
and after scorching, rather than broiling, the slices, 
ate them. 

By this time some of the dogs had gathered. They 
were not the big, long-haired, powerful animals we 
had seen with the caravans, or guarding the houses, 
but little, lean, black pariah-like creatures. They 
were extraordinarily efficient nevertheless. As the 
skinning proceeded the hunters scooped up blood 
with their hands and gave it to the dogs to drink. I 
asked why, and was told it made them fiercer. An- 
other incident I noticed was the Lolos taking the tear 
ducts for medicine, just as the Kirghiz had done in 
the Tian Shan mountains thousands of miles away. 


125 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


As soon as the skin was off we loaded it and some 
of the meat on a shaggy little mountain pony. Giv- 
ing the rest of the carcass to the hunters we started at 
once after our caravan. On the trail we met the mag- 
istrate and his little son waiting to say good-by. We 
had no interpreter, but bows and smiles answered the 
purpose. A short march brought us to our camp in an 
attractive valley sheltered by a splendid grove of 
spruce. The tents had been pitched, the fires were 
burning brightly, hot tea was waiting. 

Our old white-bearded Kashmiri cook held my 
horse as I dismounted. “Salaam Sahib,—Sab chiz 
thikhai.” Even if my kill was only a female sambhur 
it was a specimen the Museum wished, and the fresh 
meat would be welcome to all.

CHAPTER VI 
ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


THERE were many signs to tell us that we had left 
Muli and were back in territory administered by the 
Chinese. In every hamlet there were a number of 
Chinese-appearing inhabitants and the country seemed 
deforested by man, in addition to the devastation 
caused by fire and blight. 

We had secured the best maps obtainable, but we 
found none to be of much use. Occasionally we could 
identify a name recorded, but even then it was usually 
incorrectly located. Most names meant absolutely 
nothing to the natives. Probably some of them signi- 
fied “I don’t know” or “What did you say?” in the 
native dialect, for it is frequent that an unwary voy- 
ager records as a place name some such phrase with 
which his query has been met. The whole region 
would prove a very rich field for an enterprising car- 
tographer. 

At Chuilung we had to replace the few mules 
which we had hired at Metilong, and were obliged 
to make the best of the relay system. It was exceed- 
ingly inconvenient, for the changes usually occurred 
at irregular intervals during the course of our day’s 
march. The transport varied in nature, pony or mule 


or yak. 
127 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


There were a number of dogs accompanying our 
Likiang and Yungning caravans. They were a cheer- 
ful, amusing lot, and on the most friendly and in- 
timate terms with their owners. It was an agreeable 
change from Mohammedan and Hindu countries, 
where contact between man and beast is usually any- 
thing but pleasant. All but one of the dogs played 
together continually during the march. The misan- 
thropic member of the tribe was a smallish whitey 
brown nondescript belonging to Luzon. He had 
started from Likiang with a limp which he never 
shook off, but it seemed not to interfere with his prog- 
ress. Lying near the camp-fire he caught fire from 
a spark, which we vainly hoped might have the effect 
of blistering to his lameness. Although most affec- 
tionate to his master he resented any familiarity on 
the part of his fellow canines. 

The dogs in the villages were anything but 
friendly, sometimes they were small, at other times 
huge, but they were always fierce. In isolated houses 
a great brute was usually chained in a dark, gloomy 
room underneath the main floor. As far as we could 
learn they were always chained, and this form of ex- 
istence may have accounted for their evil dispositions. 
Once when we were filing past a forbidding-looking 
stone building an unusually savage-looking black 
brute of Homeric proportions burst forth and bore 
down upon the caravan. Our dogs took refuge among 

128 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


the mules, and the muleteers picked up stones. When 
our adversary, looking, if possible, more forbidding 
as he drew near, came within range one of the men 
let fly, grazing the animal. To our amazement the big 
dog squatted down uttering the most ridiculous and 
pathetic squeaks and moans in such a minor and 
plaintive key that it seemed impossible they could be 
issuing from his huge frame. 

We were drawing close to Tatsienlu, passing 
through what we had hoped would prove a good hunt- 
ing country. For two days we marched up a long 
valley, on either side of which towered great barren 
mountains, the whole reminding us of the Turkestan 
Pamirs. Enquiries failed to bring forth reports of 
any game other than burrhel—the so-called blue 
sheep, a few horns of which we saw in the villages. 
The inhabitants possess herds of yaks, great, power- 
ful beasts, but however handsome, one would not be 
inclined to follow Hilaire Belloc’s advice and com- 
mend them “as a friend for the children.” In this 
valley for the only time we saw native tents; they 
were not the yourts of Central Asia, but more closely 
resembled the black tents of the Arabs. 

Early one morning we got our first glimpse of 
mysterious Mount Koonka, rising high in white ma- 
jesty. At Yungning, Rock had told us of his am- 
bition to put in three or four months studying its 
flora, and once more we regretted the lack of time 


129 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


that made it impossible for us to make a reconnais- 
sance of its fauna. The altitude of this mighty 
peak is unknown, but there are those who claim 
that it rises more than thirty thousand feet and is 
the highest in the world. A geologist from Chengtu 
made a special expedition to establish Koonka’s 
height, but after he had taken his observations he re- 
fused for some entirely unaccountable reason to di- 
vulge them. When any one questioned him he merely 
imitated the beaver and “looked unaccountably shy.” 
Presumably some slip had occurred in his calculations 
and he was unwilling to dwell upon the error. 

It was a bitterly cold country and only the stout- 
est of poshteens could withstand the wind that came 
sweeping down from the barren snow-covered moun- 
tains. 'The pass that led into the next valley was close 
to seventeen thousand feet high, and we dropped 
down three thousand feet to where the caravan en- 
camped near a magnificent frozen waterfall. 

In order to reach Tatsienlu next day Luzon told 
us that a night march would be necessary. He an- 
nounced himself prepared to undertake it with his 
ten mules, and Aju likewise agreed to go on; so leay- 
ing the balance behind with Young, the three of us 
started out again at half-past eight. We should have 
had the benefit of a full moon, but the mist lay heavy, 
and before we had gone far the snow, which had fallen 
intermittently all afternoon, threatened to assume the 


130 


VUNOOM “LIN 


LNVLSISSV §.NOZNT 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


proportions of a blizzard. The going was abominable. 
In daylight and in fair weather it would not have 
been easy to pick one’s way among the jagged boul- 
ders forming the trail, but in the dark with snow and 
ice coating the rocks it seemed impossible for the 
mules to get through whole in limb. 

Luzon had two assistants; one was a smiling fine- 
featured young Tibetan to whom he paid a yearly 
salary the equivalent of less than three dollars, with 
clothing and food included. On one of his hands he 
had a sixth finger. The other man was older and 
had lost the greater portion of his teeth, a severe 
misfortune to a Tibetan muleteer who depends upon 
them for tightening the rawhide thongs with which 
the packs are secured. To see them run up beside an 
animal and without checking him grab the thong 
with their teeth and strain with all their might to 
draw it taut, called for both admiration and incredu- 
lous amazement. Our friend who was thus handi- 
capped was of a mixed disposition. He always em- 
ployed a great part of each march droning out 'Ti- 
betan prayers, the balance he spent in hurling what 
must have been the most frightful invectives at his 
charges. One word that constantly recurred with 
stinging emphasis was a counterpart of the name of 
the Dominion to our north, with the last syllable 
stressed: “Canada!” 

Until midnight I followed on the heels of our pious 


131 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


muleteer, really enjoying the march in spite of the 
difficulties of locomotion. We bivouacked for the re- 
mainder of the night in a hollow amid a grove of 
spruce. 

Next morning we continued our way down the val- 
ley, but it was not until after three hours’ marching 
that we came upon the first habitation. Much of the 
country through which we had passed since leaving 
Likiang was a howling wilderness. For several days 
at a time we would meet with no inhabitant nor see 
anything but the tumble-down abandoned cabin of 
some wood-cutter or yak herd. It was now not until 
we were within eight or ten miles of Tatsienlu that 
we came upon any signs of cultivated land. The town 
most unexpectedly revealed itself to us, tucked deep 
down in the valley at the junction of two rivers. It 
seemed to have changed but little since the days of 
Abbé Huc. He tells us that Tatsienlu means the 
Forge of the Arrows, a name given it because in A.D. 
234 General Wu Hou, when leading his armies against 
the Southern Countries, sent ahead one of his lieu- 
tenants to establish an arrow forge here. 

Doctor and Mrs. Cunningham, of the China Inland 
Mission, most hospitably took us all in, and faced 
with Christian resignation all the inconvenience and 
confusion that followed in our train, particularly 
when we started dividing and rearranging our grimy 
kit. Both of the Cunninghams are Scotch and have 


132 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


spent the greater part of their lives in China. They 
were of inestimable help to us, and all we could hope 
was that it would some time be in our power to re- 
turn, in a small way, some of their many kindnesses. 

Cunningham had gathered three local hunters, who 
paraded in his compound the morning after our ar- 
rival. With them we went over the pictures of the 
possible game of the region. They were suspiciously 
glib in their responses, and unduly emphatic as to the 
quantity in which each of the species existed. Giant 
pandar and takin were those in which we were par- 
ticularly interested. The former has, so far as we 
could learn, never been shot by a white man, and we 
were confident that it only existed in very small num- 
bers, and that our chances for securing one were ex- 
ceedingly remote. The hunters, however, declared 
that although they hibernated, as do bears, some were 
already up and about, and if we waited until June 
we would see any quantity of them. These remarks 
did not give us much confidence in the balance of the 
information, but as the hunters claimed that the coun- 
try of which they spoke was only two days distant 
we agreed to start off with them after a day’s halt in 
which to replenish our supplies and take care of the 
multitudinous caravan wants. 

Takin have rarely been shot in this region. Mason 
Mitchell, when he was American Consul in Chung- 
king, was the first white man to shoot takin of this 


133 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


race. It was in May, 1907, that Mr. Mitchell made 
the expedition and secured his takin two days’ travel 
north of Tatsienlu. He sent skins to the American 
Museum and to Rowland Ward in London. Mr. 
Ward wrote him in May, 1908, that the takin proved 
to be a new species and had been named in Mr. 
Mitchell’s honor. 

Since then several Europeans have shot the takin 
of this district and Roy Andrews has shot them in 
Kansu. Wallace, in his exceedingly interesting “Big 
Game of Western China,” is able to add to his text 
the immeasurable advantage of excellent sketches 
made in the field from life studies of the Kansu takin. 

Tatsienlu is the great meeting-mart of trade and 
travel between Tibet and this western section of 
China. The population amounts to about twenty 
thousand, of which half are Chinese, a quarter Ti- 
betan, and the remainder either a mixture of the two 
or representatives of border tribes. Three or four 
bridges connect the transfluvial portions of the town. 
In most Chinese villages it is to the bridges that one 
must go if seeking the junk-dealers and the possibil- 
ity of “finds.” There the peddlers will be found 
squatting during the middle hours of the day with a 
varied assortment of odds and ends spread out before 
them. Old bronzes and bits of ivory, pieces of jade 
and ancient sets of dominoes are scattered among 
cheap gimcracks, glass bottles, and tin biscuit-boxes. 

134 


ROUND, AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


Tatsienlu, perhaps because of the mixed nature of 
its inhabitants, was an exception, and the junk-deal- 
ers lived in the depths of grimy booths, from which 
we extracted a few items of interest, as well as some 
useful sausage-like rawhide bags, smelling strongly of 
yak butter and tsamba, in which to carry the quantity 
of silver coin it was necessary to take with us. 

As soon as word spread that we were interested in 
old things, lamas began, singly or in groups, filing 
into the Cunninghams’ compound. From the folds of 
their robes they produced every sort of temple orna- 
ment and accessory, bells, drums, gongs, braziers, tea- 
pots, horns and casks for wine, pictures, robes, and 
cooking and eating utensils. A few of them were 
somewhat gruesome, such as the bowls made from hu- 
man skulls and the trumpets from thigh bones. We 
selected such things as combined interest with a de- 
gree of bulk and weight commensurate with our trans- 
portation facilities. Bargaining of the most intricate 
and advanced sort lasted often all day, with many 
comings and goings and a great display of total lack 
of interest. 

Suydam spent a busy morning with his moving- 
picture camera in the caravanserais. He was par- 
ticularly interested in photographing a great pack- 
train of shaggy yaks; several times it seemed as if his 
zeal to secure just the angle or view he wished would 
cause him to fall a victim to the savage dogs that 


135 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


were guarding the loads. From Tatsienlu the yak 
trains turn back, for the big beasts cannot work at any 
lower altitude. The market here is supplied not only 
with yak butter and cheese, but with yak beef, which 
we found to be excellent. 

One evening we dined with the Magistrate. All the 
foreign population had been invited, consisting of 
Cunningham, Doctor Andrews of the Seventh Day 
Adventist Mission, and the French Fathers. Dur- 
ing the afternoon we called upon Pére Girandeau, 
dean of the colony, completing his fifty-first year of 
residence. As is the custom, his coffin was already 
built and held in readiness. These Fathers come out 
for life, and usually do not return to France, even on 
leave. The old Father was hale and strong; a most 
delightful type of French priest, as indeed we found 
each and all to be. 

The mission was represented at the dinner by F'a- 
thers Valentin and Pasteur, the former of twenty-five 
years’ residence, and the latter a last year’s arrival. 
The single Tibetan present was the head of the Ti- 
betan Board of Trade. His Chinese counterpart 
had only recently been released from solitary confine- 
ment, where the Magistrate had held him incomuni- 
cado because of his objection to the raising of a 
substantial levy from the Chinese merchants for mag- 
isterial purposes. Upon what grounds his release 
was effected we did not learn, but the greatest bon- 


136 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


hommie appeared to exist between the Magistrate 
and his erstwhile prisoner, and they pledged each 
other continually in bowls of fiery rice wine. 

The dinner was the customary succession of dishes 
of pork and duck and vegetables. The two we found 
best were a first course of vermicelli with condiments 
spread over it, and a mess of excellent Tibetan mush- 
rooms. In one corner of the room was a divan ar- 
rayed with all the appurtenances of opium smoking. 
Several of the guests left the table for a pipe during 
the course of dinner. It is difficult to see how any 
great advance can be made in this part of China to- 
ward the eradication of the use of opium. In spite 
of edicts and proclamations the drug is everywhere in 
open use, and seems to be a particular curse to the 
wretched coolies, the greater part of whose earnings 
are dissipated in “black smoke.” 

The Magistrate we found a pleasant, intelligent 
man, ready to be of whatever assistance possible. 
From the missionaries we learned that he was a great 
improvement on some of his predecessors, one of 
whom in particular had a choleric and bloodthirsty 
disposition, which had caused the death of many a 
petty offender by the firing squad or drowning in the 
river. This man had had a cook, who had been with 
him many years, beheaded because of a conflagration 
in the kitchen which caused damage to the Magis- 
trate’s property. At length he was driven out, and 


137 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


when last heard of was living in great poverty in 
Peking. 

After two days devoted to having bedding rolls 
and boots repaired, and purchasing sugar and tea and 
flour, we were ready to start. Bidding good-by to 
our friends Luzon and Aju we loaded up a ula or 
corvée caravan. This system of forced transportation 
is much in use in this region when Magistrates or 
officials are travelling. Theoretically a village is 
obliged to supply transport gratis for a certain stage, 
but practically you pay them about twenty-five cents 
a day for an animal, or its equivalent in coolies. A 
confusion of yaks and donkeys and porters gathered 
in the compound, and in course of time our loads were 
assimilated. For those who can afford to hire a 
straight yak or mule pack train ula can scarcely be 
recommended, but we were glad to have the oppor- 
tunity of trying it out where our destination lay suf- 
ficiently close to render impossible any considerable 
loss of time. During some of the marches from 
Chuilung our scratch caravan had been managed by 
women muleteers, and now more than half our por- 
ters were girls and women. 

Finances caused the usual difficulty. Yunnanese 
half-dollars were readily accepted in the town, but 
twenty-cent pieces were not. The currency in general 
use was what was known as the Tibetan rupee; oc- 
casionally an old “John Company” rupee turned up. 


138 


LYUOdSNVUL MVA 


VAL CGHYaLLNG ONINYONAO SUAALATAW UNO AO ANO 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


A simple method was in vogue for making eight-anna 
pieces; the rupees were merely clipped in two bits. 
Shanghai dollars and silver ingots were also in cir- 
culation; the latter varied slightly, but were worth 
roughly fourteen rupees. Copper cash and two hun- 
dred cash pieces were used for smaller purchases. As 
may be imagined prices and methods for calculating 
them varied greatly, but it was still more difficult 
when leaving this polyglot centre to discover what 
coins would be acceptable in the smaller towns, where 
cosmopolitanism was not so far advanced. 

Our route took us through the north Lhasa Gate. 
Beside it were two stone columns with heads carved 
upon them. They represent a sort of local Saint 
Christopher to whom caravans make offerings and 
address a prayer when starting out on a journey. 
Half a mile beyond the gate lies the Catholic ceme- 
tery, where the pedestrian explorer, General Pereira, 
is buried. Close to his grave is that of Pére Davenat, 
who never recovered from his barbarous treatment at 
the hands of the lamas. He was stationed at a lonely 
post six or seven days’ journey from Tatsienlu. 
During the uprising among the Lamas in 1911 he 
was overpowered, stripped, and tied to a post in front 
of a temple. Each lama on entering or leaving 
plucked a hair from his long beard. This happened 
in January and it was eleven days before Pére Dave- 
nat was rescued. 

139 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


It was late in the day before we got started, so 
were able to make but a short march to a dirty little 
village, where we were hospitably put up by the head 
man. Our trail had led up along a river. One little 
hamlet we passed was known for its warm sulphur 
springs. Some of these were roofed over, but three 
good-sized ones lay open to the air, and in them, in 
spite of the bitter cold, were crowds of men and boys. 
When we came back it was even colder, with a light 
snow falling, and this time it was evidently Ladies’ 
Day, for the pools were filled with women and girls, 
not in the least abashed at bathing in Eve’s costume 
by the roadside. 

In order to develop as speedily as possible the po- 
tentialities of the valley as a game country, we ar- 
ranged to split, and after tossing a coin for locations 
Suydam won the farthest down-stream. Accord- 
ingly at our noonday halt on the following day we 
left him behind in an unusually dirty stone house. All 
the way up the valley we inquired of each person met 
concerning panda and takin. Ted had taken the en- 
gravings from Milne Edwards’ “Mammiféres de 
PAsie Centrale,” and kept them in a portfolio to 
show them for identification purposes. The informa- 
tion which we drew was eminently unsatisfactory; no 
one had ever encountered a panda or would admit 
having even seen the skin of one. Takin they knew 
about, but said that they were a long way off, and 


140 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


that the deep snow rendered them inaccessible from 
this valley. 

To further fortify our distrust of our Tatsienlu 
hunters Hsuen produced a small wiry man with an 
ancient matchlock on his shoulders. He hailed from 
a district fifteen days distant, but claimed to have 
often hunted in this region. He said that we could 
find burrhel and stag and musk-deer; in another 
month the bear would be out too, but takin were too 
far away and inaccessible, and panda he had never 
seen. 

This definitely discredited our original informa- 
tion, but as we were close to my destined village, we 
agreed that Ted should stop over with me, and that 
as long as we had already lost so much valuable time, 
we would put in a day’s hunting, each taking one 
side of the valley in the hopes of picking up stag or 
burrhel. We spent a rather disconsolate evening, for 
we had clearly been led on a wild-goose chase in spite 
of the glowing accounts at Tatsienlu. All three 
men had claimed to have seen panda; Suydam’s 
hunter said he had killed one, but added that he had 
performed this feat four or five days’ march further 
up the valley. 

It snowed during the night, so our hunters made 
the plea that it was not hunting weather, and that we 
had better stay indoors. This we overruled, and I 
crossed the river and started abruptly up the moun- 


141 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


tainside. The newly fallen snow made a fairyland of 
the bush and tree-clad lower slopes, but the going 
was far from easy. Four hours’ plodding brought us 
well out on top. We had seen no fresh sign, although 
in the old snow we came upon occasional tracks of 
stag. 

As we were studying the country for game, we 
heard Ted, across the valley, shooting. I had with 
me the native hunter we had met on the previous day; 
he said that Ted was on burrhel ground, and had run 
into a herd. It was cold work spying out the coun- 
try, and our labors went unrewarded. The only fresh 
tracks we came upon were those of musk-deer; aside 
from this we saw some pheasant and partridge, and 
a troop of the Tibetan eared pheasant—Crossoptilus 
Tibetenum—a white and gray bird the size of a small 
turkey. Before reaching Tatsienlu we had run into 
these birds and had shot some with our Springfields. 
They did not prove much of an addition to the pot. 

I got back to the village after dark. Ted was not 
yet in. He had sighted burrhel shortly after starting 
out. A very long stalk brought him into range, and 
at a hundred and fifty yards he shot two fine rams of 
these “blue sheep.” He then bagged a young male, 
under the impression that it was a ewe and would 
complete the group. He had sent in for yaks to bring 
in the skins and meat, but they were slow in coming 
out, so that he and Guffer Sheikh had already 

142 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


stripped the hides off, and staggered part way down 
the mountain with their burdens before they met the 
yaks. 

My Tatsienlu shikari made heavy weather of 
the climbing, but he was a pleasant, cheerful fel- 
low. Ted’s had proved utterly useless. Shortly after 
leaving camp he became ill, and announced he could 
go no further. Ted thereupon put him in front and 
poked him along. The hunter periodically fell upon 
his knees and prayed to be allowed to go back. 
Eventually he gave out completely, and was sent in 
for the yaks. 

Jack Young had put in a busy day and had an in- 
teresting squirrel and some birds, all neatly prepared, 
as witness to his efforts. 

There were four or five houses in the village. Each 
had one room which could serve as a chapel, with 
altar, holy books, drum and gong. We were domi- 
ciled in one such, and in another house an itinerant 
lama was holding a special service. Far into the 
night he droned out his texts, and clashed his cymbals. 

Next day, the 4th of March, we turned back to 
Tatsienlu. I had written Suydam, telling him of 
our change of plans, but the messenger had been in 
no hurry, arriving only a few minutes ahead of us, 
instead of on the previous evening. Suydam had, 
therefore, already gone off hunting, and did not get 
into Tatsienlu until the following morning. His 

143 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


local hunter had proved the worst of the three; Suy- 
dam had started off for a brief hunt on the afternoon 
we left him, and at the end of half an hour the hunter 
became violently sick, disappeared, and that was the 
last that was seen of him! In spite of this Suydam 
three times saw game, each time sambhur or stag, but 
never succeeded in getting a shot. Once he came upon 
what were probably fresh leopard tracks. 

The messages we had sent on in advance to Ta- 
tsienlu to speed our transportation arrangements 
met with no better success than that to Suydam. One 
of them, with twenty-four hours start, arrived several 
hours after us. We now wished to head toward Mu- 
ping, inaccessible by animal transport, so we arranged 
to send Jack Young and much of our kit by the main 
road to Yachow while we hired coolies to take our 
necessities with us. 

In spite of the combined efforts of Cunningham 
and the Magistrate things moved slowly. Part of 
the delay we employed most satisfactorily in accept- 
ing the Andrews’ invitation to use the Japanese bath- 
tub which they had installed. The last bath I had in 
one was after a strenuous shooting trip in Korea. A 
small stove is built under the wooden tub, so that the 
water continues to become hotter, and instead of be- 
ing driven out by increasing tepidity, one is eventu- 
ally forced to choose between flight and scalding. 

Further ambling through the bazaars revealed lit- 


144 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


tle new. Among the delicacies for the table were deer 
legs, such as the Magistrates at Chuilung had given 
Suydam, with the sinews and remnants of meat that 
clung to them hanging loose in shreds. Also there 
were gruesome appearing monkey’s paws or fore- 
arms. A number of roe deer horns in the apothecary 
shop interested us. 

Lamas continued to appear in the Mission Com- 
pound and we bought a few odds and ends from 
them. I met a Tibetan in the bazaar with a skin- 
covered wooden wine-cask slung over his shoulder. 
At first he resolutely refused to sell, but some amus- 
ing and good-humored chaffing, in which all by- 
standers joined, induced him to part with it. 

On the afternoon of the 6th of March we eventu- 
ally succeeded in getting our coolies and starting 
down river. The fall in altitude is very noticeable. 
In the twenty-odd miles to Waszekow one drops bet- 
ter than three thousand feet, and from winter we 
found ourselves in early spring. The first night we 
spent at a filthy little roadside hostelry, where we laid 
our bedding rolls in the loft. The wind howled and 
roared all night as if it were about to uproot the 
house. The noise below us was continuous, and the 
rather pleasant smell of opium smoke soon yielded 
place to far less agreeable odors. Early in the morn- 
ing a most appalling row started up. At first it 
sounded as if several women were being murdered; 


145 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


then men’s voices were raised in acrid anger; the 
babel of indescribable and shrilly demoniacal impo- 
tent Asiatic wrath rose and fell below us as we break- 
fasted. Through Hsuen we learned that it was a 
family fight between the landlord and his wife and 
their daughter. The last named had “not been very 
kind to people, she call her parents very bad words,” 
a sort of “revolt of modern youth” in far Szechuan. 
The sounds of carnage had not entirely died down 
when we left. 

The next day’s marching was beneath a glorious 
sunny sky; the fruit trees were in bloom, and the 
mountain fields, heaped and fenced with the stones 
that had been laboriously cleared from their surfaces, 
showed a faint fresh green. Cactus hedges put in an 
appearance, and palm trees rose gracefully among 
the peach and plum blossoms. More than all, the 
lazy, indolent feeling of Spring was in the air, and 
after the long weeks in the high altitudes of biting 
cold we all felt relaxed and uninclined to effort. 

The traffic which we met along the road was in the 
main long lines of coolies with towering loads of brick 
tea bound for Tibet. The loads are of impossible 
weights, ranging all the way up to four hundred 
pounds. The coolies would put to shame Kurdish 
hammals in Stambul. They are of all ages and both 
sexes, the packs graduated according to the strength 
of the very old and the very young. The day’s march 
is short, rarely exceeding ten miles, but although the

ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


trail here on the main road was not too bad, after it 
turned off across the mountains it changed radically 
in character, and was difficult to negotiate without 
any burden and with the aid of a khud stick. 

The tea is packed in slender rolls of matting three 
and a half to four feet long; a load will measure as 
high as six feet, and all roadside accommodations are 
built with these measurements in view. Occasional 
stone walls along the trail are made a suitable height, 
and in the inns a bench of appropriate distance from 
the ground runs along the walls. Each coolie car- 
ries a stout stick with a crosspiece on which to rest 
his load at intervals and take the weight off his weary 
back. The shod point of the stick wears round holes 
in the rocks along the trail. 

Thirty miles below Tatsienlu we first saw river 
craft, three coracles made of rawhide, one of which 
bobbed gracefully through the rapids with two pine 
logs in tow. Close to Lutingkiao we passed a large 
scow, perhaps used as a ferry. Near by a man stood 
on a rock, fishing in the rapid water. He used a sec- 
tion of bamboo as a reel, and would cast far out into 
the stream, and then by a deft movement of the wrist 
wind the line round and round his primitive reel. 
What at first we took for a fly proved to be a bit of 
feather used as a float. The fish were bottom feeders 
and a lead sinker was attached to the line. Upon the 
fisherman’s back hung a bamboo creel. 

From Lutingkiao we turned abruptly up across 

147 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


the mountains. We got started at six-forty-five and 
plodded up-hill all morning, stopping for an hour at 
noon in a small mountain hamlet. Continuing our 
climb, no longer in the balmy air of the valley, we 
reached the crest of the pass at three o’clock. Along 
the trail were many shrines, some neglected and tum- 
ble-down, others well tended and popular. Some- 
times our coolies stopped to burn a joss stick. Larger 
shrines were fitted with a gong so that the god’s at- 
tention could be attracted before the offering was 
made and the prayer was said. Sometimes there were 
only tablets in the shrines, but more frequently there 
were representatives of Shang Shen, the God of the 
Mountains, and his wife Tu Dei, Goddess of Earth. 
The god was usually portrayed as an old man with 
a long white beard. It was difficult to understand 
why the fickle coolies had allowed some of the shrines 
to fall into decay. At one spot a most benevolent and 
well executed representative of the god was seated 
in an arm-chair with his back to the path, the rotted 
rafters of his shrine tumbling about his ears. 

Over the top of the pass billowed a cold thick mist, 
which soon shut from sight everything beyond the 
radius of ten feet. The trail was deep in snow, and 
the rocks were coated in ice. A woman and a man 
had started from Lutingkiao with us. The former 
was a cheerful, pleasant looking personage who plod- 
ded along the trail unconcernedly. The latter, a 


148 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


young official, rode on a coolie’s back whenever the 
going permitted. He arrived at the top of the pass 
with his head drooped over the coolie’s, and his arms 
dangling over the coolie’s shoulders. Among the 
Nhambiquara Indians in South America it was the 
custom to carry their pet monkeys on their heads. At 
first sight it looked as if they were wearing a cap of 
monkey skin, and the effect was startling when the 
monkey with a sudden movement raised its head. 
The coolie-back official reminded me forcibly of 
Nhambiquara land. 

We had started from Lutingkiao at an altitude of 
four thousand eight hundred feet, the top of the pass 
was ten thousand one hundred feet, and we dropped 
again to six thousand four hundred feet for our 
night’s lodging. As we descended the mist changed 
to a persistent rain which soon drenched us. Our 
path lay down a narrow gorge, with the glimmer of 
frozen waterfalls on either side. Darkness found us 
stumbling about among the rocks. Suydam and I 
were with one competent coolie. At a mountain hut 
he got a bamboo torch, which lighted us well until the 
rain extinguished it. Thereafter, until we reached the 
rambling cabin that was our destination, we from time 
to time got a new torch to help us on, and the balance 
of the distance found our way by feel and fall. It 
was quarter-past nine before the tail of the caravan 
arrived. 

149 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Next morning we were off at seven-thirty, and 
made a raw, bleak march down the valley. In the 
village we saw an ancient takin skin and at our night’s 
halt we found an old man who said he had hunted five 
years before, beyond Muping, and had shot takin and 
trapped a panda in a pitfall. He said that he had 
the skin of the latter in his house, a day’s march dis- 
tant. We agreed to purchase it if he would bring it 
to Tienchuan for us. His accounts sounded fairly 
authentic. He insisted that the panda did not hiber- 
nate, thus supporting Pereira’s report and contradict- 
ing that of Ernest Wilson. We were inclined to give 
credence to this report, for our informant was careful 
to point out in contradistinction that the black bear 
did sleep. 

Ted bought a long brown animal that he saw a 
man skinning in a house near which we passed. It 
looked to be something in the nature of the honey 
badger or ratel of Africa. Ted finished the skinning 
at the night’s halt. 

Next morning we awoke to sleet and snow, and the 
head man came in to report bandits ahead and ask 
permission to lie over a day. On the previous day 
we had passed an outlying house burned down by 
raiders a week or ten days past. Our futoh explained 
that the trail turned off from the river and climbed 
over a snowy and well-forested mountain, joining the 
river again as it emerged from a gorge. Three men 


150 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


who had started with an early caravan had come trail- 
ing back to say that they had been attacked and fired 
at near the top of the pass. 

We took our two spare rifles out and armed our 
shikarries, trusting that they would in case of need 
live up to the expectations of one of the missionaries’ 
wives, who had said she “supposed that hunting was 
difficult, but that our shikarries must be very good 
shots, and could secure the game for us.” The flip of 
a coin put Ted and Suydam at the head of the col- 
umn, while I brought up the rear. Hsuen, who since 
his adventures with the 410 had been known as the 
Wildgrave, announced that he did not wish to kill a 
bandit, but that he was not afraid, for if he were 
killed, being a Christian, he would go home to Heaven. 
Three members of the loca! militia accompanied us, 
and acquitted themselves well. As we toiled along 
through the snow on our fifteen hundred foot climb, 
they kept darting ahead reconnoitring. Twice they 
fired shots at what they reported to be the forms of 
robbers. 

The newly fallen snow weighing down pine and 
larch was as lovely as it always is, but the descent to 
the river was trying in the extreme. The laden por- 
ters fell far less often than we did; Suydam took one 
particularly disagreeable header, filling his rifle with 
mud. Leaving out the night marches, I saw but five 
falls on the part of our coolies. 

151 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Upon reaching the village on the river bank we 
learned that the head man of our last night’s halt had 
sent ahead to ask that militia be sent to meet us at 
the head of the pass. Five men had been despatched. 
They had fallen in with the bandits, twenty in num- 
ber, and had been carried off by them, rifles and all. 
Unfortunately it was beyond our power to do any- 
thing to remedy the situation. 

The valley now opened, and cultivated fields and 
gardens stretched out on either bank. The small iso- 
lated fields of tea bushes became more numerous, and 
the tall yellow mustard converted whole hillsides into 
“fields of the cloth of gold.” Fruit trees, apricot, 
plum, and cherry, were in bloom, and graced the 
courtyards of an ancient temple by which we passed. 
In former days it must have been a popular place of 
pilgrimage, now it was falling into decay. 

The great lichened stones withstood the inroads of 
time and weather more successfully than the rafters 
and columns of the buildings. A veritable galaxy of 
gods stood in rows about the walls. Between the feet 
of the principal deity in the centre temple, stood the 
wooden image of a dog, a wistful, homelike canine, 
that gave a very human feeling to the assemblage of 
strange gods and goddesses. 

There are coal mines in this valley; sometimes we 
saw coal in use as fuel, and often we met women car- 
rying basket loads on their backs. In strong contrast 


152 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


to Yunnan, we here saw no women with bound feet. 
Rope bridges were in constant use for crossing the 
stream. A heavy bamboo or grass cable was stretched 
across, with a smaller rope strung along it through 
bamboo rings. The passenger seated himself astride 
a crosspiece of wood, which was attached by a stout 
cord to a concave bit of tree trunk, five or six inches 
in length. This piece of wood slid along the cable as 
the man pulled himself across by the rope line. Coolies 
were at work rafting pine trees and clearing log jams, 
lightening their labors with shanty choruses. 

In the stream I saw wild cormorants, here appar- 
ently not in use for fishing as they are in more settled 
parts of China. Ted saw some ducks, among which 
he thought that he distinguished that excessively rare 
bird, the wild mandarin. 

On the twelfth we reached Tienchuan, a large ram- 
bling town, where we put up at the native Baptist 
Mission. Foreigners must be an unusual sight, for 
the universal curiosity of China we here found raised 
to the nth power. Swarms surrounded us if we poked 
our noses out of our room, and the paper windows 
were changed to sieves by the fingers that were stuck 
through them to produce peepholes to secure a view 
of the strange bearded people eating or reading or 
undressing. 

It seemed incongruous to find such a large town 
having no connections with the outside world either 


153 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


by land or water. Everything that was brought in 
or taken out travelled on man’s back. As we wan- 
dered through the long crowded streets we saw no 
four-footed beasts of burden, and in none of the 
shops were there pack saddles or horseshoes or any- 
thing that has to do with caravan trade, and which 
one is accustomed to see in all villages, however 
small. 

Tienchuan means Complete Heaven, but to Oc- 
cidental eyes, at any rate, there was much to belie the 
name. As in so many of the crowded Chinese towns 
dire poverty stared you in the face and the halt, the 
maimed, and the blind were omnipresent. There was 
less goitre than in many other places, but it here 
seemed to take a particularly aggravated form; fear- 
ful diseases were perambulating the streets, and a 
dead boy lay stretched out near a temple gate with 
the flies on his eyelids. There is no need to mention 
the smells. 

Instead of consisting of a single long street, there
