were several other large streets and many branching 
alleys. Intricately carved arches spanned the roads, 
and more than one spacious temple courtyard, with a 
glimpse of tall trees and stone balustrades, invited 
the passer-by to enter. In the small shrines at the 
street turnings the faces of the gods had been smeared 
with opium, to which stuck the feathers of the chick- 
ens that had been sacrificed. 


154 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


Our next day’s march was to be very long. On 
engaging the porters we had asked the futoh or head 
man how long it would take us to reach Muping if 
we travelled fast. He replied ten days, and we then 
offered substantial baksheesh to him and to each 
coolie for every day saved. This caused the futoh to 
work out a revised schedule, reducing the marches to 
eight. We paid this caravan for the weight carried, 
so much per pound, a most satisfactory method that 
I have rarely seen in use. It does not then concern 
one how many men are employed or how the loads 
are divided. The head man works it out so that those 
carrying the heaviest weights receive the most money, 
and when loads are exchanged on trail it is all taken 
into consideration. 

We got under way early, our trail leading through 
the China of the illustrations from the books of the 
early missionaries, or the old-fashioned school geog- 
raphy,—a broken countryside whose hills were ter- 
raced with paddy fields and planted from top to bot- 
tom with tea and grain. Weaving about among them 
the ancient footpath threaded its way. Busy men and 
women toiled in the fields. Occasionally a temple 
crowned a knoll, embowered in tall shade trees. Farm- 
houses dotted the hollows. Near one, amid much beat- 
ing of gongs and loud singing, a funeral was taking 
place. 

Our noonday halt was in an old millhouse where 


155 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


they were grinding cornmeal between enormous mill 
stones. Spring was in the valley, and not only were 
the fruit trees in bloom, but wild flowers bedecked the 
grassy sides of the trail. 

In the afternoon we climbed up a steep hill, and em- 
barked upon an abrupt descent over slippery stones 
embedded in deep mud in which we all of us more than 
once found ourselves floundering. Darkness fell be- 
fore we reached the bottom and it was not until 
another hour had passed that we entered the long 
single street of Lingkwan. The scene might have 
been the original of a Japanese color print. At the 
entrance to the town we passed four or five smithies 
with the clang of hammers upon molten iron and the 
sparks flaring like brilliant fireflies in the darkness. 
The street was lit with a long row of paper lanterns, 
casting in dim relief the entrance to shop and resi- 
dence. 

The inn where we took refuge was neither spa- 
cious nor clean, but after the long day’s tramp we 
wanted only sufficient space in which to lay our bed- 
ding rolls. Late as it was, an inquisitive crowd gath- 
ered and it was not until we had plunged the room in 
darkness that a shuffling of feet outside the windows 
and from behind the cracks in the wall announced that 
our spectators were dispersing. 

Next day brought us to Muping. The coolies were 
a weary lot, but only one stayed behind, a weedy little 


156 


ROUND AND ABOUT TATSIENLU 


man who seemed to suffer more than the rest from the 
effects of opium smoking. Apparently all used the 
drug to a greater or less degree. After a long march, 
much of which led through a narrow gorge from the 
rocky sides of which the path was built out on worn 
and broken logs, we reached the little town of Mu- 
ping, from which we planned to initiate our hunt for 
panda and takin. 


157 


CHAPTER VII 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 
“And the Gods of the east made mouths at me.”—Kipling. 


Murine was one of those strange little semi-inde- 
pendent feudal kingdoms that fringe Tibet. From 
time immemorial it had owed some shadowy allegiance 
to whomever might be its nearest powerful neighbor. 
Close as it is to Chengtu, which has been a great 
Chinese city since long before the days of the three 
kingdoms, it is remarkable that it retained its indi- 
viduality so long. Its annals are a continuous record 
of struggles against outside aggression. The curtain 
dropped on the drama only last summer. The ruling 
family was Tibetan. The last prince died some years 
ago, leaving a wife and one daughter. A match was 
arranged with a prince of the Chalu family of Ta- 
tsienlu, who came to Muping. About a year ago he 
was killed, either by his own vassals or the Chi- 
nese,—reports differ. ‘To-day there is a Chinese mag- 
istrate in Muping, and all that remains of its tribal 
chieftains are three women living in poverty and se- 
clusion. 

Meanwhile the Chinese people who, rather than 

158 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


their rulers, are the real invaders, have seeped into the 
country. They have intermarried with the natives. 
Now the larger towns are mainly Chinese, and even 
the smaller are composed almost entirely of a mixed 
race. ‘The pure-blood tribesmen are found only in the 
fastnesses of the mountains. 

The magistrate at Muping was a genial old fellow. 
The evening we arrived we sent him our letters, ac- 
companied by gifts of a turquoise ring and a foun- 
tain pen. The response was immediate. Our envoy 
Hswen returned to report that the magistrate was 
following hard on his heels to pay us a call. We were 
in the Chinese Christian Mission. When he arrived 
we showed him into a room where we had set up our 
roorkie chairs, and gave him a cup of tea, some fig 
newtons, a glass of blackberry brandy, and a mechan- 
ical flashlight. Then we developed our wishes. He 
was as kind as could be; he knew of takin and panda, 
and told us he would be glad to help us get native 
hunters. He was not very encouraging about the 
panda, saying that it was shot only rarely. We asked 
if we could see a skin but were told there were none 
in town. He agreed, however, to send to the outlying 
villages and try to get one. 

He showed the most astonishing lack of informa- 
tion in unexpected ways. He said he did not know 
the name of the river that flowed by the town, ex- 
plaining that he had only been here “one year.” We 

159 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


parted with much ceremony and good feeling, to meet 
at lunch at his yamen the next afternoon. 

Our day of rest was delightful. We had what our 
Kashmiri servants call a “Europe morning,” lying 
abed till seven. Then a rather slimy small wooden 
tub was produced and we soaped off what our old 
Irish nurse used to call “the biggest of the dirt,’— 
that is to say Kermit and I did. By the time Suydam 
started his bath a buzzing crowd of little boys had 
gathered to watch, so he modestly retired to the dank 
cellar-like inner room. 

A half-hour’s stroll in the warm sun took us, and 
an escort of 80 per cent of the population, from 
one end of the town to the other. There were the re- 
mains of past glories in the shape of a tumbled stone 
lamassary,—now the soldiers’ barracks,—an old fort 
crowning a near-by hill, and the ruined footings of 
what had been the king’s palace, with two big stone 
lions guarding the entrance. We stopped at the 
largest temple in town. It was Chinese. On each 
side of the court there were stall-like recesses where 
grouped figures represented scenes from hell. The 
figures were modelled in clay on a base of straw. They 
were rude but brightly colored and full of action. 
Black devils acted as jailors and torturers and were 
burning, dismembering, and hanging various sinners. 
At the far end of the court the major gods were 
grouped. They too were of clay, and differed in lit- 

160 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


tle but size from the others. Like most Chinese tem- 
ples I have seen, everything was dilapidated. One 
god lacked an arm, another a head. They seemed 
but little reverenced. The people who followed us 
swarmed among them, chattering and gesticulating 
like a crowd of children at an exhibition of waxworks. 
I noticed one discarded deity negligently rolled on his 
side beneath the seat of another. Perhaps the Chi- 
nese are discovering that these idols of theirs have feet 
of clay in more than one sense. 

Dinner at the magistrate’s was the usual affair,— 
rice wine, dishes of chopped meats, and greens. The 
guests besides ourselves were the head-man of the 
district where we hoped to get local hunters, the head 
of the local militia, and a keen-faced young fellow 
who commanded the regular troops. This latter soon 
monopolized the conversation. He asked about the 
number of troops in the United States Army, their 
rifles, their artillery, and a number of other military 
questions. Then he switched to more general matters. 
Three of his questions particularly interested me. 
They were: “What does the United States think of 
Doctor Sun Yat Sen and the Chinese republic? Will 
the United States be able to stop another European 
war? Why are Japan and England unfriendly to 
China?” 

When the bowl of rice which finishes a Chinese 
feast was brought us, the magistrate presented us with 


161 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


four dried birds. They were all shot in the surround- 
ing mountains. One I recognized as the Lady Am- 
herst pheasant. One was very large with coal-black 
breast, shading to iridescent blue and green on the 
back. One was the size of a goose with dust-brown 
plumage varied only by a patch of white on the rump. 
The last was the smallest, only the size of a Guinea 
hen. Its colors were particularly lovely, rich orange 
shot with gray. It had an orange crest. That eve- 
ning the head-man arrived from the village where we 
were to go. He told us he had shot both panda and 
takin within the past four years. We arranged to go 
with him next day. 

A pathetic incident of our day at Muping was a 
visit from the widow of the Tibetan prince from Ta- 
tsienlu. She came with her daughter, a girl of eleven. 
The woman was plain, middle-aged, and rather stupid 
looking. The purpose of their visit was to ask us to 
intercede for them with the Chengtu government. 
They were afraid that the government might have 
them executed. I am persuaded that their fear was 
groundless, but it was very real to them. They 
brought us gifts of dried meat. We gave them in re- 
turn a silk scarf. On leaving us they stopped at our 
kitchen to beg an empty tin can from the cook. 

Next morning we were packed and ready early. 
Of course the porters did not come. After waiting an 
hour I went out with Hswen to get them. We found 


162 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


they were scattered all over the town, sleeping, so we 
sent out Chinese Paul Reveres to rouse them. When 
they finally did gather they were an oddly assorted 
lot,—“Andy Hartigan’s bobbery pack.” They ranged 
from boys not much older than my own children to 
men whose beards were turning white. Only about 
half a dozen were strong full-grown coolies. With a 
scandalous amount of talk and confusion the packs 
were sorted and made up. We started down the street 
in a long straggling line, with frequent halts. First 
they had forgotten to buy food. Then they wanted to 
get more opium. 

When we had passed the massive stone gate mat- 
ters went better. The weather was fine. With just 
a touch of spring in the air. The sun shone warm- 
ly. The river droned soothingly over its rocks. Tier 
on tier of little fields lined the sides of the hills 
on either side. Our path was excellent. At times it 
ran where galleries had been hewn in the solid rock. 
They were a great contrast to the flimsy platforms of 
poles on which we had crossed the face of similar cliffs 
on our way to Muping. We were told that for pur- 
poses of defense the old rulers of the kingdom made 
access to their country as difficult as possible, and 
travel within it as easy. Sometimes in these galleries 
we came on gaily painted gods set in niches. 

Once we saw a couple of fishermen. They were 
using the usual bamboo poles with twisted wire guides 


163 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


for the line, but they were not bait-fishing. Each 
man had three hooks firmly fastened by the shank to 
the end of his line. These, with a lead to hold them 
below the surface, he cast into the rapid water. With 
a series of sharp jerks he then tried to snag such fish 
as his hook might touch. Below the rapids a half- 
dozen more natives were throwing stones into the 
stream to frighten the fish into the swift water. 

One of the fishermen wound his line on the simple 
bamboo spindle we had noticed before. The other 
used a small wheel fixed to the side of his rod by a 
wooden pin, much the same arrangement as preceded 
the modern reel in England. Both had large bottle- 
shaped creels woven of bamboo fibre. Incidentally, 
though very primitive in most things, they were both 
ultra-modern in one respect. Their hooks were barb- 
less. From time to time they would sharpen them on 
a small whetstone, much the way a trout-fisher dries 
his fly. They had just caught a couple of fish. Both 
had the trig lines of good swimmers but the fleshy 
underhung mouths of suckers. The natives said there 
were no other fish in the stream. 

Soon it became dark. As we stumbled along the 
trail there loomed a vague shape to our left which the 
clamor of some dogs stamped as a house. A dim light 
was glowing through a ramshackle door and from 
within came a droned chant, something like a sea- 
chantey, punctuated by thumps. Kermit and I 

164 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


stopped and saw, in the light of a half-dozen oil-dips, 
four men with a big tree-trunk which they were using 
as a battering-ram. They were driving wedges be- 
tween the ends of two heavy slabs of wood. When 
they saw us they stopped and smiled. We walked in 
and found they were operating a primitive press, with 
which they were squeezing out the mustard oil that is 
so generally used in this part of China. 

We finished our march by the pale light of a cres- 
cent moon,—the caravans straggling in some time 
later. We stopped at the house of the hunting head- 
man in Luchingsha. Right after our arrival he 
showed us a skin of the little panda,—fire-fox, as the 
Chinese call it because of its flaming red skin. 

On the trail we had discussed the best way to enlist 
the active aid of this head-man. We decided, guided 
largely by the advice of Hsuen, to promise him, if 
we got a giant panda, the .38-calibre pearl-handled 
Colt automatic that I carried. We asked him to come 
to the room where we slept, and first presented him 
with a mechanical flashlight. Then through Hswen 
we broached our plan. As soon as he saw the pistol 
his eyes lit greedily. When I showed him how quickly 
it could be drawn from an armpit holster, he became 
more excited. In the dim light of the lanterns his 
lean yellow face looked as fierce as that of a Tong 
hatchet-man. Several times while we explained the 
conditions governing the gift he abruptly got up, 

165 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


darted to the door, and looked through a crack to 
make sure no one was listening. By the time he left 
he had promised to get us native hunters and dogs, 
and to go with us himself. 

He then took us into the courtyard and showed us, 
by the light of an oil-dip, some horns nailed on either 
side of the door to his house. Two pairs were takin 
and the others serow. He said all four animals had 
been shot in the country to which he was going to 
take us. He asked us to wait a day at his house, while 
he gathered the best hunting-men from the neigh- 
boring villages and make other preparation. As we 
crawled into our bedding rolls we felt that the pros- 
pects for good hunting were much brighter. 

When we woke we saw our surroundings for the 
first time by daylight. We were in a small valley with 
rough mountains rising wall-like on every side. The 
house itself was a collection of buildings of brown 
hand-hewn lumber grouped around a flagged court- 
yard. The windows were of lattice-work partly cov- 
ered with tattered colored paper. At the main gate- 
way stood a wooden cage with a brown watch-dog in 
it. As the dog could not get out to attack robbers, and 
barked so continually at everything that no one paid 
any attention to him, it was hard to see what purpose 
he served. Just opposite the dog was a large manure 
pile like those in France, the smell of which was car- 
ried by the breeze into every corner of the buildings. 


166 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


Of course we were at once established as a side- 
show which charged no price of admission. Crowds of 
curious Orientals gathered around us. Some of the 
time they would talk, more often they would stand in 
solemn silence regarding us with rapt attention while 
we wrote, read, or ate. Two of our most constant at- 
tendants were little boys about three years old, clothed 
above the waist in parti-colored rags and from there 
- down in brown skin and dirt. 

During the morning the hunters and their dogs be- 
gan arriving. By afternoon some thirteen men had 
come, and we held a joint conference. They were a 
wild-looking group, mixed Chinese and Tibetan, as 
is most of the population of the valley. One that im- 
pressed us at once was a well-built, light-colored fel- 
low with a small tuft of about six hairs at the point of 
his chin. His name was Huan. He looked bright, 
but I for one distrusted his ability because he chat- 
tered so much. Another was a stocky, dark-skinned 
man with a goitre, and an astonishingly deep voice 
which contrasted strangely with the shrill Oriental 
tones of his fellows. 

Hsuen acted as spokesman for us and did excel- 
lently. We questioned them in detail about game, es- 
pecially the giant panda and the takin. It developed 
that the panda had been shot by only three of the 
thirteen and these three had shot only two all told be- 
tween the three of them. One had been killed ten and 


167 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


the other three years ago. None of the other men had 
even seen the animal. We asked how they hunted it 
and were told with dogs,—indeed, the last one killed 
had been treed. Though the cold facts of the case 
were not encouraging, the hunters were so enthusias- 
tic that we were infected and became more hopeful 
than we had been. Next we turned to takin. At first 
it seemed as if every man present was in the habit of 
going out once a week and killing a couple. Careful 
questioning, however, sadly reduced this until event- 
ually we decided that not more than half had either 
killed or even seen the animal. Again we were in- 
formed that the only way to get it was with dogs. 

We then told the natives that we wished to shoot 
the animals ourselves. This always surprises and 
grieves the jungli hunting-man. He has no particu- 
lar feeling of pride in getting in the first shot. What 
he wants is the game. Their grief was nothing to that 
of the head-man. It turned out that he always hunted 
by proxy, and that when he had told us he had killed 
animals he really meant his hunters had killed them 
for him. It had never occurred to him that we would 
do otherwise. To the best of his knowledge no impor- 
tant men did. He had thought we would camp com- 
fortably in a valley while the native hunters scattered 
over the mountains. He saw the chance of his reward 
diminishing, and lodged a violent protest. We were 
adamant, and finally issued a decree that none of the 
villagers with us should even carry their guns. 


168 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


In order to cheer them after this dictum we turned 
to the subject of rewards. We promised them, in ad- 
dition to their pay, double the price for which they 
could sell the skin of any animal we might kill. We 
also offered prizes in the shape of cheap field-glasses 
to the men who would first show us takin or panda, re- 
gardless of whether they were within range. 

Next the pack of hounds, nine in all, were paraded 
for our inspection. They were an odd assortment. 
The dominant type was medium sized, black in color 
with trimmings of brown, and long hair. One dog, 
however, was a rangy brown beast, and another was 
black and white, which made me think he might be in 
danger at dusk, for those are the markings of the 
panda. All of the dogs showed a tendency to begin 
the hunt by fighting each other on the spot. 

After this the men left to make ready their rations 
of cornmeal for the journey. We arranged te start 
early next day. 

Everything so far had raised our spirits, and we 
went to bed early to get a good night’s rest. In this 
we were reckoning without our host. Apparently it 
was spring-cleaning time in Muping, and spring- 
cleaning took the form of a religious ceremony. It 
being very evidently impossible to banish the dirt, 
they attempt at least to banish the evil spirits of 
winter. Three men officiated,—circuit-riding priests. 
Two of them clashed cymbals, the third chanted 
prayers. Each part of the house was visited in turn, 

169 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


and the ceremony repeated. Even the kitchen and 
the kitchen gods came in for attention. When I finally 
dropped off to sleep at midnight it was to the sound 
of the cymbals. 

The house hummed like a beehive as the prepara- 
tions for starting were completed. We had cut our 
kit to the bone. All we took beyond food and ammu- 
nition were our bedding-rolls, with a few extra clothes 
stuffed in them. By this time we had been on the 
trail for three months, and our clothes were torn and 
travel-stained. Kermit’s corduroy coat had been 
burnt by a spark from the fire, and patched in a ba- 
zaar with the brilliant yellow cloth used by the yellow 
lamas. Our beards had grown to formidable dimen- 
sions, and bristled out from our faces. Suydam said 
that Kermit and I looked like wild animals peering 
out of a bush. I did not realize how rough we seemed 
until Hsuen, on seeing me reading the Bible one day, 
asked in polite surprise, ‘““Are you Christian?” 

This day we were in fine fettle. It seemed that the 
end of our long quest was ahead. We passed a small 
shrine built of slabs of slate, a crude wooden image 
within of a tiger with a man standing on his hands on 
its back. We asked what it was and were told the god 
of hunting. We hoped he might smile on our efforts. 

Soon the trail became rougher and led into a gorge. 
The sides that towered steeply to a dizzy height were 
covered with a tangle of vegetation. Ferns draped 


170 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


the rocks. I counted a dozen different kinds of wild 
flowers,—blue, white, mauve, and pink. The only fa- 
miliar face among them was the violet. Streams fell 
in floating mist from the cliffs. Beneath, the river 
roared among the boulders. Though the day was well 
advanced the sun had not touched our path, and the 
air blew coldly off the water. The whole scene was so 
savage and enchanted that I half-thought I heard in 
the tumult of the waters “woman wailing for her 
demon lover.” 

Our path led over the flimsiest of branch platforms 
skirting a sheer cliff with the rapids fifty feet below. 
It is said these are never repaired unless some one 
breaks through and falls into the stream. 

By evening we reached a bit of partially level 
ground where a couple of houses formed a village. 
This Suydam was to make his base while Kermit 
and I went south into the hills. Here we spent the 
night. We put up our tents, for the houses were so 
verminous it was impossible to stay in them. There 
were some small patches of cultivation and two 
scrawny cows browsing on the hillside. The principal 
occupations of the natives seemed to be woodchop- 
ping and bee-keeping. Around both houses were bat- 
talions of hives made out of hollow tree-trunks, and 
the bees buzzed everywhere. 

Next morning we went up the valley, while Suy- 
dam started for the neighboring hills with his hunters. 

171 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


After we had scrambled through thick undergrowth 
for an hour, we came to a solitary lean-to of branches. 
Within was an old fellow carving solid wood ladles 
and scoops out of beech-wood from the hills. He told 
us that a few hours before while wandering in the jun- 
gle he had seen a troupe of monkeys. We knew that 
whatever they might be they would be either very rare 
or unknown. Perhaps they might be the golden 
monkey. 

We sent the porters ahead with the kit and, taking 
our two Kashmiris and a couple of natives, we plunged 
into the forest. The undergrowth was dense,—a tan- 
gle of vines and dead wood. The slope was steep. 
Soon we were in a bamboo jungle where the dust 
from the dried leaves choked us as we gasped for 
breath. For better than an hour we stumbled up- 
ward without seeing a thing. Then we reached a 
razor-backed ridge down which we threaded our way, 
peering from side to side through blanket-like foliage. 

It seemed an almost hopeless mission when a native 
suddenly shrieked with excitement and pointed to- 
ward the tree-tops. We could see nothing, but started 
scrambling ahead. Kermit was in front. When my 
eye caught motion among some branches to the right 
I stopped, and a second later glimpsed a yellow shape. 
It was impossible to get a shot with a rest, as the 
jungle was neck-high, so I whipped up my rifle and 
fired offhand. I was in luck, for the monkey fell 


172 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


crashing through the branches. It was a splendid 
dog-ape, with a mane of long, grayish-yellow hair 
down its back and the most brilliant orange on its 
belly. It was as big as an eight-year-old child. A sec- 
ond later I saw another and brought it down with two 
shots. Then Kermit started shooting just beyond me, 
and for a few minutes it sounded like a miniature 
battle as we fired at half-seen shapes flitting through 
the tree-tops. 

When we gathered and counted our trophies we 
found we had a beautiful group of nine, fine animals 
for mounting in the museum. What is more, they 
were the golden monkey, and these animals would 
form the only complete group in any museum in the 
world. 

Meanwhile we had heard some firing on the slope 
beneath us. We were much disturbed for we had ar- 
ranged to have all the natives leave their guns behind. 
When we reached the trail below we found that three 
of our hunters still had guns. Between them, and at 
the cost of about twelve shots, they had killed one 
monkey and caught a tiny new-born one. We gave 
them a lecture upon never shooting while with us. 
The little monkey we tried to keep alive, but it was 
too young and died within twenty-four hours. 

With our game we tramped a few miles more up 
the valley and pitched camp on some partially level 
ground. All we had besides our bedding-rolls was a 


173 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


very small single tent in which we could just lie side 
by side. The natives built themselves a shelter of 
branches by the side of a big boulder. 

We were much encouraged by our first day. The 
country was as wild and remote as we could wish. 
Our valley was cupped by heavily wooded hills lead- 
ing up to snow-covered mountains. All that worried 
us was that the jungle seemed very dense. 

We skinned the monkeys,—work at which I am not 
nearly as good as Kermit. Though our Kashmiris 
would not eat them the native hunters did, so we gave 
them all the carcasses. Then we had supper. It was 
cooked by Hsuen. As one of our cooks had been left 
with Jack Young and the other with Suydam, Hsuen 
had volunteered to undertake the work himself. Con- 
sidering the circumstances our meal was very good,— 
great heaping plates of rice and beans, with some 
rather tough, thoroughly fried chicken. Hsuen had 
proved better and better as the trip progressed. As 
an interpreter his value increased as we became able 
to understand and speak what passed with him for 
English. He was as tough as hickory and finished 
the longest day with no sign of fatigue. He would 
turn his hand to any work, and without embarrass- 
ment played the most astonishingly different réles, 
for he would sit as guest with us at the magistrate’s 
table and then a week later act as our cook. 

This evening as soon as we had finished eating we 

174 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


sent for the head man and the hunters. They came 
and squatted around the camp-fire. In the flicker- 
ing light with their tattered clothes they were a wild- 
looking lot. Hsuen dropped his réle of chef, resumed 
that of interpreter, and we started to develop our 
plans for next day. We wanted if possible to get our 
game without dogs. Conversations like this are 
lengthy. Kermit would say, “Hsuen, ask if they ever 
hunt takin without dogs.” Hsuen would turn to the 
Chinese and then would ensue ten minutes of what 
seemed like the most impassioned debate. All would 
talk at once, their tones rising shriller and shriller. 
Any attempt to interrupt would be as useless as try- 
ing to dam the Mississippi in flood. Finally there 
would come a pause. We would be all attention for 
what we expected would be a long and detailed ac- 
count. Hsuen would say, “Chinamen say no.” The 
final result of an hour or more talk was that we would 
go still-hunting in different directions and that the 
dogs would be left in camp. 

We both felt that if we saw a panda it would be 
but once. We therefore decided that if we were to- 
gether we should give up our old custom of matching 
for the first shot, and fire if possible simultaneously. 
We wanted to be full partners in the first panda, 
should the gods permit. 

Early next morning we started. My party was led 
by Huan, the man who had killed a panda ten years 


175 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


ago. After walking a couple of miles down the valley 
we turned into the mountains. At once we found our- 
selves in the densest jungle I have ever known. It 
was of bamboo six to eight feet tall, interspersed with 
hemlock and beech. The slope was forty-five degrees. 
We had to climb up on hands and knees. Deadfall 
blocked us every few feet. The dust from the dried 
bamboo leaves got into our lungs and eyes. The stems 
and matted vines through which we had to force our 
way tripped and clung to us like the tentacles of an 
octopus. The sweat ran in streams and caked the 
dust. It was impossible to see twenty feet. 

Soon we came on panda sign. It was old, but at 
least it indicated that the animal had been there. 
Then we came to snow and found the track of a takin. 

On we toiled. We passed much deer droppings 
and found an old serow’s horns lying on the ground. 
It was impossible to move quietly. We made a noise 
like a regiment of cavalry charging through a wheat 
field. We could see nothing unless we were almost 
able to touch it, while any animal could hear us hun- 
dreds of yards away. From time to time my head- 
man would climb a tree and gaze over the tops of the 
bamboo. What he hoped from such an action I could 
not imagine, as no game smaller than an elephant or 
rhinoceros could have been seen above the tall grass. 
I kept hoping I was being led to some clearing where 
a still-hunter would have some chance of sighting 
game and making a stalk. 


176 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


On we toiled for another couple of hours through 
the same country. Realizing then that we would cer- 
tainly spend most of the day in this type of jungle I 
explained by signs that I did not want the hunters 
following me in single file but spread out on the 
flanks. I hoped if they were there they might start 
some animal that would run near me in its fright. 

The sun was high overhead now, for it was noon. 
We had been going steadily for six hours. We had 
seen no water since early in the morning, and I had 
had nothing to drink since my tea at breakfast. I 
was parched with thirst and the handfuls of snow that 
I occasionally swallowed hardly helped. Higher and 
higher we worked until at last I caught a glimpse of 
an open grass slope above me. 

It was now three o’clock in the afternoon. At this 
moment the native hunters motioned that it was time 
to turn back to camp. I told them I wanted to go to 
the meadow. They protested, telling me by signs we 
would not be able to get back if we did. I was firm. 
It was the first place I had seen all day from where 
I could get any view. We pushed ahead and reached 
it a little before four. I was much disappointed for 
not only could I see no animals, though I searched 
the open places carefully, but I could find no tracks 
anywhere. Very much discouraged I turned toward 
camp. It was as rough a road as I have ever trav- 
elled. When we finally got off the mountain slope we 

177 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


struck the bed of a stream coated with ice and choked 
with boulders. I had worn shoes with crépe-rubber 
soles in order to walk softly. Every step I slipped. 
Every three steps I fell. We hurried as fast as we 
could, but it soon became dusk. My Kashmiri shi- 
kari, Gaffar Sheikh, was completely done in, and long 
before had given my rifle to a native hunter to carry, 
who took a header and bent the rear sight. 

The moon was up when I saw the camp-fire twin- 
kling. Of all welcome sights that I have seen, none 
was ever more welcome. Altogether it was the hard- 
est day’s hunting I had ever spent. Kermit, who had 
got back before me, had had much the same sort of 
time. We at once agreed that still-hunting in that 
country was impossible, and that next day we would 
go out with the dogs. 

The following morning we started off together with 
all the dogs and all the natives. We went to another 
place which differed merely in its location from those 
we had travelled the day before. Each hunter led 
his dog. Why those animals survived I do not see. 
They were dragged through the undergrowth as a 
plough is dragged through a furrow. I am sure that 
two or three times, by disentangling its hind legs 
from the clinging vines, I saved the dog just in front 
of me from being pulled in two. We soon came on 
panda sign in abundance, but all of it old. We found 
where one had furrowed the trunks of trees with its 


178 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


claws. We even found some coarse white hair caught 
in the bark of a hollow tree where one had slept. 

At this moment the dogs were loosed and disap- 
peared. We expected every moment to hear them 
pick up a trail. We did not. Occasionally we would 
hear a yelp, but it was generally one dog fighting an- 
other. All day long we scrambled up over the hill- 
sides. As one of the two pandas killed by our hunters 
had been found in a tree, our men studied carefully 
every tree that might by any chance be strong enough 
to hold the animal. Dusk was falling when we reached 
camp. 

We were both much discouraged and, after they 
had had something to eat, called the natives together 
for another conference. As we were sure we had seen 
reasonably fresh panda sign we wished to know why 
the dogs had not run his trail. To our surprise we 
were told the dogs would not trail a panda. Appar- 
ently it was simply a case of coming on it by chance, 
and then if the panda became frightened before the 
dogs it might climb a tree. 

This discouraged us a good deal. We asked more 
questions. Huan came forward with the suggestion 
that the panda he had killed ten years before had 
come near his camp after he had been cooking pig’s 
bones. He asked permission to send for some. Of 
course such a theory was the rankest folly, but it is 
always unwise to discourage natives when their sug- 

179 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


gestions are harmless. We told him to get the bones. 

It then developed that we had hunted on a “bad 
day,”—a day the hunting gods did not like. We 
found there was a trilogy of them. Uru Chang Yeh, 
the bareback rider on the tiger, was the most impor- 
tant. His second in command was Gao Chang Ta 
War, about whom we could find out little. The third 
was not a god, but a demi-god, a man who “died 
long while ago.” He was called Low Yih Wen, the 
king of the lofty mountains, and Orpheus-like, could 
call the animals from their hiding places. 

It was feared that these gods might not be well 
disposed toward us. A small temporary altar had 
been built by the men’s camp, and they were offering 
a hen. We said we would be glad to pay for the hen, 
or for a small goat or pig. We were told that the 
gods did not like pigs or goats, but that hens were 
acceptable. Incidentally the gods are not wasteful, 
for the chicken, after being killed in their honor, is 
eaten by their votaries. 

We were told that the dogs trailed takin, even if 
they would not trail panda, so we told the natives 
that next day we would make that animal our main 
objective. Again we started off with all the men and 
dogs. The day was merely a repetition of the days 
before. Never had I seen stiffer jungle. My stout 
Fox puttees were torn to rags. Again we saw panda 
sign. Once we came to fairly fresh takin tracks by a 


180 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


small muddy spring. We were puzzled when the dogs 
paid no attention to them. Evening found us back 
in camp, tired and discouraged. A note from Suydam 
told us that he was having the same experience. When 
we asked why the dogs did not trail, we were told they 
only did so “in the early morning and evening.” 

As the only value of the dogs was to trail, we left 
them and nearly all of the natives behind and spent 
next day trying to still-hunt. It was impossible. Do 
what we could it was impossible. Once we sent 
Mukta Loom, one of our trained shikaris, to look at 
a hollow tree near by. Going as carefully as he could 
he made a terrific noise. What was more, though we 
were standing on a tree-trunk we could not see him 
after he had gone twenty feet away. In a vain at- 
tempt to get where we could see something, we scram- 
bled to above the tree line. There in the coarse grass 
we found no game trails, and though we searched the 
surrounding country with our field-glasses we saw 
no sign of animals. 

The Kashmiris suggested that they spend the night 
there and watch the country in the early morning. If 
the game ever did come into the clearings to feed, that 
would be the time. If they did not, it would be pretty 
conclusive proof that game rarely if ever left the 
jungles, for both shikaris were trained men with high- 
power binoculars. 

It was long after dark when we stumbled into 


181 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


camp, and slept the heavy sleep of thorough exhaus- 
tion. 

The Kashmiris, when they came down next day, 
said they had not seen a thing. By this time our hopes 
had sunk very low. For a couple of days more we 
hunted with men and dogs for panda. Each day was 
a repetition of the former,—ceaseless struggling 
through dense jungle, without hearing or seeing any- 
thing, until we felt that the panda resembled the 
“snark” more than any living animal. The hunter 
who had gone for the pig’s bones returned with them. 
They were cooked with much ceremony and of course 
no results. We made up our minds that to stay longer 
would be a waste of time, and that it would be better 
for us to go to the Lolo country where we had heard 
there were takin. 

Before we definitely decided to go, however, we 
had the head-man and all the hunters assembled again. 
We thought possibly we might hear of some other 
place near by where we would at least have a fighting 
chance. Careful questioning brought forth nothing. 
All country was the same. Only three men among 
the thirteen native professional hunters had ever shot 
panda. The rest had never even seen one. No one 
they knew had shot panda. More illuminating still, 
we found what had been concealed from us,—we 
found that the takin was only shot in winter when 
the deep snow made it possible by trailing to over- 
take it. 

182 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


My opinion is that the giant panda is not very 
scarce in the Muping country. In the valleys we were 
hunting there may be four or five. But, on account of 
its habits and the nature of the country, it is the 
merest luck for a man to get a shot at one. 

Of course this does not mean that some one might 
not go there and kill one on his first day, but that 
same man might also hunt steadily for years without 
seeing one. It would be possible to get them with a 
well-trained pack of bear-hounds, but no dogs we 
saw or heard of in the country are competent. The 
pack would have to be brought from the United 
States. 

Personally I am sure the panda is not a bear. It 
does not hibernate in Muping, whereas the black bear 
does. All testimony of the natives was to this effect, 
and our own observations confirmed what they said. 
We hunted in March when the bear was sleeping. At 
least three times we saw traces of panda not many 
hours old. Besides these we saw quantities of signs 
ranging from a month to a few days in age. It is 
possible the panda may hibernate in other places, but 
I do not believe it likely. I think the mistake made 
in this respect by certain authors is due to the fact 
that natives whom they have asked have mistaken the 
animal. At Tatsienlu the hunters brought us a red 
bearskin saying it was panda. 

The Chinese name for the panda is Bei Shung, or 

183 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


white bear. It lives in Muping in the dense bamboo 
jungles on the steep hillside. We could not find, from 
questioning the natives or from the signs, that it ever 
left them. Its diet is in main bamboo, but as it does 
not hibernate it cannot confine itself simply to the 
new shoots as some state. The droppings we exam- 
ined, which were numerous, were oval and from four 
to seven inches in length and two to three inches thick. 
They were composed, as far as we could see, of bam- 
boo leaves and stalks only slightly digested. It sleeps 
in hollow trees or beds down in the bamboo, evidently 
turning around like a dog for the stalks were twisted 
into the semblance of a nest. We found a number of 
places of this kind with panda hairs attached to 
branches and twigs. It has, as I have said, the bear’s 
habit of measuring itself against a tree and scoring 
the bark with its claws. The natives testified that it 
climbed trees. They also said that it went singly ex- 
cept for mother and young, or in spring, when the 
male and female are supposed to stay together but a 
few days. At this latter time it is said to roar, but 
we never heard it. 

So much for the panda. It was with regret we 
turned our backs on it. May the fates give us the 
chance to match our wits against it again before the 
windows be darkened. Once having made up our 
minds we acted quickly, for we wished to get to the 
Lolo country as rapidly as possible. We sent a note 


184 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


to Suydam telling him of our plans, and arranged to 
break camp early next morning for a double march 
to Luchinsha. 

We had hunted for six days as hard as we could 
from daylight to dark. We had had fourteen native 
hunters with us, besides our trained Kashmiri shi- 
karis. During the entire time not one of the party 
had seen a wild animal of any sort. Our disappoint- 

-ment was tempered by the fact that a runner came in 
from Yachow bringing cables,—the first news I had 
of my family for more than three months. Even at 
that it was not all it may seem. It takes a cross be- 
tween Sergeant Cuff and Le Clerc to decipher an 
English telegram sent over Chinese lines. The fol- 
lowing is an exact copy of one I received at this time: 

“State Chenry Stimson Treasury melmon wsa 
assd justice william Mitchell mines sota pestmaster 
Welter browa navy Charles francis Adsmo Massa- 
chus Ctts interior ray Wilbur agriculture Cyde Mis- 
souri Lalar davis Commecr nnawnon Ced.” 

The translation is this: “State Henry Stimson 
Treasury Mellon War Good Justice William Mitch- 
ell Minnesota Postmaster Walter Brown Navy 
Charles Francis Adams Massachusetts Interior Ray 
Wilbur Agriculture Hyde Missouri Labor Davis 
Commerce unannounced.” It was the news of Presi- 
dent Hoover’s new Cabinet. 

When we arrived at Luchinsha we found Jack 


185 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Young waiting for us. We had sent ahead for porters 
to take us to Yachow, and had told the head-man 
that if he would get good men we would give him the 
pistol, even though he had not got us a shot at either 
takin or panda. All evening we spent paying off 
hunters and rearranging our kit. The head-man as 
an appreciation of our presents brought us as a gift 
the skin of one of the two panda shot by his men, 
and also a takin and black bearskin. 

Next morning we were on the road again, retrac- 
ing our steps to Muping. It was astonishing what a 
difference the eight days that had passed made in the 
valley. When we had trekked up spring was hesitat- 
ing on the threshold. Now it had flooded in. The 
trees were in leaf. Insects droned over the grain 
fields. Butterflies hovered over the flowers that had 
miraculously sprung into being where but a few days 
before there was little hint of their advent. Our heavy 
woollen clothes were a burden and we thought long- 
ingly of our cool shorts in our boxes at Yachow. 

When we arrived at Muping the friendly little 
Magistrate came around to call. While we were away 
he had sent messages to the headmen of the various 
villages asking for panda skins for us. As a result 
we got two, one shot ten, the other two years before. 

Next morning as we passed out of Muli we came 
to a temple of the old régime which I had noticed on 
the way up. As I wandered through it I saw in a 


186 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


corner a couple of beautifully carved wooden gods. 
It occurred to me I might be able to buy them as 
they looked neglected. I called Hsuen and told him 
what I wanted. At once he assumed an owl-like look 
of solemnity and called the head man of our caravan, 
who was Huan, our ex-hunter. Together they chat- 
tered in a corner and then both went to see the priest. 
In a short time they were back and whispered to me 
that I could have them at a cost of three dollars, 
which I paid. Then, with as much stealth as if they 
were robbers, they packed them in a basket. Next 
Huan stripped to the waist and wrapped his blue 
cotton coat around the basket so that no one could 
see them. I asked Hsuen why, and he replied enig- 
matically, “Perhaps trouble.” Of course there was 
no trouble. When our Kashmiri servants saw them 
they were much interested. “Kali Bhutnis” they 
called them, or “Black female devils.” 

The Kashmiris are always amusing in their outlook 
on other nations. Being good Mohammedans they 
regard pig-flesh with horror. They confided to us 
once that one could expect nothing of a nation of 
Hindoos, i. e., the Chinese who ate pig. Early on this 
expedition they enquired for “Jori Sahib ka Kah- 
wah,”—literally, George the Gentleman’s coffee. It 
took some time before we realized they were asking 
about George Washington coffee. 

The following day I went into a very large build- 

187 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


ing which was called the Temple of Hell, because it 
showed sinners in torments. I was puzzled at first by 
the buzzing of a myriad of bees. Suddenly I noticed 
that these insects had made holes through the outer 
clay of the images and were hiving in the straw in- 
side. The Chinese of this temple will find honey, not 
in a lion’s carcass like Samson, but in the bodies of 
their gods. Most of the statues I had seen had their 
mouths smeared with some black substance so that 
they looked like children who had been at the treacle 
pot. I asked Hsuen, who was with me, what this 
was and found that it was opium. Devotees to the 
drug were giving the gods their share. 

Another thing that had puzzled me was the ex- 
traordinary dilapidation of many of the deities, who 
lacked heads or arms like a careless child’s doll. When 
Hsuen was questioned on this his eyes twinkled and 
he said, “People ask god for something. Kill chicken 
for him. God not do it. Break his arm off.” : 

On the march that day we passed through country 
where there had been bitter fighting between bandits 
and the local troops. The result had been inconclu- 
sive and the hill towns were still held by the outlaws. 
In the towns in every prominent place placards were 
posted dealing with the treatment of these robbers. 
I had one translated and was given this Chinese bull: 
“All proved bandits must have their heads cut off 
without trial.” Jack Young, when he stopped at 


188 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


Yachow on the way up, saw two executed. It is a 
sad commentary on conditions that at the same time 
three high-school boys, ranging from sixteen to nine- 
teen, were shot because they had been making speeches 
against the fiscal exactions of the local soldiery, levied 
under the guise of anticipated taxes. 

When we started through this no-man’s-land I was 
walking at the head of the column. Two shots were 
fired far away in the hills. The porters behind came 
up on me hike a stampede of buffalo. It was remark- 
able to see the way they ran over the rough ground 
carrying a seventy-pound pack. Our road led 
through a beautiful gorge. In the river below I no- 
ticed several cormorants fishing. Just before we 
reached the plain one of the shikaris saw five ghoral 
feeding on the crest of a near-by mountain. We 
studied them through our glasses for a while, but 
they were little animals and it did not seem worth the 
loss of a day to stop and hunt them. 

That evening we reached the city of Lushan. Now 
abruptly we had changed from a rough wild country 
to one of the earliest settled parts of China. Long 
before the seacoast was conquered the nation was 
established in eastern Szechuan. During the Chinese 
Age of Chivalry, the time of the three kingdoms, 
Szechuan played an important part. It was the last 
stronghold of the Han dynasty. It produced Gen- 
eral Kuan Pu, as far as I know the only Chinese sol- 


189 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


dier who has been deified. We saw many temples to 
him along the road. He is always represented as wav- 
ing a sword. To him one tradition ascribes the nam- 
ing of Tatsienlu (The Place of Making Arrows), 
for it was there he halted his troops on one of his 
campaigns. 

Lushan is a very old city. A mossgrown wall sur- 
rounds it, which is supposed to have been built under 
the Hans and repaired under the Mings. It still 
serves its purpose, for we were told that not long be- 
fore it helped in the repulse of bandits. The pave- 
ments are good but rutted and worn by myriads of 
bare feet. Beyond the town is an inscribed sand- 
stone slab dating from the Hans. 

Next day was warm and sunny. The country was 
just what I, as a boy, had always imagined China. 
Valley and hillside were cultivated. The greens of 
the various crops formed a patchwork of color. 
Enormous wooden water-wheels for irrigation were 
slowly turning in the river. Fruit trees were masses 
of blossoms. There were delicate mauve lilies bloom- 
ing by the roadside. Every little while we passed 
memorial arches or tablets beautifully carved from 
red sandstone. Even in the tiny farming villages 
there were often delicately carved stone ornaments 
on either side of the doors. To add a final touch we 
passed a number of graceful white pagodas silhou- 
etted against the sky. 


190 


PERE DAVID’S COUNTRY 


The river broadened and turned from a turbulent 
mountain brook into a stream with deep still stretches. 
On it floated bamboo rafts and sampans. Toward 
the end of the march we crossed it in a heavy board 
scow filled with tea coolies, soldiers, farmers, and 
small merchants, who showed a most lively and 
friendly interest in us. A few lis beyond we came to 
the walled city of Yachow. We walked up broad 
well-paved streets, through the most active bazaar 
we had seen, until we found the Baptist Mission and 
Hospital, where Doctor Crook and his associates wel- 
comed us in the most friendly of fashions. Mail and 
hot baths were waiting, and to travellers in strange 
places such a combination comes close to forming that 
“perfect bliss” of which the hymn speaks. 


191 


CHAPTER VIII 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


Yacuow proved the best and largest bazaar that 
we had encountered since setting out from Burma. 
Mr. Lovegreen, of the Baptist Mission, although 
now a resident of Kiating, had formerly put in a 
number of years at the mission here, so he kindly 
offered to pilot me around. Everywhere we went he 
was sure to encounter some acquaintance of former 
days. We first made some purchases for the kitchen 
and bought two Dietz lanterns to replace ones that 
had been stolen. Throughout the Orient the Dietz 
lantern, of New York manufacture, is everywhere in 
evidence, and, particularly in out of the way places, 
gives one a feeling of home, almost what in Portu- 
guese would be called “saudade da terra.” 

Along the main street were a number of shops 
where opium was being distilled, and the pungent 
and not to be forgotten odor filled the air. It is said 
that the men who do this work become, from con- 
stantly inhaling the fumes, among the most enslaved 
addicts of the drug. 

In a small grocery-store I came upon two fly- 
blown bottles of a French vin mousseux. They were 


192 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


the only two bottles of wine in town, and how they 
ever found their way up along the weary stages from 
some ocean port remained unexplained. We, how- 
ever, thoroughly appreciated their presence. 

The Chinese are proverbially incorrigible gamblers. 
A stroll through the bazaar of any town is sure to 
reveal one or more games in full swing. In Likiang 
an old man set up his table on one of the bridges, and 
spread out a cloth with a set of dominoes printed on 
it; any one wishing to play put a coin over the domino 
he chose, and the old man shook three dice in a bowl. 
If the combination of numbers on the dice could be 
made to conform with those of the domino chosen, 
the player won the equivalent of his stake. A form 
of dice-throwing that I saw in Tsalo resembled our 
native game of craps; and the players encouraged 
their dice to turn up the proper numbers as eagerly 
and fluently as any Georgia negro. 

Dominoes were much in vogue; sometimes the 
pieces were of buffalo horn, or of ivory, but more 
often they were of glazed pasteboard. The game was 
always played for money. Mah-jong was by no 
means uncommon. I first saw it played at Talifu 
and afterward in many towns. Usually there were 
stray pieces to be seen among the wares of the junk 
dealers. Chess was the only game I saw played with- 
out money passing hands. You did not often see a 
game in progress in the streets, although in Tatsien- 


193 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


lu, Muping, and Yachow I watched keen contests 
taking place. In the last-named town I got a fine 
set of old ivory chessmen, of the round checquer- 
like type, with the names of the pieces carved upon 
them. 

At Yachow we left with our friends at the Mission 
a few volumes from our library, among them Wal- 
den, Jane Eyre, The White Company, and Great 
Expectations—an oddly assorted group but typical 
of the whole. Our reading matter varied greatly in 
nature, and because of that filled its niche well. 
With a few exceptions we brought only those books 
that could be found in the Everyman’s Library or in 
volumes of no greater bulk. “Revolt in the Desert” 
was an exception, but when we had read or reread it 
we found a welcome home for it with Doctor Hanna. 
Doughty’s “Arabia Deserta,” which we had in the 
thin-paper single-volume edition, although larger 
than we could have wished, was still not altogether 
too bulky. Well fortified with literature dealing with 
Arabia, we also had Burton’s “Pilgrimage to Medina 
and Mecca.” The introduction to this last had been 
contributed by Stanley Lane Poole, and I was 
amused at a lapse he made, clearly showing that he 
would have done better to have refreshed his memory 
of the text before undertaking the task. In referring 
to certain apocryphal accounts of Burton’s adven- 
tures on the pilgrimage he brands them as impossible 

194 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


because he states that Burton never carried firearms. 
Throughout both volumes there are many references 
to a particular pair of pistols which the pilgrim usu- 
ally kept under his pillow at night! 

Among the other volumes which we read and left 
behind were “Pride and Prejudice,” “Sense and Sen- 
sibility,” ‘The Moonstone,” ‘Green Mansions,” 
“Omoo,” and “The Old Curiosity Shop.” My edi- 
tion, in two volumes, of Abbé Huc’s “Voyage dans 
la Tartarie” was too old a friend and companion to 
discard after rereading. The volumes left behind 
were all bought merely to serve their purpose on the 
trip. 

We regretted that our stay in Yachow must be 
so brief, but every day counted if we were to reach 
Indo-China in time to collect certain of the big game 
that we would no longer be able to obtain once the 
rainy season set in. We were now well down in alti- 
tude, and spring was far advanced. On either hand 
stretched the familiar terraced paddy fields with 
water buffalo ploughing through them, belly deep in 
liquid mud. A single small grove of plantains added 
a tropical touch. Long trains of coolies passed us 
with loads of stag horns. The antlers were coming 
from Tibet by way of Tatsienlu. Many were evi- 
dently pick ups that had been cast, but some had been 
cut from the skull. Horns are highly valued by the 
Chinese for medicine when they are in the velvet. 


195 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Our first march lasted until aften seven at night, 
for we had got off to a late start, as was invariably 
the case when we passed through a good-sized town, 
and doubly so when we had to change our caravan. 
Indeed it was only due to indefatigable work on Ted’s 
part and the very efficient aid of Doctor Crook that 
we got off at all on the 30th. 

Next day Hsuen proudly produced two small 
birds in a cage which he said that he had bought be- 
cause they sang so pleasantly in the morning, and 
would awaken us with their music. The birds remain- 
ing silent, Hsuen shook the cage, when, evidently 
realizing what was expected of them, they burst into 
song. 

We passed many roadside shrines and noticed that 
the wayfarers did not fail to kowtow in front of them, 
repeating a prayer the while. The Gods and God- 
desses were newly furbished up; some had an entire 
fresh coat of paint, and offerings of grain were laid 
in front of them. This was all evidently due to the 
delay of the early rains. For this same reason we had 
been unable to purchase meat in Yachow, it being 
considered unlucky to pursue the butcher’s trade while 
waiting for the fulfillment of these petitions. 

We passed through Jungking, a bustling town 
built along the banks of a river. Iron mines in 
the close vicinity gave importance to the town and 
explained the quantity of forges and the roughly 

196 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


fashioned pots and implements of cast iron. The main 
street was thronged with merchants and purchasers, 
and the ancient temples attested to the antiquity of 
the place. 

The prayers for rain were answered, for it poured 
all night, and on the morning of April 1st it was still 
raining hard when we set off to climb over a ten-thou- 
sand-foot pass. Four and a half hours brought us to 
the summit. The rain had degenerated into a heavy 
Scotch mist, at times so dense that it was impossible 
to see further than a few yards. Snow lay on the 
trail, and the tea coolies bound for Tatsienlu were 
making heavy weather. They thronged the rude way- 
side hostelries which we passed from time to time. 

Descending the further side we passed two palan- 
quins halted before an inn. One only was occupied 
and its occupant was obviously much wrought up, but 
we were unable to understand his troubles until we 
later met him at the Magistrate’s at Tsingkihsien, 
and learned that his companion had been plucked by 
bandits from the other palanquin. They had carried 
him and all his belongings off into their mountain 
haunts. All this had occurred only a few hours be- 
fore we passed, so we considered ourselves fortunate. 
We had received no warning of bandit activities and 
were straggling along in the rain and mist in no very 
good form to protect our goods. The eager questions 
with which we were hailed while coming down the 


197 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


trail into town, and which had been completely un- 
intelligible to us, were now explained. We never 
learned what success the disconsolate Chinaman met 
with in retrieving his friend. 

Tsingkihsien must have once been a town of con- 
siderable importance. Seen from the mountainside 
it resembles one of the medieval walled towns of 
France. It has sadly shrunken within its walls, and 
even the main street, descending through the centre 
with steep stone steps, shows many vacant shops and 
houses. We were fortunate in locating a fairly clean 
serai, but the supreme difficulty is the finding of a 
room unaffected by the close proximity of primitive 
toilet facilities. 

Hsuen sadly displayed a cage containing the 
corpses of his two small birds. The cold and sleet en- 
countered in crossing the pass had proven too much 
for the songsters. 

The main road from Yachow to Tatsienlu turned 
off here, striking directly north, while we continued 
our southerly course. Hereafter we saw no further 
caravans of tea coolies, but the trail was thronged 
with laborers and merchants. Since leaving Yachow 
we had met with several lots of lightly built Lolo 
hunting-dogs; one man with as many as a dozen on 
leash. We noticed that there were no bitches, but 
were unable to learn whether this was because the 
Lolos sold only dogs so that the market would con- 


198 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


tinue. One of the owners told us that he was taking 
his batch of dogs to sell in Chengtu, the capital of 
Szechuan. Some of them looked only half grown, 
but he assured us that all were thoroughly trained for 
hunting. 

Gates and archways are often erected as a memorial 
to some influential personage. Many are handsome, 
but one under which we passed that morning stands 
out far above any that I have ever seen. I was un- 
able to learn its age, but it had unquestionably passed 
the century mark, and the stone had mellowed with 
the years. It was in perfect repair; on its graceful 
columns were carved in bas relief vivid scenes bu- 
colic and martial, interwoven with allegories and his- 
tories. 

The poppy fields were a mass of color, some solid 
white, others purple, others red, and still others a 
combination pink and white. Stretching out on either 
side of the road, and smiling in the sun, it was not 
easy to recall the sinister purpose hidden beneath 
their beauty. They had usurped the fields where corn 
and beans had previously thriven, and in time of scant 
crops they brought on famine. Even under the best 
of circumstances they would cause untold misery and 
suffering. Doctor Crook told us that many opium 
addicts came to him to be cured, undergoing the dras- 
tic treatment necessary. When I was looking through 
his hospital I heard the delirious ravings of one such 


199 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


wretch. Doctor Crook hesitated to hazard even a 
rough guess as to the percentage of cases in which the 
cure proved permanent. 

On April 2d we reached Fulin, a busy market- 
town, with more than its share of filth and smells. 
We lodged in a peculiarly dirty serai. We had bought 
a cheap European type saddle in Bhamo for the shi- 
karies, but found that either their method of riding 
or something in the cut of the saddle made it pecu- 
liarly fatal to the mules’ backs. It now served a good 
purpose, for we sent it as a much-appreciated gift to 
the militia officer in charge of the town. From him 
we hoped to secure advice and assistance, for we had 
here to leave the main road and turn off to Tzetati, 
two days distant up the Tung River. 

In spite of everything, the Yachow muleteers and 
some replacements we here arranged for all indulged 
in an orgy of opium and feasting which made our 
morning start a difficult and dilatory affair. In the 
face of our representations and warnings they wished 
to avoid the penalties of a late start by halting at a 
town only twelve miles out, thereby adding a day to 
the usual two-days’ journey to Tzetati. We forced 
them to go on and reached a small hamlet after dark 
on a rainy overcast night. The true rainy season does 
not commence until May, but we encountered an 
ominous number of itinerant umbrella repairers. 
These peddlers wander about the country with a 


200 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


quantity of defunct umbrellas, and will either repair 
one of these or mend your own for you. They ply a 
busy trade, for the Chinaman is very partial to the 
use of an umbrella, and they seem as necessary a part 
of his equipment as they are of that of the London 
business man. 

Next day a twenty-five-mile march took us to Tze- 
tati. Our muleteers being accustomed only to well- 
travelled trails found difficulty in places which such 
men as Luzon or Aju would have regarded as plain 
sailing. The valley was well cultivated, and there 
were many orchards of mulberry trees, for the silk- 
worm plays an important part here. The pan trees 
were numerous, and petals of their white and yellow 
flowers carpeted the fields. From the nut they pro- 
duce is derived one of the components that goes to 
make up the betel-nut chewer’s equipment. 

The head man at Tzetati should have been in a po- 
sition to supply all the information we wished about 
his district, for instead of, as is usual, coming from a 
distant part of the province, he represented the fourth 
generation to be born in the town. His knowledge, 
however, proved scanty, and he could only tell us 
that if we wished to hunt takin we could probably 
secure better information at Tsalo, a little Chinese 
town on the borders of the Lolo country. The road 
beyond, he added, had been closed to travel for many 
years, because of the uncertain temper of the inhabi- 


201 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


tants. He had never heard of the giant panda, and 
was positive that none existed in his country. 

We decided to split up, Ted and I going into the 
Lolo country to see whether we could come across 
panda or takin, while Suydam and Jack took a short 
cut back to the main road to Ningyuan, where we 
agreed on a rendezvous for the 16th of the month. 
This would give Cutting a chance to put in four or 
five days hunting in what was reported to be an ex- 
cellent game country. 

While we were busy making the division of kit, 
Suydam drew from his bedding roll one of the sau- 
sage-like Tibetan money-bags we had bought in Ta- 
tsienlu. It was unusually strongly impregnated with 
rancid yak butter and Cutting remarked he would be 
glad to have it no longer in his bedding, referring in no 
uncertain terms to its odor. Whereupon, Hsuen, who 
was standing by, instantly took up the cudgels for his 
fatherland. “That good smell, that like my brother.” 

Our first day’s march was to take us to Tsalo, but 
on the road a recurrence of the curious poisoning 
which I had first had in Yungchang obliged us to 
take time out, so we only reached a little hamlet 
called Sinchang. Here we were pleasantly sur- 
prised to find two French priests from the Catholic 
Mission at Fulin, Fathers Bocat and Flahntez. The 
former has spent eighteen years in China, the latter 
is but newly arrived. They were on a tour of inspec- 
tion of their native Catholic congregations. 


202 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


We had with us a flask of Benedictine which we 
had brought to drink with local headmen when open- 
ing relations. It was a genuine pleasure to share 
some of it with these French priests, to whom it could 
recall pleasant evenings in their far-distant France, 
a land which neither would in all probability revisit, 
for these Fathers come out to dedicate their lives to 
their work, and renounce all thought of ever re- 
turning. 

They both gave rather depressing accounts of the 
Lolo country, saying that it was greatly disturbed, 
and that they never travelled there and did not con- 
sider it safe. One of their Chinese priests had been 
captured not many months since on the borderland 
of the very country toward which we were headed, 
and his rescue had only been brought about after 
many difficulties and much negotiating. We assured 
them that we would take every precaution before en- 
tering the uncertain territory. Neither could give us 
any encouragement about the existence of the giant 
panda, and one of us sadly quoted, 


“Till instead of the Kalends of Greece, men said, 
When Crook and his darlings come back with the head!” 


We had not been long on the road before one of 
our mules Jost his footing and went spinning down 
the mountainside. Fortunately he brought up in a 
clump of thick bushes before any serious harm had 
been done, and half an hour later we had him back on 


203 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


the trail. By native wireless we learned that Suy- 
dam’s caravan had met with a similar accident, but 
with a less fortunate outcome. The mule had rolled 
down into a stream. It was killed by the fall, but the 
load was salvaged. Throughout this trip we had 
found the “native wireless” very deficient, often learn- 
ing nothing of people and events about which in 
Africa or Mesopotamia I would have been informed 
with speedy certitude by that unexplained method by 
which in the negro quarters in the South the results 
of the battles of the War of the Rebellion were known 
long before their masters in the Big House had heard. 


“The wind from the brier-patch brought him news 
That never went walking in white men’s shoes 
And the grapevine whispered its message faster 
Than a horse could gallop across a grave, 

Till, long ere the letter could tell the master, 
The doomsday rabbits had told the slave.”* 


We passed through several Lolo hamlets, and in 
one of them picked a fine-looking Lolo, Mooka, as a 
guide. He belonged to the serf class and we had to 
obtain the consent of his master. Slavery is still in 
full force among the Lolos, but there is not the rela- 
tionship between master and man that we commonly 
associate with the condition. Instead, there is a 
friendly companionship, and the sharing of food- 
bowl and wine-cup is often seen. 


*«John Brown’s Body.” Benet. 


204 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


At one village in which we made enquiries we met 
with a rather cold reception. Our questions regard- 
ing game were parried with demands to be told what 
were in our loads and how many rifles we had with us. 

The little Chinese outpost of Tsalo lies in a fertile 
valley among fields of corn and wheat and poppies. 
The head man was busy in his fields gathering poppy 
pods. He was evidently an opium addict. He knew 
little or nothing of the country ahead, although he 
said he understood that takin were to be found in the 
neighborhood of a Lolo village a day’s march distant. 
Of the panda he could give us no word. 

We were unfortunate in our choice of a lodging, 
for no sooner had we crawled into our bedding rolls 
than we heard the moaning of several sick people, and 
Hsuen came in to ask if we had any medicine for 
small-pox. He explained that there was an epidemic 
in the village and that in the next and only other 
room there was a poor woman who had lost one child 
and was now caring for her baby who was down with 
the disease. It is indeed distressing to hear the plaints 
of a sick child whom one is unable to help. There are 
times when any traveller, called upon for help in a 
hopeless case, may give a harmless pill in the hopes it 
may cause a fictitious relief or comfort, but with a 
dying infant such a procedure seems only heartless. 
A wretched woman in the next house and separated 
only by a thin partition was moaning and calling out 


205 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


in her delirium. The smell which drifted in from the 
courtyard beggared description. To add to Ted’s dis- 
comforts he was suffering from the effects of bad 
food or water. 

Altogether, when morning came we were more than 
ready for an early start. The Chinese headman had 
asked us to write him, taking any responsibility for 
our journey upon our own shoulders. We were afraid 
that to do so might cause him to pass on the word that 
we were any one’s prey; we therefore refused, telling 
him that while we didn’t require an escort, he might 
send one if he wished. Eleven militia turned up; two 
returned after accompanying us five or six miles, but 
the remainder proved a good lot, always ready to 
help or do any little odd job when called upon. We 
gave each man fifteen cents wine money a day. Just 
as we were leaving a man came along with a pony he 
wished to sell; an upstanding little bay, a four-year- 
old. We bought it for nine dollars, 

Three hours out Hsuen, who was indefatigable in 
his wayside enquiries for news of game, came up in 
great excitement. Only last night, he said, a man had 
come to a house we had just passed asking the loan 
of a rifle to protect the beehives of his village from 
the raids of a beishung, or white bear, the native name 
for the panda. It appeared that a month ago this 
animal had paid a visit to the apiary, and he had 
wounded it. Now it had returned. Unable to bor- 

206 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


row the rifle the villager had only just left to return 
to his house. Having been unable to secure even the 
vaguest news of the beast since leaving Muping, we 
had almost abandoned hope that there were any bei- 
shung in the country. This, therefore, certainly re- 
quired looking into, and after verifying the report we 
made arrangements to follow up the trail. 

We continued along for a couple of hours to a few 
houses known as Kooing Ma. We found that name 
usually referred to a group of hamlets scattered about 
within a radius of several miles. Here we got in 
touch with the local head man and converted suspicion 
into friendship through the presentation of an axe 
and a Homburg hat, followed up with a leisurely 
gossip over a glass of Benedictine. It was decided 
that we should leave our mules here, for porters only 
could negotiate the trail to the panda village. Ten 
villagers were drafted in to depart early in the morn- 
ing. 

We had gathered unto us at Tsalo a nondescript 
and rather shady appearing individual whom Hsuen 
explained to us as a “brother-man.” He was really 
a sub species of the genus entrepreneur, so common 
throughout China. They live from the proceeds of 
acting as intermediary in dealings of every descrip- 
tion. This particular “brother-man” was a Lolo who 
lived among the Chinese, and, it was suspected, added 
to his liaison work the distasteful post of spy. At 


207 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Kooing Ma he was on his mettle to secure informa- 
tion of all sorts for us, and the first bit of news he pro- 
duced was to the effect that the Lolos regarded the 
giant panda as a supernatural being, a sort of demi- 
god. This was discouraging, particularly since he 
added that he did not believe that they would take us 
hunting, for he was certain that the villagers them- 
selves never attempted to kill a panda, except in de- 
fense of their beehives, and even in that case they 
tried only to wound the marauder and frighten it off. 

This situation was not as disheartening as it might 
have been, for I had already had experience among 
natives, who, while owing to religious scruples unwill- 
ing themselves to kill a particular animal, were ready 
enough to aid a foreign hunter. We determined to 
approach the subject cautiously and to carefully 
avoid offending any native sensibilities. 

We were quartered in a clean native hut, filled with 
enormous bowls of grain. The sides were made of 
split bamboo and the roof was tiled with pine slabs, 
which failed to make it completely waterproof to the 
heavy rain that fell during the night. At half-past 
three we were awakened by a rooster that thought he 
had discovered daylight and would not let the matter 
drop. He seemed to be sharing our hut, so Ted 
crawled out of his blankets and after a short search 
announced he had found a hen in a basket sitting on 
some eggs. I protested that a hen could not crow, 

208 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


but he disregarded my protest, and quoting the line 
about “crowing hens” to bear him out, he deposited 
the vainly complaining hen, basket and all, outside in 
the rain. All was quiet for a time, and then the crow- 
ing recommenced. Both of us hopped up, and our 
search was rewarded by discovering two roosters hid- 
den away behind a mountainous pile of baskets. With 
my stick I rooted them out, while Ted directed them 
through the door, expediting their passage. Once 
more in our blankets we prepared for the few remain- 
ing hours of sleep, when a mournful but none the less 
loud crowing announced that the disconsolate fowls 
had taken refuge under the eaves within a few feet 
of Ted’s head. There was nothing further to be done. 

In the morning we gathered eleven villagers to 
serve as porters. Unaccustomed to the work, they 
could only carry very light ioads, and even so would 
go but a short distance before sitting down for a 
rest and a smoke. All the Lolos we met were invet- 
erate smokers. They hollowed pipes out of stones; 
some were white, others green, and many were 
made from a red stone which closely resembled the 
red pipestone used by the American Indian. I 
bought specimens of the different sorts, and once was 
the innocent cause of what threatened to be a riot. A 
woman had brought me a rather well-made green pipe 
with a brass stem; I had agreed to purchase it, when 
a man pounced upon it, and started haranguing the 


209 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


woman. The excitement became general, each party 
gathering vociferous adherents. It was some time 
before things were sufficiently quieted down for me 
to learn that the man claimed that the pipe had been 
stolen from him. At length it was sifted down to the 
statement that he had lost the pipe a year ago on the 
trail, and the woman had found it. Finders were 
adjudged keepers, and the sale was concluded. 

The distance from Kooing Ma to Kooing Hai is 
but a short one as the crow flies, but the trail wound 
round and about, and there were rivers to cross and 
steep ravines to negotiate, so that it was not until two 
in the afternoon that we came to the first of the group 
of houses that go to make up Kooing Hai. We had 
taken four soldiers, leaving the balance with the 
mules. We also had Mooka and the son of a neigh- 
boring head man. 

The mountainsides were glorious with rhododen- 
drons, varying in hue from deep purple to white. 
Pink was the prevailing color. In places the ground 
was sprinkled with a small blue member of the orchid 
family. Blue lilies, forget-me-nots, primroses, and a 
diminutive yellow flower dotted the path side. We 
were climbing up a valley, and as the altitude in- 
creased the crops were noticeably less advanced. 

At moments it did not look as if we would meet 
with a friendly reception. As we reached the hamlet 
farthest up the valley, designated as our halting 


210 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


place, the inhabitants took to the mountainside, and 
one man was very visible levelling a rifle at us. Our 
conductors succeeded in smoothing things out, but 
only with a good deal of difficulty, and it was not a
