friendly appearing delegation that dropped down to 
talk things over with us. I think it was the Chinese 
soldiers that were largely responsible for the distrust. 
Hsuen told us that among the Lolos we were usually 
taken for what he translated as “holy fathers,” as 
they call Catholic priests. This honor was due to our 
beards. 

Throughout the expedition we found that it was 
impossible to tell what sort of gift would prove a suc- 
cess. The knives and axes which had been a flat fail- 
ure in Muping were now of the greatest service, and 
went a long way to clear the horizon. On the other 
hand, the cheap field-glasses from which we had ex- 
pected much were firmly rejected. The ceremony of 
a glass of Benedictine stood us in good stead. The 
headman of the village was won over, but his tall, 
handsome old father did not appear to yield quite so 
readily to our attempted blandishments. It was evi- 
dent that except in defense of their property they did 
not care to attack the giant panda. We verified yes- 
terday’s story of the raid on the apiary, which stood 
close to the headman’s hut and was protected by a 
strong fence. It had taken place a month previous. 
We were also told that six years ago a beishung had 

211 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


been killed during a honey raid. This was the only 
record they could give us of one being killed. 

All afternoon and throughout the night it rained. 
In the morning the skies were overcast, but no rain 
was falling. Ten Lolo hunters with an equal number 
of dogs gathered outside our tent, which we had 
pitched close to a hut. We set out at half-past six for 
the mountains. A couple of hours’ climbing took us 
up among the bamboos, and we were soon drenched 
to the skin and shivering with the cold. Remnants of 
the winter snows lay in patches. The Lolos, shrouded 
in the brown or black capes they weave from sheep’s 
wool, seemed happy enough. They appeared to feel 
that there was a very good chance of bagging a pan- 
da, although we could not share in their optimism. 
As we made our way through the dense jungle along 
a ridge, we twice heard the dogs running something 
below us. In neither case did it come to anything. A 
halt was called and a fire was lit with much difficulty. 
One of the Lolos took a handful of moss into the 
centre of which he placed a bit of smouldering tinder. 
By dint of incessant blowing, undertaken in relay, a 
flame was eventually produced. 

Several of the Lolos had kept to the valley on 
either side of our ridge, and we had not been warming 
ourselves for long before a series of unintelligible 
shouts informed our companions that the dogs were 
running a sounder of swine. We did not want to 


212 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


shoot pig, but after a hurried debate we decided that 
it would be better for our future chances, in keeping 
our hunters keyed up, if we shot game for them when 
an opportunity arose that did not conflict with our 
beishung hunting. Such was very obviously the pres- 
ent situation, for with their dogs off after wild pig 
our Lolos could no longer hunt anything. 

The scramble down into the valley was most pre- 
cipitous; it was bad enough in the forested spots, but 
when we came to bare spaces it became all but im- 
possible. Our hopes of finding the going better along 
the bed of the torrent were quickly dashed. The 
stream thought nothing of a forty-foot drop, but to 
clamber down the slippery perpendicular rock slides, 
securing a precarious toe hold in a fissure or grasp- 
ing the doubtful root of some plant, proved a very 
different problem for us. The results of several falls 
entailed nothing more serious than abrasions and 
bruises and sprains, and we thought ourselves lucky 
on emerging on a native trail where the ravine dé- 
bouched out into the main valley. Rain and sleet 
were now falling, and most of the dogs had aban- 
doned the pursuit of the pigs. After some further fu- 
tile climbing our hunters decided to call it a day, and 
we returned to our tent with a practical working 
knowledge of the country and its difficulties. 

The headman had by now become definitely 
friendly, and we sat about the fire discussing ways 


213 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


and means of securing game, drifting now and then 
into more general topics. Hsuen’s choice of words 
and tournure de phrase were a constant delight and 
capable of cheering the most gloomy moments. The 
subject had as usual turned to sickness and to the 
medicine of which it required a constant well-balanced 
and calculating effort to preserve any of our small 
stock. Hsuen translated, “Headman say he got ghost 
in head. Ghost make head itch all time. Other peo- 
ple’s head not itch, so headman know he got ghost.” 
Ted, with great ceremony, presented a small box of 
cold cream, saying that if well rubbed in, it would lay 
the ghost. We were next told of a ghost that every 
January caused great mortality among the children. 
This was undoubtedly pneumonia, and a more diffi- 
cult apparition to deal with. 

The rain fell continuously all night, and several 
holes in the tent made it an uncomfortable residence, 
but there was no room in any near-by hut, so we had 
to make the best of it. Dawn broke cold and gray, 
with no pause in the rain. We thought it extremely 
unlikely that any Lolo would stir from his hut, but 
we were soon undeceived, for during the next hour no 
less than thirty-two men straggled in, each leading a 
dog on a string. The dogs were identical with those 
that we used in the Muping country. A good deal of 
rain had found its way into the tent during the night 
so that we were a bedraggled lot, even before start- 

Q14 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


ing; not that it made any difference, for half an hour 
would have sufficed to thoroughly saturate us with 
mud and water. 

Before setting off for the mountains our hunters 
“made medicine.” The Lolos carve no likenesses of 
their gods. To cure a sick man they hold services and 
beat drums, or burn holes in the scapular bone of a 
sheep, but nowhere did we see idols of any descrip- 
tion. Two of our hunters took sticks and whittled 
notches, muttering the while. As far as we could 
gather the main purpose was the determining of a 
propitious locality for the hunt. Hsuen looked sagely 
on. Turning to us he remarked, “They pray God,” 
then shrugging his shoulders, “Perhaps it do good, 
I not know.” 

Aside from the fact that we burrowed our way 
through a different bamboo jungle along a different 
ridge, there was little to differentiate this day’s hunt- 
ing from the previous. The dogs separated and pre- 
sumably hunted the valleys on either side. On each 
day we came across beishung sign, but in neither case 
did we feel that there was more than one animal 
indicated. From our observations we were certain 
that there were very few panda about, far less than 
in Muping. At noon we built a fire and from the 
folds of their cloaks the Lolos drew forth round 
lumps of bread liberally studded with beans. Among 
the hunters one old fellow particularly interested us. 


215 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


He must have been in the late sixties, and was looked 
upon by the others as a mine of hunting lore. His 
counsel weighed heavily in the decisions regarding the 
disposition of our forces. Somewhere en route he had 
gathered some white fungus which he roasted in the 
embers, and appeared to greatly enjoy. 

Once or twice the dogs picked up the trail of some 
animal, but they never held it for long. Aside from 
beishung signs of questionable age, all we saw were 
some musk-deer tracks, and a number of places where 
wild hogs had been rooting. 

The result of the evening conference was a deter- 
mination to put in a final hunt on the following morn- 
ing, and if nothing came of it to move further on to 
where we had been told takin were to be found. We 
paid our Lolo hunters from fifteen to thirty cents a 
day, offering a large reward to the man who could 
first point out a panda, and lesser rewards to any 
others who should really have had a part in the suc- 
cessful hunt. 

The villagers enriched themselves in other ways 
through us. We bought a sheep and eggs and some 
wine bowls and many stone pipes. One of the hunt- 
ers had a curious bamboo musical instrument which 
he played by holding it against his teeth and blowing 
upon it, while he snapped the taut bamboo slivers 
with his fingers. We asked Hsuen to get us a couple, 
and he wagged his head and said, with a twinkle in 
his eye: 


216 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


“That very bad thing, I tell you story some day all 
about it. It kill many people, oh, seven hundred.” 
Pressed to continue, he explained: “This country, 
same as everywhere, Father Mother he say I got son, 
I find him wife. He find wife. Girl’s mother, he 
find wife. Perhaps son see other girl he like. He 
play music her like this. She play music him. He 
say ‘My Father Mother say marry girl I no like.’ 
She say, “My Father Mother say I marry man I no 
like.’ He say, ‘I like you. You like me?’ She say, ‘I 
like you.’ He say, ‘We go top big mountain.’ They 
go top big mountain, they play music, they tie to- 
gether big knot round both necks, they both hang to 
tree. Oh, many people, seven hundred, die. That 
very bad.” 

Our next morning’s hunt was a repetition of those 
of the two previous days. Again we climbed a ridge. 
This time instead of rain and sleet we had snow, but 
the effect was much the same. We found no beishung 
sign. Ted saw a pheasant, and I caught a fleeting 
glimpse of a large squirrel. 

We had sent Hsuen ahead with our kit to Kooing 
Ma, telling him to gather the mules and go on to 
Litzaping, a village near which we had been told that 
takin were to be found. We followed at the termina- 
tion of our hunt, and reached Litzaping just before 
dark. 

Four headmen gathered to greet us. We first gave 
each a liqueur glass of Benedictine. We then bought 


217 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


some native wine which we poured into bowls that 
circulated among the headmen and their dependents. 
They were a fine, stalwart lot, and very friendly. We 
had heard that the Lolos were heavy drinkers, but we 
saw no indications of their being so. Their wine is 
usually made from corn. It varied greatly in qual- 
ity, though none of it could really be called an agree- 
able beverage. 

Our friends were unanimous in saying that there 
were very few takin about. We had heard there were 
many near a mountain named Tsumei Kwa. This 
they denied; one and all advising us to go on to 
Yehli, where they said there were many takin and an 
occasional giant panda. It was decided that we 
should send back the Chinese soldiers and take with 
us a son of each of the headmen. The Lolos said that 
with the soldiers accompanying us we might find our- 
selves in difficulties, but that without them we would 
be safe. Hsuen was by now thoroughly sick of the 
“brother-man” to whom he referred sarcastically as 
“our dear brother,” but he was afraid he might do us 
harm if sent back, and for several more days we gave 
way to Hsuen’s judgment. All of us were heartily 
glad when we eventually arbitrarily forced him to 
turn back. 

On the 12th of April we set out for Yehli. A per- 
sistent rain was falling, which added to the difficulties 
of the trail. Our pack animals consisted of four 


218 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


mules and two ponies. One of the latter missed his 
footing and started down the mountainside. A mule- 
teer pluckily grabbed his head stall and went rolling 
over and over among the boulders until long after it 
had seemed impossible for him to hold on further. A 
most convenient dense clump of sturdy bushes pre- 
vented pony and pack from taking the final sheer 
plunge into the river that would have definitely ended 
the career of both. We were delayed half an hour in 
lugging the pony back to the trail. 

Twice during the march a halt was called while the 
muleteers turned into road-builders to counteract the 
work of a landslide. At length the valley opened up, 
and we had easy going. The country became more 
and more lovely; giant pine trees grew beside the 
path, and we finally rode out onto a great mountain 
meadow, with a wide grassy valley, hemmed in by 
well-forested mountains terminating in barren snow- 
clad peaks. We felt that we might almost be back in 
the paradise of the Tian Shan. 

The hamlet at which we halted formed a part of 
the Yehli group. The cabins were large, and the 
ground about them was trampled with the feet of cat- 
tle and sheep. A heavy rain was falling and a dense 
fog soon shrouded the valley, making most welcome 
the pine-wood fire in the centre of the cabin. 

We started immediately on our hunting bundo- 
bust. Two self-styled experts were summoned. One 


219 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


was obviously a hunter, the other gave rise to doubts. 
The former had shot takin, and produced a head in 
proof. Both said that it was not too difficult to ob- 
tain them, and both agreed that there were a few 
beishung about, but that it would be almost impos- 
sible to get a shot at one. So far so good. Next a 
unanimous opinion was volunteered to the effect that 
it was impossible to hunt in this weather. When asked 
when an improvement might be looked for, both an- 
nounced that rain and sleet were in order at this time 
of year, and it was not possible to foretell a clearing 
up. This was indeed desolating, but Hsuen threw 
himself into the breach, bringing every argument to 
bear. The difficulty was increased by the absence of 
the headman of the valley. He had gone away on a 
journey, and the date of his return was uncertain. At 
length Hsuen made it clear that we could not await 
a change in weather, and all chance of a golden egg 
would vanish unless we were taken hunting on the 
morrow. Reluctantly the Lolos yielded. A new dif- 
ficulty was encountered when we tried to secure four 
hunters so that we could separate. The hunter with 
the aura of doubt said that he would substitute his 
son for himself. He told us the boy was eighteen, 
but when we had him paraded he was obviously no 
more than twelve. After skilful sifting it developed 
that his sole qualification as a takin hunter lay in his 
having once eaten takin meat. 


220 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


A shepherd brought in a very small wild pig, which 
he had found while tending his flocks in the hills. It 
was striped and spotted with white, and could not 
have been more than a few weeks old. It was dead, 
and conjured up visions of roast suckling pig at 
Thanksgiving dinners at Sagamore. We bought it 
for fifteen cents, and Hsuen’s culinary efforts re- 
sulted in an undeniable success. 

It was late before we had at long last straightened 
things out, and felt reasonably certain that four Lolos 
would be on hand in the morning. We then crawled 
up into a hay loft where we slept with the four heirs 
apparent of Litzaping who had come along with us as 
bodyguard. 

Saturday, the 13th of April, dawned cold and over- 
cast. It had snowed during the night and the floor 
of the valley was covered and the pine trees on the 
mountainsides were in conventional Christmas garb. 
We set off with four hunters, the takin-eating boy, 
and five slim black dogs. A thin misty rain set in. It 
was a cheerless beginning for what was destined to 
become our red-letter day. Four porters were to fol- 
low with our bedding rolls and those of the shikaries, 
for the programme called for a night or two a la 
belle étoile on the mountain where takin were re- 
ported. 

Three or four miles took us past the clump of 
cabins belonging to A’Souza, the absent headman. 


221 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


At an equal distance further up the valley we turned 
off into a ravine. It was only a short way further on 
that we came upon giant panda tracks in the snow. 
The animal had evidently passed a goodish while be- 
fore the snow ceased falling, but some sign that one 
of the Lolos found proved to be recent enough to 
thoroughly arouse all four natives. We had taken 
our ponies to ride until we should enter the jungle 
or the going become too difficult, and Mooka had 
come along to lead them back. It was now that he 
rose to the occasion and reached the high water- 
mark of his value. The Lolos hesitated to follow the 
beishung both for religious scruples and because of 
the very definite instructions that we wished takin. 
We could gather that a conference was under way, 
but believed that it concerned ways and means only, 
and there is no telling what might have resulted had 
not Mooka, as we afterward learned, stepped into the 
breach and explained that above all else we wanted 
beishung. 

This brought the conference to a close and we 
started off on the trail of the panda. It was not 
long before the dogs definitely demonstrated their 
utter futility. We passed some rat burrows in a small 
hollow. Four of our dogs lost all interest in any- 
thing beyond, and devoted their entire attention to 
the pursuit of the rats. The one that still remained 
with us trotted along at our heels baying until Ted 


222 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


caught it a shrewd clip with his stick. It retreated 
yowling into the forest and that was the last we saw 
of it. 

We now settled down to the steady business of 
tracking. Only three of the Lolos continued with us. 
The beishung appeared to be travelling along in lei- 
surely fashion, browsing on the bamboos as he went. 
The amount of sign that he left made us realize that 
we had been correct in making a conservative esti- 
mate of the number of beishung inhabiting the coun- 
try beyond Muping, in which we hunted. Soon we 
came upon a boar’s tracks superimposed on those of 
the giant panda. This continued for a mile or more 
Our quarry had evidently been in no hurry. For a 
while he followed up the rocky bed of a torrent, then 
he climbed a steep slope, through a jig-saw puzzle of 
windfalls. The fallen logs were slippery with snow 
and ice. Here we could crawl through and under; 
there we had to climb laboriously around and over. 
The bamboo jungle proved a particularly unpleasant 
form of obstacle course, where many of the feathery 
tops were weighed down by snow and frozen fast in 
the ground. Drenched by rain and soaked by snow, 
whenever a moment’s halt was called we alternately 
shivered and panted. For a few minutes the sun 
came out, and we were in dread that it would melt 
the tracks, but after a brief interval the murky clouds 
hid it again. 

223 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


We had been following the trail for two and a half 
hours when we came to a more open jungle. Tall 
spruce trees towered their giant bulk above the bam- 
boos. Lichen-covered alders were dotted about. An 
occasional blue or yellow flower poked its head up 
where the snow lay lighter. Here the panda had 
turned his attention more seriously upon provender. 
Under one tree he had made himself a nest of bam- 
boos. His claw marks scored the bark, and we lovcxed 
eagerly among the sturdy branches to see whether we 
could distinguish a black and white form crouching 
on a limb. His tracks led in many directions. The 
tough fibrous bamboos seemed to offer but an unin- 
viting dehydrated repast at best, and to judge from 
the droppings they were difficult to digest. Below 
us in the valley we heard the dogs yawping on the 
trail of some animal, probably a hog. We earnestly 
wished them elsewhere, for we felt sure that the bei- 
shung had not gone much further before seeking some 
accustomed haunt to spend the sleepy daylight hours. 

It was difficult to straighten out the trail. We cast 
first in one direction, then in another. Unexpectedly 
close I heard a clicking chirp. It might have been 
a bamboo snapping or the creaking of the interlock- 
ing branches of two trees swayed by the wind. I re- 
membered the eager interest of the Muping hunters 
at hearing just such a sound. In that case the noise 
had really been made by trees. One of the Lolo 


224 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


hunters was now close to Mokhta Lone and me. 
Noiselessly he darted forward. He had not got forty 
yards before he turned back to eagerly motion to us 
to hurry. 

As I gained his side he pointed to a giant spruce 
thirty yards away. The bole was hollowed, and from 
it emerged the head and forequarters of a beishung. 
He looked sleepily from side to side as he sauntered 
forth. He seemed very large, and like the animal of 
a dream, for we had given up whatever small hopes 
we had ever had of seeing one. And now he appeared 
much larger than life with his white head with black 
spectacles, his black collar and white saddle. 

Ted had started in a different direction with an- 
other Lolo, so Mokhta Lone and I eagerly signalled 
him. Though in reality only a short time, it was a 
nerve-wracking wait. The giant panda, dazed by 
sleep, was not really aroused, and was walking slowly 
away into the bamboos. If frightened it could vanish 
like smoke in the jungle. As soon as Ted came up 
we fired simultaneously at the outline of the disap- 
pearing panda. Both shots took effect. Not know- 
ing where his enemies were, he turned toward us, 
floundering through the drifted snow that lay in a 
hollow on our left. He was but five or six feet from 
Mokhta Lone when we again fired. He fell, but re- 
covered himself and made off through the densely 
growing bamboos. We knew he was ours, so al- 


225 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


though the Lolos had all along told us that a beishung 
was not an animal to be feared, we used caution in 
following the trail. A couple of the dogs that had 
been hunting in the valley below now came bounding 
by us, but they very evidently did not share their 
masters’ opinions as to the innocuous character of the 
beishung. They trailed, howling, behind us and noth- 
ing could induce them to go ahead. No help, how- 
ever, was needed, for the chase ended in seventy-five 
yards. He was a splendid old male, the first that the 
Lolos had any record of as being killed in this Yehli 
region. 

The shikaries, the Lolos, and ourselves held a mu- 
tual rejoicing, each in his own tongue. Our great 
good fortune could only with much effort be credited. 
We had hunted hard and long, usually in the face of 
every adverse circumstance. The previous evening 
conditions had been at their blackest, and it did not 
look as if we would be able to stir the Lolos from their 
homes. Now all was changed, and after so long hold- 
ing aloof, the Hunting Gods had turned about and 
brewed the unusual chain of circumstances that alone 
could enable us to shoot a giant panda, trailing him 
without dogs and with the crowning bit of luck that 
permitted us to fire jointly. 

When the enthusiasm had subsided sufficiently to 
allow us to think of anything beyond the immediate 
results of the chase, there were other plans to be 


226 


“HIGH, LOW, JACK, AND GAME” 


worked out. In the face of the rain and sleet, and 
handicapped by a badly burst case, I had packed my 
camera in my bedding. My first thought was to get 
it, so Ted stayed behind to struggle with transporta- 
tion difficulties while I hurried down to the valley. As 
we slithered and slipped and barked our shins and 
knees, we kept up a running recapitulation of the de- 
tails of the chase. When Mokhta Lone paused for 
breath I broke in and took up the tale. I later learned 
that Ted and Gaffer Sheikh had been similarly em- 
ployed. Mokhta Lone insisted that the panda was a 
sahib, for unlike the bear it did not ery out when shot. 

At length we reached A’Souza’s lodge, where we 
hoped to find Hsuen and the mules. There was no 
sign of them. It was a blow, for it further dimmed 
any chance of successful photography. While trying 
to learn of Hsuen’s whereabouts we sat for a few brief 
minutes at the hospitable hearth of Vooka, A’Souza’s 
wife, who acted in his stead during his absence. She 
was a tall handsome woman, wearing the conventional 
hooded head-dress of Lolo women. She was smoking 
a long-stemmed pipe with a heavy stone bowl, that 
she later told us had belonged to her mother and 
grandmother before her. Filling it with tobacco she 
thrust it into the fire to top the bowl with a pyramid 
of embers. Then she handed it ceremonially to me, 
reminding me of a Red Indian passing the peace 
pipe. One of her daughters brought half a dozen eggs 
in a bowl made of takin hide. 

027 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


There was, unfortunately, no time to be lost, so we 
hurried back to our last night’s halting-place. There 
we found Hsuen, and hastily started the mules for- 
ward to A’Souza’s. It was not until half past four 
that I again found Ted and the beishung. My pur- 
suit of the camera had taken me on a fourteen-mile 
jaunt. It was raining and sleeting, and numbed fin- 
gers made but clumsy work with the camera. These 
Lolos were unaccustomed to carrying loads, and Ted 
had had a weary and discouraging time of it. For a 
while he tried to float the panda down the stream; 
but after struggling in the ice-bound water for a short 
time he abandoned the attempt and gralloched the 
animal. He had hoped to avoid doing so until after 
the camera should have arrived. He found no trace 
of the panda having varied its bamboo diet. 

Great were the celebrations held that night at 
A’Souza’s. Vooka ordered a sheep to be slaughtered, 
and resolutely refused to allow us to foot the bill. In- 
termingled with it all, there was strongly pervading 
an element of superstition. The beishung was at first 
not permitted in the compound, and we were afraid 
that we would have to skin it in the rain and snow and 
mud. Religious scruples were at length so far re- 
laxed as to permit the shikaries to carry it into an 
isolated hayloft. A deeply interested group sur- 
rounded us at our work, but not an omnivorous Lolo 
of the lot would touch a morsel of the flesh. Hsuen

told us that after we left a priest was to be sent for

The feasting lasted late. The sheep was quartered 
and plunged into a huge cauldron that was raised on

A silent ring of expectant Lolos lined the walls of the 
big raftered building. They were shrouded in their 
long capes, and it was as well, for there were many 
chinks in the roughly shingled roof, and the rain fell 
ever faster. When the sheep was ready, Vooka’s ser-

around among the guests. Soon wine was poured 
into a wooden beaker and circulated through the 
throng. It was in the main a silent gathering, lit by 
the flickering fagots of pine that were thrust from

in the face of so many difficulties, had not been fa-

It was late when we crawled up into the hayloft

sleeping bags more gratefully welcome. Although we 
were past the age when “visions of sugar plums”

CHAPTER IX 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


“In twenty years they all came back, 
In twenty years or more, 
And everyone said, ‘How tall they’ve grown, 
They’ve been to the lakes and the torrible zone 
And the hills of the Chankley bore.’ ” —Leer. 


Suppose that you had, by the hardest sort of work, 
unexpectedly broken the world record for the mile 
run. Suppose that, tired but jubilant, you were sit- 
ting on the sidelines. Then suppose that at that mo- 
ment some one came up and said, “You must get up 
and run that mile again!” That is just the way Ker- 
mit and I felt when, after getting the giant panda, 
we realized that we must resume our interrupted 
takin hunt. We could not afford to take even a day’s 
rest, because we had to be in Indo-China for the hunt- 
ing as early as possible, since the summer rains make 
the low-lying jungles an almost impenetrable morass. 

Next morning early, when we sent for the native 
hunters, we found them far from keen. The feast the 
night before, and the bowls of corn wine, had made 
them feel more like gossiping in the courtyard than 
going again into the snowy mountains. Finally, after 

231 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


a good deal of urging we got them started about 
seven, which is very late when on the trail. The bed- 
ding and supplies for the two shikaries and ourselves 
were made into four bundles to be brought after us by 
porters. 

Fortunately the day was clear, and occasional 
bursts of sunlight lit the white snow with dazzling 
brilliancy. Soon we had passed the place where the 
day before we had come on the panda’s trail. The 
ravine got more steep and rugged, the jungle denser, 
and the going increasingly difficult. The snow lay 
deep on the ground. It had caught the tips of the 
bamboo grass, bent them down, and frozen them solid. 
Struggling up through them was like walking through 
croquet hoops. The Lolos were as usual as good- 
natured and shiftless as children. Every hour or so 
they would squat down in the snow and, grinning 
ruefully, rub their aching heads. It was hard to make 
them carry even Kermit’s camera. 

Once as we were plunging through a thicket we 
flushed a flock of jungle fowl. I was unable to get 
a clear view of them but, judging from a glimpse of 
one that I caught as it hurtled through a tree, they 
were as large as blackcock and of a dark color. 

We kept constantly on the watch for signs, as an 
animal that passed would leave a clear trail in the 
snow. We saw nothing but the delicate footprints of 
a barking deer until, well on in the afternoon, Mokhta 


232 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


Lone picked up with the field-glasses half a dozen 
trails running parallel across the snowy summit of 
a mountain. We asked the natives what they were 
and were told Yea Gnu (takin). None of us thought 
them large enough, but we felt we would chance a 
mistake. Leaving three of the local hunters behind, 
we started toward the mountain with the fourth. 
Soon he wished to sit down and let us go on alone. 
After a rather sharp argument we convinced him his 
plan would not work. 

It did not take us long to realize why he had wished 
to wait behind. The first part of our way lay through 
the usual bamboo thicket, but this time it was grow- 
ing among rocks covered with snow. Every ten yards 
some one’s leg would slip into a crevasse between 
two boulders, and down he would come with a thud. 
The snow had drifted into the valley up which we 
were climbing, and sometimes we floundered armpit- 
deep. After nearly an hour of this work we came out 
on the barren mountainside. It was very steep but 
still held the snow and ice in a glacier-like formation. 
At this moment the sun came out, and soon we were 
dripping with sweat and half-blinded by the glare. 
At length after climbing a particularly steep shoulder 
we reached the trails. They were not takin but wolf. 
As we sat panting, our main consolation was that one 
of the native hunters who had given us the wrong in- 
formation had suffered with us. 

233 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Undoubtedly two of the natives who were with us 
had at some time killed takin, but it was most difficult 
to get anything approaching accurate information 
from them. They told us the animals were at times 
savage. They feared them, particularly when in 
herds, and said when they found a number together 
they always climbed a tree before shooting. 

Half-scrambling, half-sliding, we reached the val- 
ley again in a fraction of the time it had taken us to 
climb up. A walk of a quarter of a mile brought us 
to our three hunters, squatting around a fire that they 
had built in the snow under a gnarled old tree. They 
explained by signs that this was where we were to 
camp. Soon the four porters joined us with our 
equipment. We had brought two single tents, each 
weighing twelve pounds. One we gave to our Kash- 
miris. The other we kept for ourselves. We soon had 
them pitched and our bedding unrolled within on the 
snow. 

Neither Kermit nor I eat much when we are hunt- 
ing hard, but at that we felt we were cutting ourselves 
fine when we found we had rationed ourselves each 
per day at the rate of one-fourth of a small tin of 
cornbeef, half a tin of U. S. Army emergency ration, 
and a few graham crackers. The best part of our fare 
was the hot cup of soup-like chocolate made from the 
iron ration. After supper we dried our socks before 
the fire and then turned in. 


234 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


Toward morning it became bitterly cold and neither 
of us could sleep. In the first faint light we decided 
that the day had begun, and hobbled stiff-legged to 
the fire. It took no time to get breakfast as there 
was nothing to cook, and before the sunrise we were 
going up the valley which led into the main ranges. 

After an hour and a half hard going we found our- 
selves near the salt spring on which the natives had 
pinned their faith. Very cautiously we approached, 
careful lest a twig should snap. At last we got where 
we could see it. There was not an animal anywhere. 
Not only that, but when we walked up to it we could 
see no tracks in the snow, which was a great disap- 
pointment as it meant that no takin had been there for 
many days. The spring itself bubbled from the 
ground in tiny spurts. I tasted the water and found 
it rather warm and brackish. 

The Lolos were very much discouraged and ex- 
plained that there could be no takin in the country, 
for if there were they visited the spring every few 
days. They wanted to turn back at once. Kermit 
and I felt that we did not want to leave until we were 
sure we had no chance. We split the party and fol- 
lowed up the various ravines, stopping wherever we 
could get a good view and combing the surrounding 
mountains with our field-glasses. After covering much 
country and failing to see even a track, we reluctantly 
decided that wherever the takin might be they were 
not in this place. 

235 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Our minds made up, we started back as rapidly 
as possible. Stopping just long enough at the place 
we had spent the night to break camp and start the 
coolies, we pushed on. The bright sun of the last two 
days had melted much of the snow, especially in the 
lower part of the valley. Peeping through their 
white coverlid were leaves with the fresh green color 
of early spring. 

When we reached the home of our host, the Lolo 
chief, it was mid-afternoon. We went into the com- 
bined living-room, bedroom and kitchen, and told 
Hsuen we had made up our minds to leave next day 
for Ningyuan. Our caravan men were glad to get 
this news, for as they were part Chinese they were 
far from popular in Lolo Land. Then we said we 
wanted to give a feast for the people of the valley. 
Our host, A’Souza, had not yet returned, but Vooka, 
his wife, at once protested. She said they would give 
the feast. The household was feudal in the best sense. 
They were generous to a fault and regarded hospi- 
tality as an obligation of their position. 

We never saw the Lord of the Manor, as he was 
away, but Vooka, his wife, pictured him fairly to us. 
She was accustomed to power. She had a pleasant 
low voice, a great contrast to the usual acrid shrill- 
ness of Asiatic women. Of only medium height, she 
looked tall. One of her eyes was terribly injured by 
some disease. Her clothes were voluminous, dirty 


236 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


and torn. Nevertheless, even when squatting by the 
fire smoking the old stone pipe which was her con- 
stant companion, there was an air of unconscious 
dignity about her that would have shamed many the 
grand lady. Once we tried to give her a gold ring 
set with a turquoise. She refused, with the statement 
that she greatly liked the idea because she knew it 
meant “our hearts were good,” but that she was an 
old woman and had no use for gewgaws. 

It was only when we insisted that we too felt the 
obligation of dispensing hospitality, that Vooka at 
length consented to let us buy food for the festivities. 
We purchased a sheep and rice. We tried also to buy 
some native wine to have served. It is an interesting 
witness to the general sobriety of these mountain folk 
that none was to be found. 

Kermit and I then set about packing and going 
over the loads, so as to have all in readiness for an 
early start next day. 

Meanwhile the feast was in course of preparation. 
A sheep was brought in from the hills and slaughtered 
in the courtyard. Without an instant’s delay it was 
carried into the house, skinned and quartered,—for 
animals are not kept but eaten immediately. The 
great iron pan-shaped pot was rested on the stones 
that surrounded the fire, and filled with rice and 
chunks of mutton. 

Soon the people of the neighboring houses began

TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


to arrive. Kermit and I sat in our rhoorkee chairs on 
one side of the fire. Around in a deep circle were 
ranged the natives squatting on the floor. Each had 
a bowl into which he continually dipped rice and mut- 
ton from the cauldron. There were no knives or — 
forks. Every one ate with his fingers. The only light 
came from the fire on which the cauldron was sput- 
tering. Once the unmarried daughter of the house 
told Hsuen to ask us if we could give her a cake 
of our soap. I happened to have an extra piece of 
Pear’s soap done up in its paper wrappers, which we 
gave her much to her delight. Gradually the sheep 
meat disappeared and the bones were thrown to the 
dogs. One by one the guests, thoroughly stuffed, 
drifted out into the night and their houses. By nine 
o’clock the festivities were finished. 

When they had all gone we presented the son and 
heir of the house, vice his father, with Kermit’s 32 
Colt automatic. His mother was really embarrassed. 
She explained that she had no valuable present for 
us, saying they had lost most of their family treasures 
when they were driven into this valley from their old 
home near Tachow. 

Next morning when we bade her good-by she had 
her family and many of the guests of the previous 
evening around her. I believe they were sorry to 
have us leave, and I know we were sorry to go. It 
seemed almost unbelievable that this kindly old wo-

man and these smiling natives were the savages we 
had been told would rob us. Nevertheless the fact 
remains that the route through Yelhi, though a short 
cut from Tatsienlu south, had been closed to cara- 
vans for many years. 

A bodyguard of sons of some of the local head men 
accompanied us as we struck east along the valley. 
The sun had just risen. The surrounding mountains 
were capped with snow and girdled with dark for- 
ests. The level floor of the valley was dotted with 
sheep and goats. Through the centre flowed the small 
stream that had its source where we had hunted takin. 
The rocks around which its water rippled were bril- 
liant red from some bright-colored lichen that cov- 
ered them. 

By ten we had crossed a small divide and dropped 
into a mountain meadow surrounded by splendid old 
spruce trees. Here we lost our faithful companion, 
Mokhta, who had been at least in part responsible for 
our chance at the panda. He told us this was as far 
as he wanted to come. We paid him his wages and 
gave him a sheath knife. The last we saw of him he 
was striking into the mountains on a short cut to his 
home. 

As the hours passed the valley broadened and be- 
came less rugged. We saw houses and cultivated 
fields. From the hillsides would rise groups of Lolos, 
wild-looking fellows with their black turbans and 


239 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


great capes. They streamed down on our caravan, 
far from friendly until they saw our escort. One 
mountaineer had his hair cut in a scalp-lock like 
an American Indian. Outside of two or three old 
smooth-bore, fuse-fired guns I saw no weapons, but 
probably the folds of the cloaks concealed some. 
Much of the march we had from ten to twenty walk- 
ing along with us. Like our friends at Yelhi, once 
confidence was established they were most friendly, 
smiling at us and looking with interest at our equip- 
ment. 

Looking down the trail ahead I saw moving white 
dots a mile or so away. For a moment I thought 
they were animals,—then I realized they were natives 
carrying coffin-boards to market. As we drew closer 
I could make out the outline of the coolies, who 
looked like industrious ants moving their eggs across 
the garden walk. These coffin-boards are enormous, 
three or four inches thick, between two and three feet 
broad, and six or seven feet long. It is this demand 
for large solid timbers that has contributed greatly 
to the deforestation of China. Not only that, but the 
cost to the living is tremendous. Respect for one’s 
ancestors is excellent; but when it is perverted to 
mean burying much of the family’s sorely-needed 
means with the deceased, and spending most of the 
balance on senseless formalities at the funeral, it is 
criminal folly. 


240 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


At three o’clock our Lolo escort gathered around 
us and Hsuen said they wished to bid us good-by, 
as Tachow was but twenty li distant and they dared 
go no further. We gave them “wine money” and 
they smiled and bowed their thanks. Gathering on 
a near-by ridge they watched until we disappeared 
from sight. 

Coming down the valley the seasons shifted with 
kaleidoscopic rapidity. On the top of the pass it was 
still winter. A few miles lower we were treading on 
the heels of spring. The poppy-fields were in full 
bloom. Still further on it was almost summer, for 
the blossoms had finished and the pods were being 
gathered. Soon we came to signs of Chinese occupa- 
tion. The types of people who stared at us from the 
fields were more Chinese. 

We decided to push ahead faster. The muleteers 
hurried their animals. Glancing back while we were 
jogging along one level stretch I saw an example of 
everything that should not be done in riding. Gaffer 
Sheikh was coming behind. He had dropped the reins, 
which flapped loosely around his pony’s neck. With 
both hands he was grasping the saddle pommel. He 
shook from side to side like a piece of corn in a pop- 
per. Most remarkable of all, he neither considered 
that he was in difficulties nor that he was riding badly. 

Of all bad riders, and I have seen many, I con- 
sider the Kashmiris in a class by themselves. No horse 


241 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


or mule that breathes can last long under them. At 
the end of about a week there is a sore on the back 
or a swelling in the mouth, and the same plaint, 
“Sahib, hamara ghora na thik hai” (Sir, my horse is no 
good) ,—for no native will ever admit that he is by 
any chance to blame for anything that may go wrong. 

It was evening when, after crossing and recrossing 
a little river by numerous fords, we came to the small 
walled town of Tachow. An open-mouthed crowd 
gathered at the gate and watched our battered train 
file in. We knew we were objects of unusual interest 
but could not tell why as we had no means of speak- 
ing to any one. Hsuen, the faithful, had stayed be- 
hind on the road to make sure the beishung bones 
which were carried by a porter were not lost, for “It 
make too much sadness if lose.” 

When Hsuen appeared we sent him at once to the 
magistrate with our cards, the letter from the Central 
Government, and the pair of oft-rejected field- 
glasses. As Kermit said, “He can’t very well send 
them back if Hsuen leaves them.” 

In a comparatively few minutes the “evangelist” 
returned beaming with smiles. The unexpected had 
happened. The stone which the builders had rejected 
had become the headstone in the corner. The field- 
glasses had produced a sensation. “He suspicious. 
He ask what we want for give such valuable present. 
I say nothing but make happy. He come quick.” 

242 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


So said Hsuen, and almost at once the local of- 
ficial appeared. He was a nice looking, spare young 
fellow of about thirty. With him came another young 
fellow who was a local headman. We made them 
join us in the black den which was our bedroom in 
the back of the inn. There by the light of a guttering 
oil lamp we offered them each a liqueur glass of bene- 
dictine, the last drop of anything we had. 

Then we started the usual formalities. We found 
the magistrate had been born in a village near by. 
He asked what we had been doing. We told him 
hunting and showed him a picture of the panda. 
Neither he nor any of those who had come with him 
had ever heard of the animal. It was another 
puzzling and discouraging instance of the extreme 
localization of information in Western China. Here 
was an intelligent man, born in this place, who did 
not even know of the existence of the beishung though 
we had killed one but twenty-five miles away. 

Preliminaries finished, we came to the matters that 
really interested him and the explanation of the rea- 
son we had been regarded with such enthralled inter- 
est. He wanted to know where we had been, and 
how we had come in so peacefully, unrobbed and un- 
troubled, from hostile Lolo Land. He said never in 
his knowledge had a white man been there, and that 
for many years no Chinese had ventured there. It 
was as if in the tenth century a party of Kuropean 


243 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


Christians had turned up healthy and unharmed at 
Constantinople, explaining they had just come from 
Mecca. 

We were now on the borderland between the Lolo 
country and China. Like the border between Scot- 
land and England it was the scene of practically con- 
tinuous warfare. Raid and reprisal succeeded each 
other in an interminable procession. Tachow itself 
was a frontier outpost, always under arms. Raiders 
had broken in a few months before and burnt a num- 
ber of the houses. Only that morning the magistrate 
told us a village not a mile from the town walls had 
been sacked by the men from the hills, and one of his 
soldiers had been killed in the action. The population 
of the town and its surroundings was mainly Chinese, 
with a mixture of valley Lolos who stood to their 
mountain kinsmen much as the lowland Scots stood 
to the highlanders. 

When he asked what he could do for us, we said 
we wanted him to help us get to Ningyuan as rapidly 
as possible. He was much pleased at this very mod- 
erate request, and said he would not only send an 
escort of soldiers to see us safely to the first big town, 
some twelve miles away, but would lead the party him- 
self. 

Next morning, in spite of the promises the mule- 
teers had made, they did not appear. Kermit went 
to hurry them, and I asked Hsuen to buy a small 


244 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


image we had seen in the front room of the inn. It 
was a delightful little carved wood figure mellow 
with the gloss of ages. The people did not wish to 
sell, but made this obvious in an odd fashion. They 
hid it, put another in its place, and to the end insisted 
it had never been there. 

The pack-train ready, the fatisisate appeared 
with our escort. It consisted of thirteen Chinese sol- 
diers and twenty-two Lolos. The magistrate had 
given the latter a wine and mutton feast the previous 
evening on the condition that they would come. 

In the usual noisy eastern confusion, we clattered 
and slipped over the grimy paving stones. Pigs 
grunted and scrambled out of our way, dogs barked, 
women and children screamed and chattered. As we 
passed the old gray stone gate the sun had just risen, 
and the country had that fresh cleanness of new sun- 
light. Behind and around us lay the black hills and 
mountains of the Lolos. To the west was a particu- 
larly beautiful snow-mountain. We were told it was 
called the Golden Mountain, and indeed its snow- 
fields did not look unlike gold quartz as they sparkled 
in the sun. 

As we filed along I glanced back. Our caravan 
was certainly a jungle outfit. Kermit and I, with our 
ragged beards and worn hunting clothes, were as wild- 
looking as any of the mountaineers. Behind us came 
the Lolos with their black turbans and rifles of every 


245 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


type and age. Then came the ponies with our kit. 
Such American-made boxes as we still had were bat- 
tered beyond semblance of their former selves. The 
rest of the things were in native skin-bags. For ex- 
ample, we carried our basins and toilet articles in 
Tibetan goatskin-bags, tanned with hair on and the 
feet sewed outside. The few pots and pans that 
formed our kitchen were black with smoke and dented. 
In a few minutes we passed a small village which, 
the magistrate explained, was the one which had been 
sacked the day before. Then we came to a forest. 
Apparently this was the point where danger was first 
expected. Our escort doubled forward and scattered 
left and right through the woods. It was not done 
clumsily for show, as is so often the case when Chi- 
nese soldiers are anxious to give you the impression 
that they are guarding you from danger where none 
really exists, but with the careful efficiency of men 
who mean business and know what they are doing. 
The magistrate himself dismounted, took a rifle, and 
went forward with the advance guard. Whether due 
to the excellent screen that enveloped us, or because 
no robbers were there that day, we climbed the hill 
and without incident wound our way down the other 
side. At the foot the Lolos gathered and we were 
told they were leaving us. We gave them a few dol- 
lars wine money and left them happy. The magis- 
trate and the thirteen real soldiers stayed with us. 


246 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


The country became more open. Grain and poppy 
fields alternated. The men and women working there 
straightened up, and gazed at us with the awestruck 
look of country children watching a circus parade. It 
was still morning when we reached the gray moss- 
draped walls of Mienning-hsien. It was a busy, dirty 
trade-centre where caravan trails from a half dozen 
places met. The crowds almost blocked our way as we 
pushed through the bazaar. We stopped for a mo- 
ment to say good-by to the nice little official from 
Tachow, and fee his soldiers. Then, leaving Hsuen to 
make our excuses and give our cards to the magis- 
trate of the city, we trotted on to join the caravan, 
which was already on its way downstream. 

We had travelled steadily for eleven hours when 
we reached the village of Lokow. There we had luck, 
for we found a Chinese Catholic Mission and had 
clean rooms in which to sleep. Of course with our 
long beards we were taken for French Fathers. For- 
getting this, I explained by signs to one of the local 
Chinese that each of us had four children. He took 
it in good part, however, evidently feeling that we 
were merely some different type of Holy Father. 

Here we got news from Suydam and Jack Young. 
At first we were able to find out nothing but that they 
had passed through some four days ago. The post- 
office, where they were supposed to have left a letter 
for us, denied any knowledge of it. Knowing Chinese 

Q47 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


post-offices, however, we would not take no for an 
answer. Finally, we wormed out of them the infor- 
mation that some days ago a letter had been left ad- 
dressed in a foreign tongue, and that they had sent it 
to a Catholic Mission fifteen li away. ‘The postmas- 
ter said he would try to get it for us if we would pay 
him in advance. We said he had the cart before the 
horse. He would get his backshish when he brought 
the letter. He did, and we were glad to hear that all 
had gone well with the other echelon of our party. 

Both Kermit and I were sick. Kermit had a re- 
turn of his poisoning and I had an infected leg and a 
go of fever coming. We had seen on the map where 
the Amning river ran from Lokow to Ningyuan and 
decided to try to get a boat. We hired one to meet 
us at a place about twelve miles from town, and offered 
to take the loads with us but, for some reason which 
we were never able to fathom, the caravan men pre- 
ferred to go the entire way by the road. 

As we were riding out next morning we passed a 
foundry where iron pots were being smelted in clay 
moulds. There was one remarkable innovation,—a 
bellows run by water power. It was made of a cylin- 
der cut from the log of a tree in which a piston was 
operated by an eccentric crank attached to a hori- 
zontal water-wheel. It impressed me greatly, for it 
was the first time I had seen anything in western 
China suggesting a competent labor-saving device in 
industry. 


248 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


When we came to the boat we found it the usual 
clumsy barge built of ill-fitting planks. Of course it 
leaked and an inch or more of dirty water swashed 
around the bottom. Two natives handled it with great 
sweeps,—one at the bow, one at the stern. Kermit 
and I made ourselves comfortable on the bottom and 
I started at once writing this chapter. We drifted 
with the current down the winding muddy stream. 
On each side low-lying green rice fields stretched. 
Unwieldy, nearly hairless water-buffaloes slowly 
tugged ploughs through the black mud, or turned to 
stare at us with mild incurious eyes. On the sand- 
bars gray and white cranes were standing. Snipe 
rose and flickered off. Occasionally as we rounded a 
bend we startled a flock of Brahmini ducks which 
rose with a splatter of water. The sun was blister- 
ingly hot. It seemed impossible that only three days 
before we had been in rough forests waist-deep in 
snow. Kermit and I had bought a couple of big 
straw cartwheel hats to act as sunshades. When he 
put his on he looked, with his ragged beard, like a 
disreputable medizval saint with a halo. 

At noon the boatmen brought our craft to the bank 
and disappeared toward a cluster of huts. Hsuen 
told us they had gone to their homes to get “things 
to eat.”? This seemed reasonable, but, when they did 
not return in forty-five minutes, we sent him to tell 
them we had engaged them to take us downstream, 
not to feast. 

249 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


It was four o’clock when we reached the place, some 
twenty-five li from Ningyuan, where they were to 
land us. Our road now led us across the paddy 
fields. For the last hour or so a storm had been ris- 
ing. Soon a wind blew from the mountains in gusts 
that constantly grew in violence. Trees were bent 
like blades of grass. A branch was broken off as we 
passed through a village and nearly killed a little boy 
who was running across the street. Then came the 
rain. Soon we were soaked and chilled to the bone. 
The rubber soles on our shoes made it nearly im- 
possible to stand. We slipped and slid about like a 
dog on a frozen pond. We had Hsuen ask the few 
draggled people we passed the distance to the city. 
We got the usual answers. A born optimist would 
say ten li. Then we would plod along for half an hour 
and inquire again, only to be told fifteen. It was the 
longest twenty-five li I ever walked. The city was 
hidden by trees, rain, and mist. We came on it sud- 
denly. Even then we were not through, for the Mis- 
sion was at the other end of town. Nothing ever 
looked better than its courtyard, with Suydam and 
one of the Fathers coming forward to greet us. 

The caravan was there, so we went upstairs at once 
and put on dry shirts. Then we came down to dinner, 
which had been kept waiting. Soon we were seated 
in real chairs around a real table eating potage and 
drinking red wine. 


250 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


There were three priests. The Bishop, Mon- 
seigneur Baudry, was a slight, kind-faced man, a 
Rochellese by birth, but it had been thirty-five years 
since he had left China. Pére Le Mercier was a thick- 
set, jolly man of thirty-six, typical of the France he 
loved. Pére Le Bouetté came from Breteuil, and had 
served in China fifteen years. All three had long 
beards and wore the black gown of their order, Les 
Missions Etrangéres. 

This French Mission is a most remarkable organi- 
zation. It has branches all over the world,—Africa, 
Ceylon, China. The aspirants are sent to a seminary 
in Paris where they study for six years. Then one 
day comes an order, “You will go to Ceylon,” or 
maybe, “China”—and they go, often the next day. 
What is more extraordinary, the man does not know 
a word of the language of the land to which he is be- 
ing sent, for languages are not taught in the semi- 
nary. He is expected to learn when he gets to his 
destination. The members of the Mission for the most 
part never see France again. A very, very few, such as 
Monseigneur de Guébriant, the great explorer, rise to 
the head of the order and return to Paris. An equally 
small number, I know of one case only, are afflicted 
with some incurable and totally disabling disease, such 
as paralysis, and are sent home. The rest live their 
lives and die in the country to which they are sent, 
without ever leaving it. There has been only one ex- 


251 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


ception,—the Great War. During those days when 
France was fighting for her life the members of the 
Missions Etrangéres came home and joined the army. 

All three of the Fathers at Ningyuan were de- 
lightful. They were cultivated, and kept thoroughly 
in touch with what was happening in the world. There 
was a library of many hundreds of volumes. Mon- 
seigneur remarked, when I complimented him on it, 
that it had been burnt three times during the civil dis- 
orders. 

Chatting over the wine we heard many interesting 
side-lights on their daily lives. Pére Le Mercier had 
been a sergeant in the army. He had been captured 
by the Germans and twice had all but escaped. He 
joined the Mission after the war, and was sent to 
China. The day he left Yunnanfu he was captured 
by bandits, robbed of everything, and turned loose on 
the country without being able to speak a word of 
the language. Once Baudry, when sick with fever, 
had been attacked by twenty outlaw Chinese soldiers 
and an officer. He was mounted at the time and fled. 
They fired on him and pursued him. He distanced 
them on the level, but when he had to climb a hill they 
gained. The chase ended when some Lolos, who were 
friendly, gathered and killed all the soldiers and the 
officer. The ride cost Monseigneur the sight of one 
eye, for the strain greatly aggravated the fever. 
Fourteen years before Monseigneur Baudry had been

back to the same part of Lolo Land, where we had 
hunted. He said he believed that he was the only 
other white man who had ever been there. 

Besides the Fathers there were two Sisters who 
devote most of their attention to care of the sick. 
They were very kind to me and it was largely due 
to them that my attack of fever was broken so quickly. 

One of the most interesting characters in the Mis- 
sion was the catechist, Chow Tze Kow. He was a Man- 
chu. His family had lived in Ningyuan for ten genera- 
tions. He was a tall handsome man with a long white 
beard. Originally he had made his living as a scribe, but 
had taken opium to such an extent that he could not 
work, and his father had turned him out of the house. 
Thereupon, entirely without aid, he decided to stop 
the drug. He did, though for fifteen days he hung 
between life and death. Then he joined the Mission. 
He had a small room within the Mission walls. It 
was like a curiosity shop. Every corner was piled 
with odds and ends,—old native books, musical in- 
struments, bits of old watches, pictures of the Holy 
Family and the saints. His professions were all-in- 
clusive. He cobbled shoes. He mended clocks and 
watches. He cut blocks for Chinese calling cards for 
me. One day he took an old Chinese astrolabe and 
took the time by the sun for us. There was nothing 
he could not and did not do. Above all, he was a mu- 
sician. 

253 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


One evening he gave us a concert, bringing his or- 
chestra with him. His orchestra was almost as re- 
markable as he was. There were three of them,—the 
interpreter of the Mission, a little, old, typical 
Chinaman, very thin, with his brown skin drawn tight 
over his cheeks. Another had a kindly, wrinkled face 
like an old apple, and was professor in the local gov- 
ernment school. The third was a tall, handsome Man- 
chu who was in the local customs service. All of them 
played stringed instruments. The catchist, at the 
head of the table around which they were gathered, 
pulled the stroke oar. He played on a harpischord. 
They gave selections from old Chinese operas, each 
taking a part and singing in turn. The music was in 
a minor key and monotonous to a European ear. 

Another day he alone played to us, this time on 
a lute. The very names of the selections were pic- 
turesque. “Swans Flying Over the Rapids,” ‘“Con- 
fucius Studying,” “The Foreign Mandarin Leaving 
for His Home with Music.” 

The city of Ningyuan was administered by Gen- 
eral Chao, a big, handsome man. He had been orig- 
inally a leader of free companions, but now was firmly 
aligned with the government. He neither smoked 
opium nor drank. He was Christian,—though of 
what denomination I never knew,—and perhaps be- 
cause of this he had his headquarters in the defunct 
Baptist Mission. 


254 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


While strolling through the bazaar, we had an il- 
lustration of the fact that China is composed of back 
eddies where almost anything may collect. We 
stopped at a silk-shop and there on the wall, yellow 
with age, was a sheet from a New York daily fifteen 
years old, giving the photographs of men who were 
attending the first Plattsburgh Camp,—Robert Ba- 
con, John Purroy Mitchel, etc. In a grocery store we 
saw the china figure of a woman with a skirt trail- 
ing the ground and a muff and a boa, evidently made 
in Germany in the seventies. 

It was three days before we were able to get a mule 
caravan. The morning that we left all the Fathers and 
the catechist came out to wish us Godspeed. Our stay 
at Ningyuan had been a delightful interlude and we 
knew we had made three good friends. 

Although we felt, when we reached the French 
Mission, that we were in civilization and a part of the 
outside world again, this was really far from being the 
case. There was no telegraph in the city, and even 
mail took between ten days and two weeks to get to 
the nearest railroad. 

Our aim now was to get to Yunnanfu in the most 
rapid time possible. Day after day we travelled from 
dawn to dark. At first our road led us down the broad 
valley of the Amning Ho. The country was lovely. 
The hedges were a mass of wild-rose, not the wild- 
rose of America, but full-bodied pink and white blos- 


255 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


soms. The cacti were tipped with a saffron-colored 
bloom. Pomegranate trees were gay with orange 
flowers. By the irrigation ditches we saw young girls 
acting duck-herds for flocks of goslings. They kept 
their diminuitive charges from straying by sweep- 
ing them together with light, long-handled bamboo 
brooms. 

One night at Kungtuying we were awakened by 
the beating of a gong. When we inquired what was 
happening we were told that robbers had looted a 
house on the outskirts and had taken “‘a horse, a mule 
and a goat.” 

On the fifth day of hard travel we reached Hweili- 
chow. There we went at once to the French Mission, 
where we were met with the bitterest disappointment. 
There was no mail. Through some error everything 
had been sent north to Tatsienlu. Some letters had 
gone only a few days before, according to the post- 
master. It was four months since we had letters, as 
the last we had received were dated the end of Decem- 
ber. Father Andren did his best to cheer us, but, 
under the circumstances, with no success. 

Our trail now led us through a rough, uncultivated, 
sparsely settled country. On the second day we 
crossed the Yangtze, a broad, swift-flowing stream, 
red with silt. In spite of the fact that theoretically 
we were still in the dry season, it had rained contin- 
ually. The day we crossed the river we finished our 


256 


THE END OF THE TRAIL 


march after dark by climbing one of the slipperiest 
clay hills it has ever been my misfortune to meet. 
Men, mules, and horses slipped and fell every few 
steps as they struggled up in the face of a blinding 
storm. I was very glad to see the dark little flea- 
infested hole where we were to spend the night. 

Day followed day in rapid succession. The marches 
of the mule train were remarkable, averaging nearly 
thirty miles. We started in the gray dawn, and dark 
would find us filing into some small town to spend the 
night. One of the villages had the delightful name of 
Shih Mah Lung Kai, or “Market Place for Horses 
and Dragons.” We found none of the latter, but on 
the following day, with the aid of a dozen small boys, 
killed a large snake,—the first we had seen on the 
trip,—in the mud wall of a house. 

Once while we were climbing a hillside we met a 
member of the Chinese Inland Mission, Mr. Hinks, 
who was travelling north to his post with his wife and 
baby. Much to our surprise he greeted us by name. 

The morning of the seventh day we started even 
earlier than usual. Five hours fast going brought us 
to the crest of a low ridge. Before us lay the city of 
Yunnanfu, with beyond the placid waters of the lake. 
We were back at the railhead again after two thou- 
sand miles of travel by boat, on foot, and on horse- 
back. 

Mr. Chamberlain, the American Consul, was most 


257 


TRAILING THE GIANT PANDA 


kind, and with his aid and that of Messrs Page and 
Hoyt of the Standard Oil Company, we succeeded in 
getting our arrangements made to leave the follow- 
ing day. 

Then came the really sad part of the expedition— 
saying good-by to both men and animals of whom 
we were really fond. Our three ponies we gave to 
friends, who promised they would always have a good 
home. We paid the men in the evening, but they 
were all at the train next morning to see us off. They 
had been with us for long months. Together we had 
shivered in the bitter winter cold of the high moun- 
tains and sweltered in the damp heat of the semi- 
tropics. ‘Together we had passed through troubles 
ranging from lost mules to bandits. Now in all prob- 
ability we would never meet again. As the train rat- 
tled out of the station, our last glimpse showed the 
three mute figures of Hsuen, Chin and Chow. 

So ended our expedition in China. The hunting in 
Indo-China is another story, for mischance or sickness 
split our original party.
