APPENDICES 


APPENDIX A 


ZOOGRAPHICAL NOTES ON THE SINO 
TIBETAN BORDERLANDS 


Berore starting on the expedition we visited many 
libraries specializing in books of travel and exploration 
and studied the catalogues of numerous old booksellers, 
but we could find very little that dealt in any way with 
the big and small game of the country to which we were 
going. We found, as usual, that the shelves of Francis 
Kdward’s bookshop in London had the most to offer, but 
even there we were unable to obtain much of the informa- 
tion we sought. It has seemed, therefore, that a short 
résumé of the country from a hunter’s standpoint might 
not be amiss. 

The parts of Yunnan and Szechuan through which we 
passed can by no stretch of the imagination be termed a 
big game paradise. It is no country for the inexperi- 
enced hunter nor for one who wishes to secure a large bag 
without undue expenditure of time and effort. An ex- 
perienced hunter, who is not obliged to hurry, can, how- 
ever, secure a varied bag, and be certain that practically 
every animal he collects will prove of great interest, with 
a very definite possibility of finding it a new species or 
sub-species. 

The first prize is the giant panda, whose scientific name 
is Hluropus melanoleucus. The natives know him as the 
beishung or white bear. T’o the best of our judgment he 
has a fairly wide area of distribution but is to be found 
only in pockets, and is never abundant, even in these 
pockets. He lives in the bamboo jungles in altitudes va- 


261 


APPENDIX A 


rying between six and fourteen thousand feet. We came 
to the conclusion that it could be safely assumed that 
where there were no bamboo jungles, there were no bei- 
shung. Native information required careful checking, 
for even after a detailed description of the animal, accom- 
panied by showing a plate depicting it, we could not rely 
on a native’s word as to its presence in a district. Some- 
times this was due to very hazy notions of coloration and 
the assumption that the white crescent on the black bear’s 
chest entitled it to be called a beishung. At other times 
misinformation was wilful, either with the common im- 
pulse to give pleasant and agreeable news or because the 
native counted on earning some money as guide before his 
deception could be discovered. We took no one’s word as 
guarantee of the presence of beishung. Where he actually 
exists, his droppings are frequent and easy to find, and 
even easier to identify. They are egg shaped, from five 
to seven inches in length (for the adult) and composed 
of partially digested bamboo shoots. The Muping and 
Yehli districts were the only places where, after diligent 
and unceasing inquiry throughout the trip, we could be 
convinced of the presence of giant panda. Doctor Rock 
told us that he had heard of them at Tangpu in Kansu. 
While he had neither seen skins nor had time to look for 
sign, he was convinced from the detailed description given 
him that beishung were actually to be found. Owing to 
their scarcity and the nature of the country in which 
they live the prospects of getting a shot at one still hunt- 
ing are very slender. With a pack of good bear hounds 
they should not be difficult to secure. 

The beishung does not hibernate. We found fresh sign 
in regions where the brown and black bears were hiber- 
nating, and the one we shot was living in a locality where 
the black bears had not yet awaked from their winter’s 
nap. We came upon his tracks one morning in the newly 


262 


APPENDIX A 


fallen snow. They were partly obliterated, for four or five 
hours had passed since he went by. Three hours’ trailing 
through dense jungle brought us to the spot which he had 
selected for his siesta. We caught sight of him emerging 
from the hollow bole of a giant fir tree, and fired simul- 
taneously. 

The giant panda, from all we could learn, is not a say- 
age animal. After the shooting our Kashmiri shikaries 
remarked that he was a sahib, a gentleman, for when hit 
he had remained silent, and not called out as does a bear. 
In the nearest Chinese village, perhaps twenty-five miles 
distant, no one had ever heard of a giant panda, nor seen 
the skin of one. Other incidents of this nature contin- 
ually confirmed our belief in the giant panda’s scarcity. 

In Northern Yunnan and in Szechuan both brown bear 
(probably some sub-species of Ursus arctos) and black 
bear (Ursus torquatus) are to be found. The latter are 
known locally as gho shung (dog bear). They are very 
numerous in a number of districts through which we 
passed. They were hibernating at the time, and we were 
told that they holed up in December and did not come out 
until May or June, according to the locality. We saw 
much old sign and were ready to credit the native hunters’ 
statements that they were not difficult to get. Gaffer 
Sheikh either saw or thought he saw one near Tienwei 
on January 22d. The hunters were unable to explain its 
being out so late. We often saw bearskins used for coats 
and rugs. In the Lamaseries masks made from bears’ 
heads were frequently to be found. The Lamas use them 
in their devil dances. 

The brown bear, known by the natives as ma hsiung, 
or horse bear, is by no means as numerous as the black, 
but we saw masks made from their head skins in several 
Lamaseries, and were told by the Lamas that the animal 
lived in the neighboring forests. We also saw a number of 


263 


APPENDIX A 


hides. As far as we know it has never been collected. It 
is not mentioned in such accounts of the animals of the 
district as we have read. As well as we could determine it 
does not exist south of Szechuan. It is much feared by 
the natives, and is unquestionably a disagreeable cus- 
tomer for a woodcutter to meet. It seems possible that 
there may be more than one variety. A good series would 
be of great value to a museum, and would deserve careful 
scientific study. They vary markedly in coloration, some 
being so light a brown that it was to these that the na- 
tives thought we referred when inquiring for beishung 
(white bear). It may be remarked, however, that of two 
bears we shot in the Tian Shan in 1925, one was dark and 
one straw colored. 

Takin (Budorcas tibetanus) existed in a number of the 
localities in which we hunted. They are known by the 
natives as yeh niu (wild cattle). Doctor Rock saw one in 
the mountains west of Kienchwan-chow in Yunnan. The 
first time we personally got any reliable information con- 
cerning them was not until four days after we had crossed 
the Yalung River at Metilong. At Tatsienlu we saw both 
heads and hides. Near Muping they are not common. 
We felt that February would probably be the best month 
in which to hunt them, for they would then be more easily 
trailed in the snow. At Chuilung we met a wandering 
Lolo who said that he knew of a mountain which he called 
Yung Shuei Ting, meaning Salt Well Mountain, where 
takin were to be found in great numbers, and where we 
could get them for a certainty. He said that this moun- 
tain was seven long marches away, but he was ready to go 
with us if we would guarantee him protection against un- 
friendly Lolos. Lack of time prevented our taking ad- 
vantage of this opportunity, which we bitterly regretted 
missing. As far as we could determine his mountain must 
have been somewhere in the neighborhood of Yehli. 


264 


APPENDIX A 


We felt that Yehli was the best takin country through 
which we passed, and given time, a good shikarry should 
be able to count upon takin as a certainty. We invariably 
found the natives to have great respect for the takin’s 
fighting qualities, and to regard them as no mean ad- 
versary. 

Sambhur (Cervus unicolor Dejeani) have a very wide 
range, and from the time we left Bhamo until we reached 
Ningyuan we were rarely out of sambhur country. All 
large deer are known as malu by the Chinese. Almost 
any sambhur from this region should prove most inter- 
esting to the scientist, for from the greater part few if 
any specimens have been brought out. We found sambhur 
signs at altitudes varying from four to fourteen thousand 
feet. Many of the horns we saw were both long and ex- 
ceptionally massive. The sambhur is irregular in shed- 
ding his horns. The female we shot was exceptionally 
dark in color, almost black. 

There are undoubtedly large stag in parts of the coun- 
try. There have been so few of them collected that it is 
not definitely known what they are. Indeed as far as we 
know only females have been shot by Europeans. Their 
range does not extend as far south as that of the sambhur. 
The natives say there are two varieties, but this may be 
merely because there is a wide range in color. At times 
they have been classed as wapiti, at times as a sub-species 
of Kashmir barasingh. 

Wapiti (Cervus songaricus). We first heard of them 
when not far south of Tatsienlu, and it was in that town 
that we saw the greatest quantity of horns. They are 
known in the Tatsienlu region as ghwar. It was difficult 
to determine the exact locality from which these came. 
Many probably were brought from many marches back 
in Tibet. The Chinese set such store by stag horns for 
medicinal purposes that it is amazing that any deer should 


265 


APPENDIX A 


survive. The natives track them in the snow, or organize 
drives for them, or hunt them with dogs. The horns are 
of greatest value when in the velvet. We never felt that 
we were where wapiti were abundant. At Kooing Hai 
when we were hunting panda there was an old man who 
was regarded as the best local hunter, and we were told 
as witness to his prowess that each year he got a stag. 
This certainly did not point to their being plentiful. Here, 
as elsewhere, they appear to be absolutely regular in shed- 
ding their antlers. Those we saw in Tatsienlu must have 
represented a number of variants or sub-variants. There 
is much pioneer work to be done in classifying the Szech- 
uan and neighboring stags. 

We saw roe deer (Capreolus pygargus) horns in Ta- 
tsienlu but could not learn the definite locality from which 
they came and never heard of them in any place where we 
were hunting. This roe should be akin to the roe that we 
shot in the Tian Shan. They are there abundant. 

Musk deer (Moschus moschiferus) have a very wide 
distribution, and in spite of the fact that they are every- 
where assiduously hunted they are plentiful in many lo- 
calities. In their pursuit the local hunters almost invaria- 
bly use dogs. Musk commands a good and ready market 
and the tushes of the deer are in common use as orna- 
ments. They are called chau by the natives. 

Barking deer (Muntiacus lacrimans) are also of very 
frequent occurrence. Not having any particular com- 
mercial value, they are not molested to the same extent as 
the musk deer. During the entire journey we were rarely 
out of muntjac country. The native name is hwang ya tze. 

Burrhel or blue sheep (Pseudovis nahoor) we first heard 
of when we were leaving the Muli territory. They are 
known locally as pan yang. We saw heads and skins, and 
were told that if we wished to trek from a day to two days 
away from the trail we would find them plentiful. They 


266 


APPENDIX A 


live at about fourteen thousand feet elevation. Besides 
those shot two days north of 'Tatsienlu, we heard of them 
near Muping. They should also live in the mountains near 
Yehli, but we there saw neither hide nor horn. 

Serow (sanlu) and ghoral (iyang) were reported in all 
the mountain ranges through which we passed. We often 
saw heads and skins. We only once saw a live serow, and 
twice saw live ghoral. The natives use dogs to chase them. 
Both being mountain animals the dogs often bring them 
to bay in a corner of rock or an unscalable precipice. 
They are able to give a good account of themselves with 
their horns and dogs are not uncommonly badly gored or 
killed outright. 

The cat family is well represented, running from large 
handsome leopards (pao) to spotted cats no larger than 
a house cat. Here, as everywhere, they are rarely seen, 
and to get them recourse must usually be had to hounds 
or trapping. 

There are wolves in the higher country, though we never 
saw anything but their tracks. The wild pig is found in 
almost all the wild country through which we travelled. 

Methods of transport vary. A good muleteer, and one 
accustomed to the country, can take mules many places 
where a poor muleteer would insist that only porters could 
be used. Unless going into a territory where mules are 
unattainable and where there is a danger of being 
stranded it is better to hire, rather than buy. Of course 
it is sometimes essential to own your own pack train, but 
it is exceedingly difficult to get mule men who will take 
as good care of the animals as if they were their own. 
You are also almost sure to pass out of the territory where 
they are accustomed to travelling and then find that a 
local man would be infinitely preferable. Besides this it 
is almost invariably cheaper to hire than to buy. Prices 
vary in different parts of the country and under different 


267 


APPENDIX A 


local conditions. Our hired mules cost us from thirty to 
sixty cents gold a day. 

It goes without saying that the lighter the equipment 
the better. A few mules can be readily obtained where it 
is impossible to secure a larger number. 

It is easier to get a small caravan started, and halts 
can be made in many places where fodder and accommo- 
dations for a large train are not to be had. Most mule- 
teers, quite naturally, prefer to halt in a village when- 
ever it is possible, so that much of the time one does not 
use one’s tent, but a small strong tent is of very great 
value when in the actual hunting country, or crossing the 
barren ranges. Muleteers and native hunters can often 
manage to do without one where the foreigner would find 
conditions far from agreeable. 

We regretted no part of the trip, for the country had 
always much of interest to offer, but had we been able to 
know in advance much that we could learn only from ex- 
perience, we felt that we could have put in our time to 
better advantage had we come in by Yunnanfu and gone 
straight to Yehli. The man who could not afford to go 
further would find a most interesting hunting ground 
there. It would, however, be most worth while to then 
cross the unexplored territory between Yehli and Chui- 
lung, hunting on the way, and spending a week or so at 
the latter place. From there one could go to Tatsienlu 
and to Muping, coming out down the Yangtze by way of 
Chengtu or Kiating. This would seem to offer a maxi- 
mum of interest and chance to obtain results in a mini- 
mum of time. It would be a trip affording an abundance 
of hard work. The months between November and May 
would be the best. Travel is difficult and disagreeable dur- 
ing the rainy season, and hunting in most instances offers 
less prospects of success. 


268 


APPENDIX B 
ROTEIRO 


In reading the following itinerary it must be borne in 
mind that the distances are only approximate, arrived at 
by estimation and by inquiry. There is of necessity room 
for so much variation in transposing Chinese place names 
into English that we have adopted the spelling employed 
in (1) Postal Guide of China, Shanghai, 1921; (2) 
1:4,000,000 map of Asia, British War Office, 1926. When 
the names do not appear on either of them we have adopt- 
ed the form given by our interpreter, Jack Young, who 
is well acquainted with both languages.
